Fantasy The Feast of a Hundred Fires PDF

The Feast of a Hundred Fires

An original adult fantasy novel

Tapio Kinnunen

Part I

Chapter 1 — The Refugee Kitchen

Savra cut the burned skin from an onion while the ration line argued itself awake.

Forty-three people waited beneath the patched awning, though the slate beside the oven promised breakfast for thirty-six. The first seven had come before dawn and marked their places with sandals, baskets, or sleeping children. Those arriving later disputed the custom. Rain from the night storm had found every tear in the canvas. It dripped into bowls, flattened charcoal dust on the paving stones, and made the younger children press close to the oven wall for warmth.

“Thin it again,” Dima Orr said from the kneading board.

Savra looked into the copper pot. Chickpeas had cooked down to a velvet thick enough to carry oil. Another pitcher of water would turn breakfast into an apology.

“Seven more mouths,” Dima added. “Unless you’ve learned to feed sandals.”

“Sandals complain less.” Savra poured the water.

She worked under the name Veyra in the refugee kitchen, slept above the old spice store with three widows and a roof rat, and accepted no task involving seals. The arrangement had held for eleven months. Dima knew she could judge grain by touch and sharpen a warped knife so it cut straight. He did not ask how a woman with a field cook’s scar on her left forearm had learned either skill. Kelar received people who had survived questions; good manners required no repetition.

At the back hearth, a boy fed broken packing slats between bricks. Their own ember jar had gone dark during the rain. Someone from the night shift had borrowed coals from the market ovens, and the flames burned with an uneven blue tongue Savra disliked. She crouched, caught the boy’s wrist before he added a painted board, and smelled the smoke.

“Where did these coals come from?”

“Bread row. Or the soldiers’ cookcart.” He pulled against her grip. “Hessa gave me the scoop.”

“Hessa has three opinions before sunrise and none after.”

The boy grinned. His name was Piri, ten years old if the ration register told the truth, with close-cropped hair and a gap where his left canine should have been. Savra let him go and separated the blue-burning coal with her tongs. Rain had washed most ash signatures to mud, yet one ember carried a lacquered sheen. Oil, perhaps. Military kitchens used pressed sesame waste to keep cart fires alive.

A bell sounded beyond the eastern roofs.

Not a temple bell. The note came low and blunt, struck from iron rather than bronze. One peal, then two close together. Army meal signal. Kelar’s occupying garrison kept its barracks uphill, far enough away that a cook in the market ought to hear only the largest watches.

Savra lifted her knife and scored three lines into the chickpea cakes. No seal, no binding, only a baker’s habit to help them break evenly. She made herself breathe.

Dima ladled bitter fenugreek into the pot. The broth took on its morning sharpness, medicinal at first, then warmer as smoked onion oil spread across the surface. Savra tasted from the wooden spoon. Fenugreek, onion, old chickpeas, iron from the pot. Beneath those sat a faint clean numbness, like salt held too long against a cut.

“Stop service,” she said.

Dima had already filled four bowls. “On what grounds?”

“Bad ember.”

“Bad how?”

Savra searched for an answer that belonged to Veyra. Wet fuel. Paint smoke. Spoiled oil. None explained the numbness gathering at the back of her tongue.

Piri took a cake from the cooling cloth.

The second bell struck.

His right hand snapped backward.

No one touched him. The wrist bent with a dry sound, fingers driven toward his forearm as if a horse had stepped on the palm. Piri stared at the wrong angle. For one suspended breath he made no noise. Then the scream tore through the awning.

Dima crossed the kitchen before the wooden spoon hit the ground. He caught the boy by the elbow, supported the hand, and shouted for splints. Two people in line lurched at once. An old man clutched his ribs. A nursing mother dropped to one knee with blood starting from her nose. Near the awning post, a girl named Seli cried that something had gone through her thigh, though her skirt showed no tear.

Savra swept the four filled bowls from the serving ledge. Pottery broke on the stones. Hot broth ran around bare feet and sent the line scattering.

“No one eats,” she said. “Spit out what’s in your mouth.”

A bearded cooper grabbed her shoulder. “My daughters waited since dark.”

“Then let them wait alive.”

He heard the certainty and released her.

Dima set Piri’s wrist between slats while the boy bit on folded leather. “There’s no impact wound. No fall.”

“There was an impact,” Savra said. “It happened elsewhere.”

His hands paused for less than a heartbeat. The kitchen noise filled the gap: children crying, a pot lid rattling under someone’s heel, rainwater ticking from canvas. Dima tied the splint tighter.

Another meal bell rolled over the roofs.

Pain entered Savra beneath the collarbone, narrow and hot. A spear point, she thought, driven through leather at a rising angle. Her body carried no puncture. Breath still came. Across the yard, three diners folded around the same invisible thrust.

Wounds without battle. She had heard villagers use that phrase years ago, before officials replaced their testimony with clean numbers. A person woke with an archer’s shoulder after no bow work. A child limped on a cavalry fracture. A woman bled into her lungs while the nearest fighting lay four days away. Hall clerks called the reports ration hysteria. Savra had helped prepare the tables those clerks defended.

She seized the back-hearth shovel. “Dima, get everyone beyond the awning. Those who swallowed broth go first. Keep them together.”

“What are you doing?”

“Finding the seat list.”

His stare sharpened. Veyra should not have known that term.

Savra overturned the ember basket onto wet flagstones. Ordinary charcoal hissed and went black. Three live coals held their color. She separated them with the shovel and saw pale ash clinging underneath in deliberate crescents. Not a complete seal. Someone had scraped an imperial hearthstamp against the cookcart grate, then emptied waste coals into a common market bin. Rain joined signatures that dry handling would have kept apart.

No binding should have survived such neglect. Yet the chickpea flour came from imperial ration sacks, donated after the west granary inspection. Grain oath and ember signature had found each other. Food remembered no intent. It carried only preparation.

Savra dragged the copper pot off its stones. Heat blistered her palms through the cloth. The linked diners groaned when the broth sloshed, their pain rising with the disturbed oil.

“Leave it still,” she ordered herself.

The words brought back a tiled testing room in Namarra, Rovan’s hand steady above a silver spoon, his voice patient: agitation changes distribution before it changes flavor. She forced the memory shut.

Dima moved the line into the rain. Fourteen people had eaten, perhaps sixteen if children had shared cakes. Piri had swallowed half a mouthful. The broken wrist belonged to some cavalryman receiving treatment while his portion traveled outward. A spear wound had dispersed among at least four adults. Smaller burdens showed themselves now: saddle chafing across a widow’s thighs, exhaustion in a mason’s back, the bitten tongue of a rider thrown hard against his teeth.

Someone had weighted the military seats light and every concealed civilian seat heavy. The kitchen had not joined by chance alone. Chance had supplied contact. Design had supplied the bias.

Savra took a clean cup, skimmed broth from beneath the oil, and added vinegar from Dima’s medicine shelf. Sourness accelerated a bond. If she tasted now, the table would pull her into its active structure quickly enough to read the weighting through her own body. It might also hand her a soldier’s dying.

Dima saw the cup. “No.”

“Can you tell me how many are bound?”

“No.”

“Then hold Piri’s arm.”

“You know what this is.”

“I know enough to stop it.”

His mouth tightened. Dima had the heavy forearms of a flour porter and the precise fingers of someone who had stitched too many wounds with poor light. He could have knocked the cup away. Instead he returned to Piri and braced the splint.

Savra drank.

The vinegar struck first. Bitter seed followed, harsher than it should have tasted. Then bodies opened around her, not minds or memories, only a field of exertion. Horse muscles labored in borrowed rhythm. A rider’s left knee burned. Someone shivered under blood loss while three distant bodies took the warmth away. The kitchen diners appeared as deep wells into which every burden ran. Their shares did not carry names in language, but position had its own grammar: near seats, declared seats, reserve seats hidden beneath the salt stroke.

One place in the structure pulled harder than all the others. Piri.

Children weighed efficiently because officials counted small bodies as fractions while recipes could invert the fraction. The boy’s grain-oath mark had likely entered the register when soldiers issued his family displacement flour. His half mouthful now carried more of the cavalry table than a full-grown rider’s bowl.

Savra gripped the ledge as another force drove through her left thigh. Her leg remained whole. Pain insisted otherwise.

“Blacken the broth,” she said.

Dima looked up from Piri. “With what?”

“Soot. Ash. Anything that makes it inedible. We have to stop anyone taking another bite.”

A woman poured stove ash into the pot. Another emptied a bucket of wash water over the chickpea cakes. The acts offended every hungry person watching. Their anger had shape and justice. Savra accepted it because spoiled food could be replaced and a shattered child could not.

Severance offered three routes. Break the seal, kill the origin ember, or wait for digestion. Waiting meant hours. The seal remained elsewhere, perhaps on a field-kitchen cauldron. That left fire.

She studied the three stubborn coals. Extinguishing local embers might cut only Kelar’s accidental branch. If the military origin still burned, recoil would strike the remaining civilian seats. She needed to weaken the local share before breaking it.

“Bring salt,” Savra told Dima. “Not market gray. White preserving salt.”

“We have half a jar.”

“All of it.”

He fetched the jar without asking why. That obedience frightened her more than suspicion.

Savra drew a circle on the wet stones around the live coals, then divided it with four straight salt lines. She could not create open seats or remove hidden names. Those refinements required a complete seal. She could, however, obstruct the accidental branch and spread its recoil through the food already ruined. Broth, ash, cakes, spilled oil: all had touched the ember. Matter could carry a share until cooling ended it.

The riders’ pain surged again. Piri’s splinted hand jerked. Savra tasted blood where she had bitten her cheek.

“On my word, pour the pot over the fire.”

Dima lifted one handle. The cooper took the other. Savra placed herself inside the salt circle. A cook who severed from outside could mistake escape for success. If the recoil needed flesh, hers should answer before the diners’ did.

She cut the first salt line with her knife.

Heat crowded under her skin. The second line gave her the cavalryman’s crushed wrist, not as damage but as an instruction every nerve believed. Her knife fell from fingers that no longer trusted themselves. At the third, fourteen heart rhythms stumbled into rough accord. The last line remained between the kitchen and the origin fire.

“Now.”

Dima and the cooper tipped the pot.

Ash-thick broth smothered the coals. Steam burst upward. Savra broke the final line with her heel.

Recoil struck her spine and threw her onto both knees. Around the awning, bound diners cried out once. Piri fainted. For a terrible moment Savra felt the whole cavalry table searching for somewhere to set its weight. Then the wet ash took the heat, the scattered cakes took a pulse of bitterness, and the connection collapsed as the last lacquered ember went dark.

Rain returned to being rain.

Dima checked Piri first. The wrist remained broken. Severance could not undo an injury already transferred; nothing in a hearth returned what it had consumed. Yet no new bruises rose on the boy’s arms. The mother’s nosebleed slowed. The old man drew a shallow breath without the spear pain tightening it.

Savra stayed kneeling until her own nerves released the borrowed crush. Her palms had blistered for real where the pot cloth failed. She wrapped them in a strip torn from her apron.

Beyond the awning, iron-shod boots entered the market.

Soldiers lowered smoke shutters across both ends of the lane. Their officer read from a wax tablet while pairs of infantry inspected ovens, ember jars, and ration sacks. Someone had noticed the broken branch. A field tablewright would feel the sudden recoil even if no clerk knew where it had gone.

Market people understood a closure more quickly than any proclamation. Spice sellers pulled reed mats over their fenugreek, cumin, and dried lime. Bakers buried live coals beneath cold ash, though hidden heat left a wavering stain above each brazier. A butcher tipped blood from his gutter and turned his block facedown. These acts did not make the lane innocent. They made every stall equally difficult to accuse.

Savra counted six soldiers at the east shutter and four at the canal end. Two more moved door to door with long-handled ember spoons. The spoons carried white clay cups at their tips so inspectors could lift a coal without joining its signature to their own equipment. Proper field practice. The officer had not brought clerks or healers, only men trained to preserve evidence and close exits. Whoever commanded the cavalry kitchen valued the broken bond more than the people it had injured.

“Wash the ladle,” Savra said to the woman nearest her. “Use sand first, then water. Put every ration sack on the north wall with the oath marks facing inward.”

The woman obeyed until Dima said, “No. Marks outward.”

Savra looked at him.

“If we hide them, they search longer,” he said. “If they see donated flour, they blame their own storehouse before us.”

He had read the danger correctly without knowing the craft. Savra turned the sacks herself. Black registry strokes crossed the coarse cloth: WEST KELAR RELIEF, third measure, issued under the Hall’s winter oath. The flour had entered the kitchen as charity after inspectors seized better grain for the garrison. Every cake served this morning had therefore carried an imperial claim inside it.

That realization hurt differently from the borrowed spear. The empire had not needed a tablewright in every alley. It needed grain made official once, embers allowed to travel, and hunger strong enough to prevent questions about the source. A refugee kitchen assembled those pieces every dawn.

At the neighboring bakery, a soldier plunged his spoon into a banked fire. The baker objected. An infantryman pinned him against the oven with one arm while the inspector held the recovered coal to a tablet striped in wax. Nothing answered. They dropped it into a lidded jar and moved on.

Savra rubbed ash into the wet stones around their own dead hearth. The broken salt lines still showed pale against the flagging. She smeared them with her heel, then mixed spilled chickpea mash through the marks until they resembled clumsy cleaning. Her blistered palms protested each time she leaned on the shovel. Real injuries had evidentiary value. If questioned, she had burned herself saving breakfast from an overturned pot.

Piri woke on the door litter. “Did the horse get away?”

“No horse here,” his sister said.

“I felt it fall.”

Savra met Dima’s eyes. The boy had received exertion and injury, not a rider’s memory. A falling body could teach the nerves a shape that the mind later supplied with the nearest explanation. She needed Dima to keep that distinction clear if soldiers asked. Claims of visions would help officials dismiss everything as panic.

“Tell him what happened to his wrist,” Savra said quietly. “No more and no less.”

Dima checked the splint again. “And what did happen?”

“A burden reached him through breakfast. It came from a fighting table. That is all we can prove.”

“Can we prove even that?”

Savra felt the hidden seal press against her palm bandage. “We can if I keep what came out of the ash.”

Dima helped Piri onto a door used as a litter. “They’ll search this kitchen.”

“They’ll search every fire that borrowed market coal.”

“You sound certain again.”

Savra picked up her fallen knife. The asymmetrical edge had chipped against stone. Eleven months of hiding had depended on no one recognizing how she held it.

“Take the injured through the dye stalls,” she said. “The south lane has no oven smoke. Tell the inspectors breakfast burned.”

A girl crouched beside the dead coals and pointed. “There’s copper.”

Savra moved the ash with her shovel. Beneath the slurry lay an oval ceramic tag no longer than her thumb, its glazed face stamped around a copper eye. The market coals had carried it from the military cart. Ordinary hearthseals named authorized companies with radial marks. This seal carried those marks in the outer ring, but the inner clay held dozens of pinpricks, each impressed after firing with a civilian ration punch.

Some matched Kelar district signs. Savra recognized the hooked well of the west refugees, the split roof of the burned dyers’ quarter, and the small open square used for unregistered children.

The seal had not merely found civilians. Someone had prepared places for them.

Dima watched her turn it over. “Can you read that?”

“Enough.”

“Enough to run?”

Boots struck the lane gate. The officer demanded entry to the refugee kitchen.

Savra thought of her upstairs bundle: one spare shift, whetstone, two silver measures she had never spent, and a road token under the floorboard. She could cross the canal before soldiers finished the ovens. By sunset she might become another cook under another shortened name.

Piri whimpered on the litter. His sister held the unbroken hand. Behind them stood people who had lost breakfast, trust, and pieces of a battle they had never seen.

Savra slipped the hearthseal beneath the bandage at her blistered palm.

“Dima, open the gate,” she said. “I’m staying with the pot.”

Chapter 2 — Wounds Without Battles

The rear axle split nine miles short of Kelar, under three hundredweight of grain they had no spare wagon to carry.

Teren Avo heard the crack through the rain and felt the cart settle before the driver called out. He raised one fist. Four wagons stopped along the canal road, mules tossing their heads as harness chains slackened. The escort behind them vanished into roadside tamarisk and watched the western rise. Imperial requisition riders had crossed that rise before noon yesterday. If the road remained dry, riders could cover the distance in half the time a grain convoy needed.

“Unload eight sacks,” Teren said. “Not the barley. Rice first.”

Noro, the axlewright, crawled beneath the tilted bed and swore. “Unload twelve if you want the wheel square.”

“Eight gives you a handspan.”

“Twelve gives me fingers afterward.”

“Ten.”

The bargain cost one breath. Teren assigned six carriers, two to each side and two at the tail, so no one wasted strength pulling against another person. Free Granary marks had been painted over the wagon boards with river mud, but wet weather exposed the blue sheaf beneath. From fifty paces the convoy looked like another merchant train caught by an ambitious load. Up close it announced grain withheld from the empire.

Teren carried the first sack himself. Red canal rice shifted against his shoulder. His old left ankle held until he stepped off the paving, then sent a familiar blunt warning through his calf. The joint had been crushed between barges eleven years earlier. It tolerated roads, ladders, and most weather. It objected to mud with arithmetic precision.

“Captain,” a lookout called from the tamarisk. “One runner east.”

“Uniform?”

“No. Barefoot.”

The runner reached them bent nearly double, a girl of perhaps fifteen with mud to her knees and one hand clamped beneath her opposite arm. She wore a reedworker’s short coat. No weapon, no village badge, no shoes. Teren took her weight before she hit the road.

“Water,” he said.

She drank twice and pushed the cup away. “Ruva needs a healer.”

“We have bandage linen.” Free Granary convoys carried no healer on runs this close to occupied towns. “What happened?”

“Spears.”

The escorts stirred in cover.

Teren looked east. The canal road ran straight for more than a mile, empty except for two goat carts and a man dredging weed from an irrigation cut. “How many soldiers?”

“None.”

“Who used the spears?”

“Nobody. That’s why you have to come.”

Noro struck out the broken axle pin. “Riders won’t care why we stopped.”

Teren measured what delay could buy and what it could cost. Ten sacks lay on oiled cloth. An axle scarf needed half an hour if the split remained clean, longer if the iron collar had warped. Ruva sat less than a mile north of the canal, according to the marker stones. A detour there and back would add another hour. Imperial riders, if already moving, could close nine miles in less.

The girl showed him her hidden hand. Purple swelling crossed the palm below the thumb. The skin held no cut, but two parallel grooves pressed deep into the flesh as if reins had burned through it.

“Do you ride?” Teren asked.

“We own one ox. He doesn’t use reins.”

“Did you eat this morning?”

Her face tightened with impatience. “Millet porridge. Half the village did. Then Nima took a spear under his ribs while he sat by the oven. Come see.”

Teren turned to his second, Haro Sen. “You own the repair. If riders appear, send two wagons toward the canal ferry and hide the grain from this one in the drainage culvert. I’ll take four people.”

“You’ll take six,” Haro said. “That ankle makes you worth half on wet ground.”

“Six, then.”

Ruva held forty-three houses around a communal oven and two reed sheds. No blood marked the central yard, though eight people lay on doors beneath an eave. Teren checked every lane before he accepted the emptiness. Cooking smoke, tethered animals, children peering from roofs. No broken gate. No hoof marks newer than the rain. No place for an armed party to hide.

A gray-haired potter named Nima breathed in shallow measures. He had lifted his shirt to reveal unbroken skin above his right hip. Each inhalation stopped at the same point.

“Feels like iron went in here,” he said. “Came out my back. My wife put a mirror there. Nothing.”

Teren knelt without flexing the bad ankle. “When?”

“First sun.”

“How long after breakfast?”

“Not long. We ate when the canal watch struck.”

Nearby, a woman’s left shoulder had swollen around no visible injury. Another villager could not put weight on his right knee. Three children slept with the heavy stillness of people who had marched all night. Their pulses ran steady. Their feet carried no blisters.

Teren asked the same questions in the same order: food, time, work, visitors, soldiers, shared water, new grain. Answers meant little until arranged. The village had eaten red millet from an imperial relief sack issued five days earlier. Their oven had burned local tamarisk. No one had used an official feastwright’s seal. Pain began after the canal watch sounded two long notes and a short one.

The runner, Eda, followed him from patient to patient. “Can your grain fix them?”

“Food fixes hunger. This needs a cause.”

“You’re Free Granary.” She said the name as children said floodwall, a thing built to keep consequences outside.

Teren did not correct her. He ordered two sacks of rice left at Ruva because injured people could not grind millet, and rice could boil with less labor. He assigned one escort to record names, symptoms, handedness, and precise times on the back of a toll invoice. If imperial riders found it, they would see arithmetic and no accusation.

A bell carried faintly from the south.

Two notes, a pause, then one.

Nima arched against the door. Eda’s rein burns reddened. Teren felt a sudden pressure around his chest, not pain exactly, but the exhaustion of a body pulling air at the end of a charge. His own breath answered before he could refuse it.

Every patient had eaten. So had Teren, though his breakfast came from convoy flatbread baked yesterday at Penrill storehouse. The pressure eased within seconds.

“Did anyone hear that earlier?” he asked.

Nima’s wife nodded. “Same ringing before the hurts.”

The canal watch used a wooden clapper. Those iron notes came from farther away.

Teren returned to the road at a pace his ankle disliked. Noro had fitted a green elm scarf around the broken axle and bound it with the old iron collar. The repair would hold to Kelar if they moved six sacks forward and kept the rear wagon off ruts. Haro had bad news waiting.

“Smoke west. Two miles, perhaps three. Mounted.”

“How many?”

“Road dust says twelve to twenty.”

Teren pointed to the ten unloaded sacks. “Two stay at Ruva. Put four each on the front wagons. What’s the next village east?”

Noro looked at him as though he had proposed reversing the canal. “Kelar lies south.”

“I asked for the next village.”

“Daret Ford. Mile and a half beyond the sluice.”

They moved before the repaired axle cooled. Teren put the slow wagon second so a fresh axle failure would not trap the whole train. Four escorts remained in roadside cover to show false tracks toward the ferry. Two rode empty mules ahead, not fast enough to advertise fear. The convoy left twelve minutes after the first hammer blow.

At the sluice, Daret Ford’s meal yard looked like a skirmish after everyone had removed the weapons. A miller rolled on packed earth holding his thigh. Two women braced a clothworker whose jaw had dislocated. Children carried water to adults who seemed too tired to stand. On the road verge, an old draft ox cropped grass without concern.

Teren did not stop the wagons. He walked beside them while Haro gathered answers from the mill keeper. Imperial barley had entered Daret seven days ago. Local hearth, common breakfast, symptoms at first sun. Two-one bell sequence heard across the fields. No soldiers sighted in nineteen days.

“Second place,” Haro said.

“Third report,” Teren corrected. “Eda’s hand counts apart from Ruva’s yard.”

“Three people don’t make a village.”

“No, but a rein injury in a reed settlement makes a route.”

He left another sack. The choice reduced their reserve below the amount required to bribe Kelar’s south-gate weighers. Haro marked the loss in charcoal on the wagon rail and did not argue. Free Granary rules gave a captain authority over emergency distribution. They did not make replacement grain appear.

Beyond Daret, the road divided. The paved branch bent south toward Kelar. A towpath continued east to Iskar Fen, where thirty households harvested lotus root and sent nothing to the garrison except canal tax. Teren saw people gathered at the fen oven and ordered the convoy onto the towpath.

Noro spat into the water. “That scarf lasts to Kelar, not through every hungry hamlet in the basin.”

“We’re checking one.”

“We checked two.”

“We found two.”

Iskar Fen supplied the pattern its missing quantity. Three unwounded villages had eaten from different sacks, cooked different grains, and burned local fuel. Ruva used red millet, Daret barley mash, Iskar rice crust softened with goat milk. Each village had received imperial grain under the same district oath. Each reported injuries after identical distant bells. No road connected them in an order cavalry could have ridden before breakfast.

At Iskar, six lotus cutters carried the ache of saddle work in their hips. One man had a shallow cut appear across his brow while he repaired a net, though no blade touched him. A pregnant woman suffered repeated cramps timed with the bells. Teren made her the first recipient of the clean rice they carried, then stopped himself before promising it would help.

Food could carry the problem. More food did not necessarily carry a cure.

He asked to see the ration seals. The village head brought broken ceramic tags from three sacks, kept because official collectors sometimes demanded proof of lawful use. Teren laid them on a fish-drying rack beside a tag from Ruva and Haro’s charcoal rubbing from Daret.

Outer symbols differed by grain measure and issuing clerk. The inner hook repeated on all three: a narrow bend crossed by two dots. Teren had seen similar marks on military requisition inventories, though never inside a civilian issue ring.

“What does it say?” the village head asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Then why are you looking at it like a confession?”

“Because someone took the trouble to put it there.”

Teren wrapped one Iskar fragment in cloth. He had handled manifests, toll exemptions, false guild stamps, canal-duty waivers, and enough forged measures to know when a mark exceeded its stated work. Reading hearthseal notation required a feastwright. Most served the Hall of Measures. One did not.

Years ago, before the Free Granary had a name, a deserter from the imperial kitchens had sent river towns a warning about flour oaths. Savra Kel’s letter contained no apology and no request for shelter, only seven ways to prevent requisition grain from retaining a military signature. Teren had copied those instructions by hand. He had never met her. Three separate informants placed her in the western basin under different names; the most recent pointed to Kelar.

A rider arrived from the paved road. Haro’s rear scout slid from the mule before it stopped.

“Imperials found the false ferry track. They’re turning east.”

“Number?”

“Fourteen. One officer.”

Teren looked at the towpath. Water on one side, reed beds on the other, no room for wagons to turn. The nearest bridge stood behind the riders. Ahead, Iskar’s grain sheds had raised stone floors and doors too narrow for sacks to pass quickly.

“Cut the third wagon loose,” he said. “Put its mule teams on the others. We cache its load under the drying racks.”

Noro swore again, this time with admiration or despair.

They shifted twenty-four sacks in nine minutes. Iskar’s lotus cutters formed a line without being asked. Each person carried one sack from wagon to rack, stepped through the water beneath, and passed it upward through a loosened floor panel. The pregnant woman counted loads from a stool. Teren left six sacks visible in the abandoned wagon, enough to support the appearance of a merchant loss without giving the riders reason to dismantle the village.

His ankle failed on load seventeen. The foot folded inward. Haro caught the sack before it pulled Teren down.

“You’re done lifting.”

“Three loads remain.”

“Two remain. One’s on my shoulder.”

Teren accepted the correction because command did not alter weight. He bound his ankle over the boot and positioned the disabled wagon sideways where the towpath narrowed. When imperial riders reached it, they would have to dismount or push it into the canal.

The convoy departed through the reed beds on a fishermen’s maintenance path. Wheels sank to the hubs twice. Mud stripped the paint from one board and exposed the Free Granary sheaf fully, but no one had time to conceal it again. Behind them, men shouted at the blockade wagon.

At the second mire, Teren ordered the rice sacks moved by hand before anyone tried the wheel. Wet grain could gain a tenth of its weight through cloth, enough to turn a manageable rut into a broken hub. Iskar’s cutters laid split reeds beneath the rim while Noro levered from the axle. No one pulled until Teren counted. On four, the paired mules leaned together, six people lifted, and the wheel climbed onto the reed mat without twisting. The method cost three minutes and spared another wagon. Teren marked both facts against the margin in his head. Delay mattered. So did arriving with something left to unload.

A new series of bells sounded near midday.

This time Teren counted before pain arrived. One long peal. Two quick. A pause sufficient for serving bowls. Then three strokes.

The lotus cutters behind them cried out in scattered sequence. Haro grabbed his left arm as if a shield strap had torn the shoulder. One mule stumbled under a burst of fatigue that came from no exertion of its own; animals could occupy declared ration seats when grain oaths named a transport team, but this mule had eaten only roadside grass. The effect passed around it, not through it.

Teren took out the toll invoice covered with symptoms. He wrote bell patterns beside every time. First sun at Ruva: two-one. Daret: same. Iskar: same. Midday: one-two-three. Those patterns matched meal calls used by field companies, not temple hours or canal watches. Breakfast sounded once for first line and twice for second. Midday separated mounted units from infantry by serving order.

The injuries followed meal bells, not roads.

That conclusion reduced a wilderness of possibilities to one dangerous route. Soldiers ate. Villagers hurt. Grain seals joined them across distance. If the garrison controlled the meal schedule, every ration line in its issue district could become a hidden part of military provisioning.

Teren folded the invoice into his document pouch beside the Iskar seal fragment.

“We’re still carrying twenty-eight sacks,” Haro said. “Kelar was promised forty-two.”

“Leave four at the east safehouse. Send six back to these villages by fishing skiff tonight.”

“That puts Kelar at eighteen.”

“Twenty-four. The wagon bait held six, and the promise already lost those.”

Haro gave him the look reserved for commanders who used correctness to make bad news sound manageable. Teren knew the habit and disliked it more when another person noticed.

“Twenty-four, then,” Haro said. “What buys the shortfall?”

“Evidence.”

“Evidence doesn’t boil.”

“No. It tells us why people fall before the pot does.”

At the final rise, Kelar’s walls appeared through rain haze. Smoke shutters darkened the market quarter. The east barracks signal tower stood above them, its iron meal bell visible beneath a tiled hood. Two companies lined the south road, stopping carts and opening ember jars.

A Free Granary contact waited in an abandoned lime kiln outside the canal gate. He had arrived with word of a morning disaster: refugees injured over breakfast, a cook who ruined the pot, inspectors searching market fires. He did not know the cook’s true name. He knew she had ordered salt placed like a trained tablewright and had stayed when escape remained possible.

Teren gave him Savra Kel’s old description: compact build, scar along the left forearm, forty years or more, knives sharpened unevenly.

The contact nodded at the scar. “Could be her.”

“Could be anyone who worked an oven.”

“You came nine miles on a broken axle for anyone?”

Teren looked back at his remaining wagons. Twenty-four sacks for a district expecting forty-two. Fourteen riders somewhere behind them. Three villages carrying a garrison’s morning injuries, with another meal due before sunset.

“Get the grain through the canal store,” he told Haro. “No common ovens. Sell from sealed jars until I return.”

Haro glanced toward the closed market. “And you?”

Teren tightened the binding around his left ankle and put the wrapped seal fragment in the inner pocket of his coat.

“I’m going to find the cook before the next bell.”

Chapter 3 — The First Taster

Issen touched the white loaf to their lower lip and waited for the hall to become silent.

Silence never arrived all at once in the Hall of Measures. Bronze weights ceased ringing first. Petitioners along the east wall quieted next, followed by grain clerks at their stepped desks. The provincial assessors took longest because rank allowed them an extra breath of conversation. When the last whisper ended, Issen bit through the scored crust.

The loaf carried Namarra’s public standard: sifted wheat, one part old grain to nine new, city-cistern water, sesame oil at the permitted minimum. Its underside held a dusting of pale ash from the civic ovens. No sourness, no mold, no narcotic seed, no unregistered hearth signature. Issen chewed twelve times and swallowed.

“Fit for the common measure,” they said.

A clerk struck the approval tablet. Two hundred loaves passed from inspection tables to blue-robed distributors. Petitioners watched the bread leave by a door they could not use. The morning ceremony certified that the capital’s grain met its own standard; it did not require the loaves to reach everyone present.

Issen lifted the next cup.

Their silver tasting chain hung from neck to belt in seven graduated links. Each link represented a class of danger the office had once claimed authority to reject: rot, adulteration, poison, oath defect, hearth contamination, hostile weighting, and unnamed seats. During Issen’s eleven years as First Taster, officials had narrowed the last two into ceremonial questions. They might ask whether a table held hostile intent. No document defined who got to decide hostility.

The cup contained lentil broth from a barracks allotment. Cumin had scorched against the pot before the cook diluted it. Issen found iron residue at the rim and a trace of sleep-poppy too weak to violate soldiers’ regulation. They approved it with a condition that the pot be sanded.

Three more samples followed. Canal rice. A salt-cured fish paste. Oil from the west presses, rancid beneath clove meant to hide it. Issen refused the oil. The presiding clerk grimaced because refusal required paperwork, but the barrels would not enter hospital kitchens that afternoon.

Small protections kept an office habitable.

The refused oil required a witness procedure. Issen poured the sample into a narrow glass tube, stoppered it with red wax, and pressed the sixth link of their chain into the seal. A junior clerk copied barrel marks while an assessor from the west presses objected that cloves naturally darkened storage oil.

“Clove darkens the color,” Issen said. “It does not produce the wax taste at the back of the tongue.”

“The barrels passed at the canal gate.”

“Then the gate taster may join me for a comparison.”

“He has other duties.”

“So do the hospital patients.”

The assessor withdrew his objection rather than summon a colleague whose approval would become part of the record. Issen watched the clerk add the withdrawal in smaller script. Official truth often depended less on what a mouth detected than on which person could be forced to taste beside it.

At the east wall, a petitioner raised both hands. “Taster Mar, my district received that oil yesterday.”

The presiding clerk ordered silence.

Issen should have let the guard remove the speaker. The tasting office certified samples placed on its table, not shipments already distributed. Jurisdiction again. Instead they asked, “Which district?”

“Lower Potters. Four infirmary jars. Same barrel stamp, west crescent with two reeds.”

The junior clerk’s stylus stopped. Marks on the refused sample showed one reed crossed by a press line. Similar enough for merchants to confuse, different enough for a Hall advocate to deny.

“Bring one sealed jar before sunset,” Issen said. “Do not open it.”

The presiding clerk leaned close. “That instruction exceeds the docket.”

“Then place it on tomorrow’s docket.”

“Tomorrow holds military allotments.”

“Rancid oil keeps.”

A few petitioners laughed before remembering where they stood. The clerk colored. Issen returned the sealed tube to the evidence rack and made certain its slot number entered two ledgers. One copy could disappear. Two required coordination.

For eleven years, such maneuvers had persuaded them that the chain retained substance. They had stopped adulterated vinegar, flour cut with kiln chalk, fish paste tainted by a cracked lead glaze, and sleep-poppy added to workhouse porridge. Each refusal saved bodies that could be counted. Yet no link granted access to the grain-oath room, where clerks assigned villages to supply districts. No taster saw military seat lists. When a laborer ate lawful bread and woke carrying lawful pain, every ingredient could pass inspection.

The office protected mouths from what policy admitted food might contain.

What policy refused to name passed under a covered lid.

At the seventh tasting, doors behind the weighing dais opened. Four Hall attendants entered carrying a covered ceramic vessel between them. Their gloves reached above the elbow. No sample card hung from the handles. Grand Provisioner Rovan Dast walked behind them in an undecorated gray robe, his only badge a square of black bronze at the throat.

Provincial assessors stood. Petitioners knelt because everyone else had.

Issen remained seated. Ceremony required the First Taster to meet all food at mouth height, regardless of who sent it.

Rovan stopped beside their table. At sixty-one he had the composed face of a man who slept enough because other people were paid not to. Gray threaded his beard. Steam dampened one cuff where he had steadied the vessel himself.

“An unscheduled measure,” he said.

“Then it lacks a lawful place in the public sequence.”

“It has a private one.”

Issen glanced at the covered vessel. Heat still moved beneath its lid. “Private food needs no public mouth.”

“Today it needs yours.”

He never raised his voice. That habit made refusal sound like an administrative error rather than a possibility.

The presiding clerk dismissed the hall for a recess. Petitioners protested as distributors carried away the remaining bread. Bronze doors closed on their voices. Rovan’s attendants turned toward a narrow stair behind the grain standards.

Issen wiped their spoon in salt, rinsed it, and fastened it to the lowest link of the chain. “I will need the seat declaration.”

“You will have what the test requires,” Rovan said.

“That answer has no flavor, Provisioner.”

“Come taste before the broth cools.”

The stair descended farther than public plans acknowledged. Hall kitchens occupied the first underground level, where bakers prepared inspection loaves and clerks ate at different bells according to rank. Rovan led Issen through those rooms and down another flight cut into older stone. The air grew dry. Ventilation shafts carried smoke upward without sharing the smells of cooking.

At the bottom stood a kitchen with one door, two tables, and a circular hearth sunk into the floor. White tiles reflected firelight. Copper pipes entered the wall from four directions, each terminating above a lidded ember cup. Ninety-nine cups sat around the hearth rim, small enough to fit in a closed hand. One larger brazier occupied the northern point.

Issen counted twice.

“One hundred fires,” Rovan said. “In representation.”

“Representation by what?”

“Provincial signatures entrusted to the Hall.”

Several cups carried no live coal, only ash sealed beneath mica. Others showed pinpricks of orange. The northern brazier burned steadily under a cage of blackened bronze. Issen recognized it from civic processions as the capital’s oldest maintained ember, though they had never seen the vessel without flower garlands and a guard of children.

A cook waited beyond the salt line. She wore no Hall insignia and kept both hands visible. On the preparation table lay pale barley, rendered lamb fat, dried mint, sour plum, and a shallow horn dish filled with coarse black crystals.

Issen pointed with their clean spoon. “That salt has no registry color.”

“Not a market article,” Rovan said. “A hearth instrument.”

The cook’s face did not change, but her left thumb pressed hard against her forefinger.

Issen moved close enough to inspect without crossing the floor line. Each crystal had a dull center and glassy edges. Ordinary salt fractured white. These grains held ash inside their structure, fused by heat or long contact with a concentrated ember signature.

“Black hearth-salt,” Rovan said. “It lets the primary fire keep order among lesser arrangements.”

“Whose order?”

“The recipe’s.”

That answer carried enough truth to evade correction.

The attendants uncovered the vessel. Barley broth released lamb fat and mint into the room. Sour plum sharpened the steam. Nothing about the smell suggested poison. Beneath the surface, an oil spiral divided into many narrow arms. Each arm touched the bowl’s rim at a scored point.

Issen counted visible scores. There were twenty-five, each quartered by hairline cuts. One hundred places compressed into a ceremonial cup.

“Seat list,” they said.

Rovan gestured toward the hearth. “The represented fires.”

“Fires do not have wrists or stomachs.”

“The populations attached to their lawful grain oaths provide the tested field.”

“How many people?”

“This measure concerns reach, not census.”

“Then you have not answered.”

Rovan’s attention rested on them with what others might mistake for patience. “The office of First Taster exists because no list can name every mouth affected by provisioning. You stand for those mouths.”

“I eat before them. I do not become them.”

“Today we may learn whether the distinction can be made useful.”

An attendant placed a thumb-sized cup before Issen. The cook stirred the vessel once, clockwise, without scraping the bottom. She filled the cup from beneath the oil and added one black crystal with bronze pincers.

Source fire: the old capital brazier, carrying lesser signatures. Food: barley and fat, souring to accelerate. Seat declaration: provincial grain oaths treated as populations. Weighting remained unstated. Duration likely followed digestion unless the hearth went out.

“Severance?” Issen asked.

Rovan indicated a hood above the northern brazier. “The primary ember can be smothered.”

“By whom?”

“The supervising tablewright.”

The cook’s eyes stayed lowered.

No personal route. No independent witness. No authority in Issen’s hands despite the chain that said otherwise.

They could refuse. The public record would show an incomplete safety test. Rovan would select another mouth, perhaps one less practiced at distinguishing pain from damage. Refusal would protect Issen’s body and leave the measure untouched.

“Record my objection,” they told the attendant holding the test slate. “Seat identities withheld. Weighting withheld. Taster lacks severance.”

The attendant looked to Rovan.

“Record it,” he said.

The stylus scratched wax.

Issen raised the cup. Barley touched their tongue first, cooked until its husks opened. Lamb fat carried heat along the palate. Mint arrived stale. Sour plum cut through everything, and the black grain dissolved without saltiness.

They swallowed.

A hundred ember signatures unfolded across their body.

The experience contained no landscapes, voices, or thoughts. Heartbeats imposed themselves as rhythm alone: quick, slow, arrhythmic, sleeping, laboring, frightened. Thousands overlapped until Issen’s own pulse disappeared inside them. Hunger entered next, not as one emptiness but as pressures of different depth. A field worker’s steady deficit. A fever patient refusing food. Children whose stomachs had learned not to demand breakfast. Soldiers digesting meat while distant bodies absorbed the heaviness.

Issen gripped the table edge. Their silver chain clicked against tile.

A right wrist broke.

The force bent Issen’s hand backward. Bone did not part in the hidden kitchen, but pain arrived complete, carrying the clean instant of fracture and the swelling that followed. One child somewhere in the represented field had taken an injury too large for that small body. Issen knew childhood only from the scale of the pulse and the speed at which shock scattered breath. Nothing offered a name.

They tried to speak. Breath came with someone else’s exhaustion.

Across the salt line, the cook reached for the brazier hood.

Rovan raised two fingers. “Wait.”

The cook froze.

Issen watched him through tears. “End it.”

“Can you distinguish the reaches?”

“A child.”

“Anything else?”

“End the measure.”

“The distinction matters.”

Pain multiplied in the left thigh, beneath the ribs, around a rider’s knee. Issen could not know which bodies originated those burdens and which received them. That uncertainty formed part of the design, not a weakness in their palate.

They hooked the lowest silver link around the cup and pulled. Ceramic struck tile. Broth spilled across the inner edge of the salt line. The personal portion broke contact, but the larger vessel continued to steam. Relief came incomplete.

“Smother it,” Issen said to the cook, using the command form reserved for immediate contamination.

Her eyes lifted. Issen held them. Ceremony gave the office few powers, but trained kitchen staff still recognized that one.

She dropped the hood.

The northern flame vanished. Around it, the smaller cups dimmed. Recoil moved through the broth as a shudder in the oil. Issen’s stomach cramped hard enough to bend them over the table, then the field of heartbeats withdrew.

Their own pulse returned, rapid and solitary.

Rovan regarded the covered brazier. “The test ended sooner than planned.”

“It ended later than ordered.”

“You identified both scope and an individual injury. That exceeds our smaller trials.”

“A child’s wrist broke while you measured reach.”

“The injury did not originate here.”

“Neither did the hunger. You still served it.”

He dismissed the attendants except the recorder and cook. When the door shut, he drew a stool to Issen’s side but did not touch them.

“The basin approaches another provisioning crisis,” he said. “River storehouses refuse assessments. Salt guilds set local advantage above common measure. Armies and villages already exchange consequences through seizure, blockade, and price. They merely do so blindly.”

Issen flexed their right hand. No fracture. Nerves continued to report one.

“You propose improving the blindness?”

“I propose ending it.”

“By hiding the seats from the person who tastes.”

“By establishing that distance need not protect one province from another’s condition.”

Rovan asked the recorder for the slate. He read Issen’s objection, then added a notation in his own hand. His writing leaned neither forward nor back.

“The public certification will state that the sample showed no toxic adulteration,” he said.

“I did not certify it.”

“You found no poison.”

“I found concealed weighting and involuntary reach.”

“Those belong to policy review, not food safety.”

The familiar division settled between them. Every dangerous measure acquired a room where one office lacked jurisdiction and another lacked a tongue.

Issen unhooked the broken tasting cup from their chain. “Then invite me to the policy review.”

Rovan smiled with genuine weariness. “I am inviting you now.”

“To approve?”

“To understand. The Equinox demonstrations begin soon. A First Taster who understands the instrument can keep panic from wasting its value.”

“You want a mouth for the Hall.”

“The Hall already has many. I want one trained to tell differences.”

He stood. At the door he paused beside the nameless cook. “Prepare a second baseline tomorrow. Half souring. No peripheral military burden.”

The cook inclined her head. Issen noticed that Rovan still had not asked whether the child’s wrist remained broken.

Two attendants escorted Issen to a recovery room on the upper kitchen level. Officially, tasters rested there after strong spices. A couch, washbasin, and covered chamber pot furnished the space. No window offered a view of time.

They sent the attendants away and vomited into the basin.

The body did not respect ceremonial distinctions. Sweat chilled Issen’s back. Their right hand curled protectively though the bones remained aligned, and a pulse of saddle fatigue tightened muscles they had not used. They tested each finger against the basin rim, then pressed the wrist from both sides. Actual swelling would persist under pressure; borrowed pain shifted with attention and returned when they released it. The examination gave them facts, not relief. No ritual prescribed what to do when another person’s injury remained real after the taster’s sensation faded.

Barley, fat, and black residue streaked the porcelain. Their wrist pain faded as nerves surrendered the distant instruction. Hunger lingered in layers. Issen drank water carefully, one measured mouthful at a time, and counted their own pulse until it no longer matched remembered rhythms.

Ceremonial doctrine called this recovery: identify what belonged to the sample, separate it from actual bodily condition, and return to public composure. The doctrine assumed every dangerous taste had an endpoint the office controlled.

Issen rinsed the basin. One black crystal remained caught beneath the rim, smaller than a lentil grain. It had not dissolved. Perhaps it had fallen from the cup when the ceramic broke, clung to their cuff, then dropped while they washed.

They lifted it on the tip of the silver spoon.

Ash shifted inside the crystal when held toward the lamp. One side carried a minute groove from bronze pincers. Evidence of an unregistered hearth instrument, if any examiner dared name it. Also a live fragment of whatever hierarchy had held one hundred signatures beneath the capital fire.

Footsteps stopped outside the recovery door.

Issen opened the clasp at the seventh link of their tasting chain. The hollow catch traditionally held a pinch of neutral salt for cleansing the tongue. They emptied the white grains into the washbasin, placed the black hearth-salt inside, and fastened the clasp at their throat.

When the attendant entered, Issen stood with the chain in order and the basin clean.

Chapter 4 — The Cold Seats

Vinegar ate the old dye from the hearth bricks while soldiers searched the street above.

Savra kept the pan below a boil. Too much heat would send white steam through the roof vents and announce that someone had relit the abandoned dyehouse kitchen. Too little would leave the seal’s glaze closed. She fed the fire with one charcoal chip at a time, each taken from Dima’s medicine brazier and scrubbed of ash before it entered the grate.

Across the cellar, Piri slept with his right wrist splinted against a paddle handle. Fever had not come. Swelling had. His sister Seli sat beside him, trimming linen into strips that no one presently needed because work kept her from listening to boots.

Dima watched the stair. “Third patrol since sunrise.”

“Second patrol,” Savra said. “The first came twice.”

“That should comfort us.”

Rain had ended during the night. Kelar’s market lanes now carried sound cleanly: shutters raised under inspection, jar lids breaking, officials calling registration marks. The garrison had found two kitchens with mixed military ash and arrested their owners. Neither kitchen reported injuries. That meant the field tablewright still searched for the branch that had recoiled hard enough to disturb the cavalry meal.

Savra’s palms made lifting the vinegar pan difficult. Blisters crossed both hands where heat had passed through wet cloth. Dima had dressed them in honey and linen, then wrapped the right more tightly because she refused to stop using it. The imperial hearthseal lay in a shallow clay dish beside the fire. Its copper eye had turned green around the edges.

She had washed all loose ash from it without scratching the glaze. Civilian pinpricks remained visible inside the company ring. The deeper notation stayed concealed. Hall seals often carried three levels: public authorization on the face, weighting under heat-softened glaze, and a custody instruction written in metal salts visible only through sour steam.

Veyra should not know that.

Dima came down two steps. “Someone’s in the drain court.”

Savra covered the vinegar with a board, killed the brazier flame under a sand cup, and drew her chipped knife. Dima lifted a dye paddle in both hands. Seli woke Piri without letting him cry out.

Water moved behind the cellar wall. A rusted grate shifted inward. Muddy fingers appeared through the gap, then a broad-shouldered man forced the grate aside and entered bent nearly double. His coat carried canal slime to the chest. He favored his left foot.

A second man remained beyond the opening with a short spear.

The first raised empty hands. “I’m looking for Savra Kel.”

“No one here by that name,” Dima said.

The stranger’s attention passed over the room. It stopped at Savra’s left forearm, where her sleeve had ridden above the old oven scar. Then he looked at her knife. Not the face. Competent.

“My name is Teren Avo,” he said. “Free Granary.”

“Anyone can paint a blue sheaf.”

“Mine showed when rain stripped the wagon.”

“Then you’re a poor smuggler.”

“I’m a grain captain. Smuggling happens because the empire changed the noun.”

Dima lowered the paddle a finger’s width. Savra did not lower the knife.

Teren drew a folded toll invoice from inside his coat. The paper had stayed dry under two layers of waxed cloth. He set it on the floor rather than approach her. Rows of names and bodily injuries covered the back, followed by times and bell patterns.

“Ruva,” he said. “Daret Ford. Iskar Fen. Different local fires, different breakfasts, one imperial district oath. They hurt when the Kelar garrison eats.”

He placed a wrapped ceramic fragment beside the invoice. Its inner hook matched one of the marks on Savra’s complete seal.

Dima stared at it. “More children?”

“Three in Ruva. Two at Daret. Iskar kept theirs from the morning pot after we warned them.”

“Why come here?” Savra asked.

“Because eleven years ago Savra Kel wrote seven precautions against military grain oaths. Yesterday a market cook used salt lines to break a fighting table. Either I found her or I found someone she taught.”

Savra had sent those precautions to six river mills after she deserted. She had signed no name, but a copyist could identify a hand by its measurements. Rovan had taught that too.

“What was the fourth precaution?” she asked.

“Never cool official bread above a civic ember, because fat carries both signatures through the next heating.”

“The fifth?”

“Grind oath grain with an unregistered measure.”

“Wrong. Reweigh it. Grinding changes texture, not claim.”

Teren accepted the correction without embarrassment. “Then the copy reached us damaged.”

“Or you remembered badly.”

“Both can be true.”

The answer irritated her because it left no loose edge to pull. She sheathed the knife.

Dima opened the cellar door for Teren’s spearman, who introduced himself as Haro Sen. Haro stayed near the drain with a hand on his weapon and concern fixed on Teren’s ankle. These men had not entered to make arrests. They had also brought no grain. Savra distrusted causes that arrived empty-handed.

“We delivered what survived the road to a canal store,” Teren said when she asked. “Twenty-four sacks.”

“Kelar expected forty-two.”

“Six stayed with villages carrying other people’s wounds. Six bought us time at Iskar. Six remain hidden for those villages.”

“You count absence cleanly.”

“If I don’t, someone else pays it unnoticed.”

Savra looked at Piri’s swollen wrist. “Someone always pays.”

She relit the brazier. Teren crouched beside the hearth but kept outside arm’s reach. Haro took the stair from Dima, freeing the healer to check the boy again. No one suggested trust. They arranged labor around danger.

Savra poured vinegar back into the pan and added a strip of blue-dyed wool from the cellar floor. Old madder or indigo would have carried mineral contaminants. This wool released an alkaline blue that made metal salts stand darker against ceramic. She set the complete hearthseal on a pierced spoon above the steam.

“What do you expect?” Teren asked.

“Writing under the glaze.”

“Why hide it there?”

“So a ration clerk sees permission, a feastwright sees weight, and a commander can deny both.”

Teren opened the toll invoice. “Which mark means village?”

“None. Villages receive positions, not names.”

The first sheen appeared along the outer ring. Savra rotated the spoon with wrapped fingers. Vinegar steam condensed on the ceramic and ran toward the copper eye. Brown lines rose beneath the glaze, faint as old scars.

Four broad strokes divided the seal into serving groups. Cavalry occupied the upper arc, with twelve authorized squad marks distributed equally. Lower arcs contained no public symbols, only tiny sockets punched through the glaze. The hidden writing connected each socket to a civilian grain-oath hook.

Teren counted. “Thirty-six.”

“Thirty-six classes, not people. Each class may hold a household register or district issue.”

“Kelar’s west refugees?”

Savra pointed with a wooden splint. “This hooked well.”

“And the open square?”

“Children without household registration.”

Seli stopped cutting linen.

Piri’s seat mark had no individual name. The empire had built a category large enough to catch him.

Steam thickened. A second layer emerged near the center, written in a feastwright’s narrow ratio notation. Teren leaned closer. Savra put a hand against his chest before he crossed above the pan.

“Vinegar accelerates residue. If that copper still carries a live trace, breathing over it can join you.”

“You’re breathing over it.”

“I know where not to stand.”

He shifted back. “Show me the ratio.”

Twelve cavalry marks shared a single shallow stroke. Civilian sockets descended in groups toward a deep central cut. The recipe gave every soldier one part burden across the table, then divided that part through the lower seats with a multiplier that changed by body class. Workers carried standard weight. Registered dependents carried half. Unregistered children carried double because official fractioning had been inverted in the hidden layer.

Teren’s jaw set. “Yesterday the villages had adults hurt too.”

“The hooks can overlap. A person belongs to a household, district, and relief class. If the seal does not exclude duplicate claims, one body may occupy several positions.”

“How much can they take?”

“As much as their combined weighting directs until digestion ends or their bodies fail.”

“Death?”

“Does not transfer. The burden stops with the dead seat and recoils across whoever remains.”

Dima came to the pan. His hands carried honey from Piri’s bandage. “You knew this could be done.”

Savra kept the seal turning.

“I knew the notation existed.”

“That wasn’t my question.”

Before she answered, the copper eye clicked.

A thread no thicker than hair uncoiled from inside the seal. It had been wrapped around a ceramic spindle beneath the metal rim. Vinegar loosened the binding resin. Savra caught the thread on her splint and drew it free without touching skin.

Copper wire held three knots of blackened salt paste. Each knot aligned with a serving bell notation.

Teren set Ruva’s symptom record beside it. “Two-one. One-two-three.”

“Breakfast companies and midday order,” Savra said.

“Active, then.”

“Active yesterday. We need a heat response to prove it remains so.”

Dima’s expression hardened. “You’ve proved enough for Piri.”

“For a witness, yes. Not for a salt guild or a town council. They’ll say this came from an old campaign seal and the injuries followed panic.”

“They said that before?”

Savra heard the question he had not asked: Who said it, and where were you?

She took a chickpea crumb from yesterday’s ruined-cake basket. Dima caught her wrist.

“No more tasting.”

“I don’t need to swallow.”

She pressed the crumb against the seal’s outer ring and held it over steam. Official flour residue answered the grain oath. One black paste knot softened. Heat traveled along the copper thread toward the military arc. The civilian sockets darkened first.

Cold seats, Hall tablewrights called them. Not because the bodies felt no heat. Because they sat away from the visible hearth, unnamed and unable to touch the fire that governed them. Their distance made them useful reservoirs. A feastwright could drain fatigue, pain, or hunger toward those positions while declared diners experienced only the relief.

Teren spread the village record beside the steaming dish and sorted injuries by the seal’s four broad serving groups. Ruva’s spear pain clustered under the mounted-company arc. Daret’s dislocated jaw aligned with a smaller notch for cart crews, likely a driver struck by a lurching pole. Iskar’s saddle aches belonged under remount handlers. The match did not identify the soldiers, but it connected categories of military work to categories of civilian receipt.

“Could the seal send pain the other direction?” he asked.

“Yes. Weighting has direction, not morality.”

“Could villagers make soldiers carry this back?”

“Not through a branch they don’t control. A recipe at the origin governs flow.”

“Then stealing the ledger won’t stop tonight’s meal.”

“No. It tells us which ember entries sustain it and where the signatures came from. With those, we can break source seals instead of severing one kitchen at a time.”

Dima took the charcoal copy and compared Piri’s open-square mark with the complete seal. “If we break the source, where does his share go?”

Savra appreciated the question because it refused the easy shape of rescue. “Across every seat still active when the break happens. If we extinguish the cavalry origin during service, burdens recoil through soldiers and concealed civilians together. Some may receive less. Others may receive a last concentrated share.”

“So you wait until no one is eating.”

“If the table ever empties. Campaign kitchens overlap meals and keep ration bread in soldiers’ mouths between bells. A sustained bond may run for days.”

Teren tapped the midday pattern. “Change of company, not an empty hour.”

“Probably.”

He revised a note on the invoice. No safe severance assumed. Savra watched him underline it once. Most officers treated uncertainty as a gap to cross. He treated it as a weight added to the load, though she did not yet know whether that made him safer.

Seli laid her linen strips beside the seal. “What did the soldiers lose when Piri took the wrist?”

“Pain, swelling, and some loss of function spread away from the injured rider,” Savra said. “The break itself remained where force made it unless enough of the bodily consequence shifted early. Piri received a matching break because his weighting carried too much at the instant of injury.”

“Then two children can break from one soldier.”

“Conservation prevents unlimited copies. Several bodies can carry fractions. A badly weighted small body may still fail under its fraction.”

Seli returned to Piri without accepting the distinction as comfort.

Savra explained the remaining notation without using the Hall’s euphemism until Teren stopped her.

“What do you call the positions?”

She looked at the tiny punched square for unregistered children. “Cold seats.”

Seli crossed the room and stood beside her brother’s litter. “Say what that means where he can hear it.”

Piri’s eyes had opened.

Savra forced herself to face him. “Soldiers ate from a table that sent their injuries into people they did not name. Your breakfast put you in one of those hidden places. You did nothing to cause it.”

“Did I break the soldier’s hand?”

“No. The soldier’s injury broke yours.”

“Does he still hurt?”

“Less than you.”

The boy considered that answer. “Then he should know.”

No Hall notation accounted for knowledge.

Boots scraped the street grate overhead. Haro raised one finger. Everyone went still.

An inspector outside asked a neighbor whether smoke had risen from the dyehouse. The neighbor complained for a long time about soldiers ruining her saffron inventory. Kelar’s market people had learned to weaponize detail. After a minute, the boots moved on.

Savra lowered the pan. With cooling, the hidden lines began to fade.

Teren copied them onto the margin of his invoice, starting with the hooks and ratio strokes. His hand made a soldier’s block letters, poor at curves. She corrected three marks, then took the charcoal from him and completed the diagram herself.

“You write it as though you’ve used it,” he said.

Dima closed her eyes once.

Savra could lie. The steam would cool, soldiers would leave, and Teren would carry proof to whatever council still listened. Her knowledge could remain a useful accident. Yet they planned to challenge a live military table. A hidden cook endangered everyone beside the fire.

“I trained in the Hall of Measures,” she said.

Teren did not move.

“I served as an imperial feastwright. Field tables, march provisions, ration bonds. Rovan Dast supervised my work.”

“How long?”

“Long enough.”

“That’s not a number.”

“Seven years in field design. I left ten years ago.”

Dima’s voice came quiet. “After the villages started waking hurt?”

“Yes.”

“Before or after you believed them?”

Savra looked at her bandaged palms. “After.”

Silence held no forgiveness. Good.

Teren finished one notation line before setting the charcoal down. “Did you make this seal?”

“No.”

“Can you identify who did?”

“Not from ratio work. Hall hands copy one another by rule.”

“Can you stop the source?”

“Not with a branch seal. The field kitchen keeps an ember-ledger for every active signature. Break the entry, kill the origin ember, or alter the recipe at service.”

“Where?”

“Cavalry kitchens stay behind the east barracks while quartered. If the army moves, they use the old tileworks road.”

Haro descended from the stair. “Army carts started leaving the east gate at dawn. Flour carriers went with them.”

Teren opened a road map drawn on the front of his toll invoice. “Tileworks road crosses the canal three miles north. One field stop before Ember Bridge.”

“Too exposed for an attack,” Dima said.

“We don’t attack,” Teren replied. “We carry flour.”

Savra studied him. Mud still dried on his coat from entering through the drains. His left ankle binding had bled damp through the leather, though he had not mentioned pain once. He counted risks as if naming them prevented concealment. The habit could become another way to command, but for now it offered a surface she could test.

“Field-kitchen carriers have clay permits,” she said. “Meal officers check the thumbprint under hot oil.”

“I have a printer who can lift one.”

“Oven crews inventory workers by hand burns.”

He looked at her wrapped palms. “You have fresh ones.”

“They need two carriers.”

“My shoulders can pass for flour work.”

Dima stepped between them. “Yesterday a child’s wrist broke. Today you mean to walk into the fire that did it.”

“Today we know the fire can move,” Savra said. “Tomorrow its ledger may be beyond Kelar.”

She lifted the complete seal from the cooling spoon. Once dry, the hidden notation vanished beneath glaze again. Teren wrapped both it and the Iskar fragment in separate cloths. He placed them in the inner compartment of his document pouch, under the symptom record and above the road map.

Custody spoken aloud mattered when people did not trust one another.

“Teren Avo holds the complete seal and village fragment,” Savra said. “Dima Orr and Seli witnessed the opening. I copied the weighting.”

Teren repeated the statement exactly.

Piri shifted on the litter. “Bring back the soldier’s name.”

Savra could not promise that an ember-ledger listed diners. It would list units, hearths, weights, and service times. The boy wanted a person where the seal supplied only a category.

“I’ll bring back every name it has,” she said.

Above them, the east garrison sounded the wagon bell. Axles answered from the road, one cart after another leaving Kelar.

Teren tightened his left boot. Savra banked the brazier and poured the vinegar into a dye jar so no inspector would find a fresh forensic pan.

They had until the moving kitchen lit its next meal fire.

Chapter 5 — Smoke over Kelar Market

Inspectors nailed the east smoke shutter open before noon, turning the market lane into a chimney.

Every oven had been ordered hot. Bakers burned proof fires under white-clay cups while soldiers compared the rising ash to registry tablets. Smoke gathered beneath the awnings, escaped through the forced-open shutter, and marked each active hearth for the watchtower. No cook could bank a fire without attracting a spear butt against the door.

Savra walked through Kelar Market carrying a basket of spoiled onions. Teren followed six paces behind with a flour carrier’s shoulder cloth and his document pouch sewn under one arm. Haro had gone ahead to collect forged permits from a printmaker near the canal. Dima, Seli, and Piri waited in the dyehouse with thirty refugees due to move when the afternoon inspection changed shifts.

That plan lasted until Savra saw Piri’s door litter beside the ration scales.

Soldiers had emptied the dyehouse while she and Teren were gone. The refugees stood in two fenced lines between the chickpea sellers and spice row. Dima knelt with her hands laced behind her head. An inspector in a yellow Hall sash arranged stacks of official flatbread on the weighing table. Beside him, a caged brazier held one clean red coal.

Teren passed Savra without looking at her. “West awning. We count first.”

She kept walking.

Forty-one detainees. Twelve soldiers. Two market exits barred, canal steps watched by one archer. The Hall inspector had three assistants, one tending the ember and two pressing bread against a fresh ration seal. Refugees who accepted a loaf received a clay discharge token. None had eaten yet. Dima had delayed service by demanding a healer examine Piri’s splint.

The tokens created the trap’s second wall. Anyone released without one could be arrested at the smoke shutters for refusing lawful relief. Anyone carrying one had to surrender it at sunset roll, proving that official bread had entered the assigned household. The Hall did not need soldiers inside every room. It made food and passage validate each other.

A widow at the front of the second line turned her loaf over three times. She could not read oil strokes, but distrust had taught her where officials placed danger. The assistant slapped her knuckles and ordered her to hold it flat. Behind her, two small children watched the bread with the still attention of hunger. If one bit before Savra reached the fire, both might enter through a household grain oath even if only one name appeared on the register.

Dima had arranged the detainees by vulnerability while pretending not to. Piri’s litter blocked the first serving point. Three elders with weak lungs stood where smoke blew least heavily. Adults able to run occupied the fence ends. Seli had hidden a water knife beneath the litter blanket, useful for rope and almost nothing else. The healer could build an evacuation from people and furniture as readily as she organized a kitchen.

Teren shifted to the onion stall’s far side. His position gave him a straight line to the first fence joint and kept soldiers from seeing his injured ankle. Haro remained absent. No forged permit could matter now unless the trapped refugees left before the ration bond formed.

The yellow sash belonged to an oath registrar, not a trained feastwright. He could join grain and ember through an existing seal but could not alter weighting. The new seal on his table showed the same cavalry arc Savra had opened in vinegar steam.

Teren stopped at a smoked-onion stall and tested one as if price mattered. “Can it bind before swallowing?” he asked without turning.

“Food takes a seat at the first swallowed bite.”

“Then we move them before that.”

“Break both fences, twelve soldiers close the lane. Someone eats in panic.”

“What do you need?”

“The live coal.”

Teren weighed the distance to the brazier. “Sixteen paces.”

“Seventeen.”

“Your route?”

“Through the line.”

A soldier pushed Dima’s shoulder. “You asked for a healer. Here.”

The registrar glanced at Piri’s wrist and did not touch it. “Stable splint. Feed him.”

“Tell me what table,” Dima said.

“District relief.”

“Name the seats.”

The registrar’s gaze moved from Dima to the waiting line. “All lawful recipients in good standing.”

Piri spoke from the litter. “Am I good standing?”

A few market sellers laughed under their breath. The registrar heard contempt and responded with procedure.

“Give the child his portion first.”

An assistant lifted a flatbread.

Savra set down the onion basket.

She entered the fenced lane at its open end, joining three women from the west quarter. A guard held out his hand for her issue mark.

“Kitchen loss,” she said. “Morning register burned.”

“Name?”

“Veyra.”

The old answer came easily. It might still save her if she left the line, let Dima refuse until soldiers used force, and reached the tileworks before anyone connected the dyehouse seal to an imperial deserter. She could warn field kitchens later. She could not repair Piri’s second break.

The assistant pressed bread into the boy’s left hand.

Savra stepped over the fence rail.

“Don’t swallow,” she said.

The registrar turned. Recognition did not reach him, only annoyance at an unregistered cook interfering with service. “Return to your place.”

She took the flatbread from Piri. Its underside carried a fresh oil mark divided into four serving groups. Heat from the caged coal had softened the seal paste. The bond waited in the crumb.

“Who authorized this weighting?”

Market noise changed. Bakers understood the question even if they could not answer it. Soldiers understood only that a detainee spoke as an official might.

The registrar straightened his sash. “District tables fall under the Hall of Measures.”

“I asked for the authorizing feastwright.”

“State your office.”

Savra could feel Teren counting positions behind her. Dima looked up from the stones. Piri’s left fingers closed on nothing.

She raised the bread so everyone could see the cavalry strokes in the oil.

“Savra Kel,” she said. “Former master feastwright, western field provision.”

Her true name crossed the lane in a murmur. An old baker repeated it. A spice porter shouted it toward the west gate. The registrar lost color, which meant the Hall had kept her description alive.

“Arrest her,” he said.

Teren threw the smoked onion into the brazier cage.

Oil and skin flared. Two soldiers turned toward the fire. Savra broke the flatbread over the registrar’s seal, forcing crumbs into the wet lines before any detainee swallowed. She seized the preserving-salt jar from the weighing table and emptied it across the four arcs.

Salt alone did not erase a seat. It hardened boundaries. She needed the origin coal separated from the bread while the seal remained unfed.

A guard caught her bandaged right hand. Fresh blisters tore beneath the linen. Savra drove her forehead into his nose, pulled free, and overturned the bread stacks into the salt. The registrar reached for the seal. Dima surged up from her knees and locked both arms around the man’s waist.

“Every seat aloud,” Dima shouted.

No one answered.

Teren hit the first fence with the weighing-table bench. Thin wood split at its pegged joints. Refugees pushed through the gap. Soldiers could not lower spears without striking one another in the narrow lane.

Savra tore the caged brazier from its stand. The handle burned through her bandage. She dropped it onto the salted bread and kicked the cage door open.

The live coal rolled among crumbs.

Heat woke the seal.
