For one breath, every oil stroke gleamed. Savra saw civilian hooks reaching toward mouths that had not yet eaten, a prepared structure seeking bodies. She drove her chipped knife through the soft center and twisted. Ceramic fractured. The incomplete bond discharged into bread, salt, and the registrar’s own hand resting on the outer ring.

He screamed and collapsed with a rider’s knee pain. No civilian had swallowed. The burden had nowhere else to go.

A soldier swung a spear shaft at Savra’s head. Teren caught it on the broken bench, stepped inside the reach, and shoved the man into the weighing table. His left ankle buckled. He stayed upright by holding the spear.

“West awning,” he said.

Dima lifted Piri’s litter with Seli at the other end. Refugees scattered into spice row as bakers dropped their shutters, trapping smoke under the forced-open roof. Market sellers slashed awning ropes. Canvas descended across the lane in broad folds, hiding bodies and tangling soldiers.

Savra snatched the coal from the ruined bread with bronze sugar tongs. It still glowed. She pushed it into the onion basket, where Teren had lined the bottom with a small clay ember cup before their approach. Ash from the ration brazier clung to its red surface. A live signature from the active field kitchen could answer permit seals and help them trace the source.

She covered the cup with onion skins, leaving two vents.

“Move.”

Fenugreek dust filled the west awning after a fleeing porter struck a spice bin. Smoke caught it and turned the air bitter enough to blind. Savra followed the sound of Piri’s litter rather than sight. Her torn palms left damp prints on stall boards. Behind her, the registrar called her name again, this time as a charge.

At the lane’s end, Haro cut through an awning seam with his spear knife. “Permits are gone. Printer burned them when soldiers came.”

“Then we lift new ones at the field stop,” Teren said.

“You’re limping.”

“I was limping yesterday.”

They crossed roofward through a ladder stall. Dima and the refugees continued at ground level beneath closing awnings while Haro led them toward Free Granary grain carts hidden in the old pottery yard. Savra climbed because the basket could not survive a crowd. Teren followed on his right leg and used both hands to spare the left ankle.

Tiles above Kelar retained rain in their hollows. Savra moved along ridge beams, jumped one alley, then slid beneath a laundry frame as an archer climbed from the market watch. Teren kicked a water jar behind them. It broke across the tiles and took the archer’s feet away without sending him over the edge.

“No killing if delay works,” Teren said.

“You issue that rule while running?”

“I issue it before people improvise.”

At the spice merchants’ court, they descended through a saffron loft. A widow opened her trapdoor, saw Savra’s face, and said, “The Hall wants you alive.”

“Unkind of them.”

The widow pointed toward the canal wall. “Dyers’ runoff gate. Haro paid me yesterday.”

Teren handed her two silver halves anyway. His accounting included debts no one had invoiced.

They reached the runoff channel while bells sounded an arrest closure behind them. Gray water rose to Savra’s knees. The ember cup hissed each time the basket dipped. She held it above her head and passed beneath the wall through a tunnel crusted with mineral blue.

Outside Kelar, Free Granary carts waited in a stand of pollarded willows. Only two carried grain. The others held people beneath reed mats. Dima had already loaded Piri and the injured refugees. Haro counted heads twice, finding forty-one on the second count because an old man had hidden under a cart to avoid slowing it.

Soldiers closed the canal gate.

Teren spread his road map on a wagon board. “The field kitchen stopped last night at the east tileworks. It moves north on the old kiln road, then serves before Ember Bridge. Flour carts join here.” He touched a fork six miles ahead. “We approach on foot before dawn. The refugee wagons take the canal track to the bridge.”

Dima looked at Savra’s torn hands. “You can’t carry flour.”

“The inspectors saw burns. The field crew expects them.”

“Piri asked for names.”

“I remember.”

He did not ask her to stay. That restraint cost him.

Savra removed the ember cup from the onion basket. The coal pulsed red beneath military ash, still alive after market smoke, rooftop rain, and canal water. Teren nested the cup inside a sand-filled transport jar, leaving the lid vented and the handle wrapped against accidental contact.

They tested the vent before moving. Too little air would kill the signature before dawn; too much could ignite the packing straw or let ash touch a wagon hearth. Savra set the jar inside an empty grain measure and wedged it with three wet clay tiles. Teren assigned no one else to carry it. If one of them fell, the other would take custody and speak the transfer aloud.

By sunset, Kelar’s walls stood behind them and the old kiln road opened north through abandoned clay pits.

Savra walked beside Teren with one live stolen ember between them.

Chapter 6 — The Ledger Under the Oven

Teren carried one hundred pounds of flour into the imperial field kitchen because ninety would have looked comfortable.

The sack rested across both shoulders, balanced behind his neck so its weight traveled down the straighter leg. His left ankle had stiffened overnight. Every fifth step sent a dull line upward through the shin, but field laborers did not arrive with clean boots or sympathetic joints. He kept pace with the six hired carriers ahead of him.

Savra walked two places behind, a sixty-pound sack on her back and linen wound around both palms. She had rubbed soot into the fresh bandages to make them look older. The sand-filled transport jar hung beneath her flour cloth, warm against her ribs. Their live stolen ember had breathed all night through a hole no wider than a grain stem.

Dawn had not cleared the clay pits. Red kiln walls rose around the field encampment, broken by ramps where carts once hauled roof tile. The cavalry kitchen occupied the largest firing yard. Four canvas roofs joined around a brick oven built against the old kiln face. Cookfires showed as low orange wounds under iron grates.

Teren counted fifty-six tents, three hundred tethered horses, fourteen ration carts, and at least two infantry companies camped beyond the north rim. Ember Bridge lay another eight miles along the road. Dima’s refugee wagons had taken the canal track. If both groups maintained ordinary speed, Dima would reach the bridge near sunset. The army could reach it before midday.

A sergeant at the flour gate slapped each sack and checked each carrier’s hands. Legitimate crews bore old burns across thumb webbing from lifting hot bread trays. Savra’s damage passed easily. Teren had rope calluses and one crescent scar from a wagon brake, insufficient for oven work.

“Permit,” the sergeant said.

Their printer’s papers had burned in Kelar. Teren handed over a market transfer token Haro had taken from an abandoned grain cart. It authorized six measures of chickpea flour, not two unregistered people.

“This has no hand assignment.”

“Market registrar said field kitchens had lost half their carriers,” Teren replied.

“Market registrar doesn’t staff my yard.”

Behind them, another eight sacks approached. Delay would create witnesses. Savra shifted under her load and let a little air escape the hidden transport jar. Military ash warmed the token in Teren’s hand. Its oil mark darkened as the stolen signature answered.

The sergeant saw a live seal response where no response should have existed. Bureaucracy supplied the explanation he preferred.

“Late validation,” he said. “Hold it closer next time.”

He pressed Teren’s right thumb into hot sesame oil and then onto the token. Pain cleared Teren’s face of any need to pretend. The sergeant inspected the print, saw no registry match because he had no comparison tablet, and waved them through.

“One meal only,” he said. “Return tokens at second bell.”

Teren lifted the sack again. The burned thumb found every coarse thread in the cloth.

Inside, labor followed a sequence more disciplined than many infantry drills. Flour entered the west roof. Sifters removed stone and husk. Kneaders mixed dough in troughs long enough for a body to lie down in. Bakers shaped ration loaves, pressed their undersides to oiled seals, and moved them through the brick oven on flat paddles. Finished bread cooled beneath the east roof, where meal clerks stacked it by company.

No one ate in the kitchen.

Savra had warned him. A feastwright who stood outside the declared table could work without receiving its heat or fatigue. Kitchen laborers often occupied no seat either. Their burdens stayed their own.

Teren dropped his flour onto the west inventory mat. A clerk marked one hundred pounds without weighing it, proving the disguise had chosen the right excess. Underloaded carriers invited suspicion. Overloaded ones invited lazy gratitude.

“Water team,” the clerk said. “Both of you.”

They joined a line passing clay jars from a cistern cart to the kneading troughs. The work gave them reason to cross the yard and let Teren measure the oven. Old tileworks bricks enclosed a new iron throat. A cooling flue ran into the kiln wall at knee height, barred by a grate. Ash workers cleared it after each service. Beneath the oven mouth, one brick showed a thin green stain.

Copper hidden nearby.

Savra saw it too. She tipped her water jar half an inch, an agreed signal to wait.

Meal bells sounded from the north rim: one long, two quick. Infantry lined up at the east roof. Cavalry riders came later, still tightening girths and fastening armor as they collected bread. Every loaf had cooled only minutes. Oil on the undersides remained bright.

Teren watched a young spearman limp toward the serving clerk. The man’s heel carried an open blister stained through his sandal. He accepted a loaf, broke it at the company line, and swallowed while standing.

Relief changed his posture before the second bite.

His shoulder settled. Weight returned to the injured foot. The red stain remained, but the muscles around it stopped guarding. He walked away without a limp.

Elsewhere, someone received that pain.

A cavalryman arrived breathing hard from a horse drill. Two mouthfuls slowed his chest. Sweat cooled along his temples. Another soldier rubbed a swollen wrist until he ate, then flexed it against the ground as if testing a repair. Skill had not moved. Wounds had not vanished. The table redistributed heat, pain, and muscular cost so efficiently that the camp looked rested.

Teren carried another water jar. Numbers imposed themselves. Three hundred riders. Perhaps four hundred infantry. If each sent only a quarter of daily fatigue outward, the receiving villages took the labor of one hundred seventy-five people without performing it. Add injuries and repeated meals, then multiply across a campaign. A province could feed an army grain and bodies without seeing either requisition entered in a ledger.

The cooling racks showed how the table avoided empty hours. Breakfast loaves carried a straight oil stroke and remained active until the soldiers digested them. Midday rounds already waited with a hooked stroke designed to overlap that duration. At dusk, sour broth would accelerate the evening share before the first bread released its hold. The kitchen did not need one permanent meal. It laid temporary bonds across one another like roof tiles so rain never found a gap.

That method cost grain. Teren counted racks, loaf size, and company stacks. The camp issued nearly a third more food than its visible head count required. Extra bread fed bond continuity rather than muscle. Free Granary scouts had reported imperial units requisitioning beyond appetite, and Teren had assumed officers were hoarding. Some likely did. Here, waste served timing. Stale end pieces remained under seal for night pickets, ensuring their seats stayed reachable even when the soldiers did not need calories.

He marked the implication for the bridge. A cavalry unit arriving already fed could carry its bond across the crossing without a cookfire on the causeway. Destroying the kitchen cart would not free civilians until digestion or origin severance. Any defense based on stopping meal service would begin hours too late.

At the far serving table, a soldier collapsed before eating. Two cooks dragged him behind a screen. No one used a healer’s call. A feastwright in a clean brown apron brought broth from a lidded pot, checked a company seal, and spooned it between the man’s lips. His trembling eased.

Savra set down her empty jar. “Origin pot,” she murmured as Teren passed.

“Ledger under the green brick?”

“Likely custody tube. Oven flue opens after second service.”

“When?”

“Forty minutes if they keep standard heat.”

Teren looked toward the north road. Three cavalry units had begun striking tents. “We have twenty.”

A rider in a captain’s red-edged coat entered the kitchen yard. He carried his helmet under one arm and no visible injury. Men straightened around him without fear. Captain Orel Tas, according to the quartermaster’s boards Teren had studied before the occupation. Tas had refused a winter order to burn Eren Ford’s mill because the canal would have flooded three loyal districts. The same officer now commanded troops who stood upright on hidden civilians’ pain.

Tas spoke to the brown-aproned feastwright. “The bridge company moves first. Keep them fed through crossing.”

“Next loaves leave in thirty minutes.”

“Make it fifteen.”

“Oven heat won’t answer command.”

“Then issue what’s cooled and send the rest by cart.”

Not a sadist. Not ignorant of logistics. He simply treated unseen harm as one more ration source because the institution made it available.

Teren passed close enough to hear Tas lower his voice.

“Kelar reported a seal broken by Savra Kel. Does this table remain secure?”

The feastwright wiped his spoon. “The branch recoiled. Origin integrity held.”

“Can she trace it?”

“Not without our ledger or ember.”

Tas glanced across the working yard. “Inventory both.”

Teren continued walking. His burned thumb left a wet mark on the jar handle.

Savra reached the cistern cart and stumbled deliberately. Her vessel fell against the wheel, cracking at the neck. Water spread beneath stacked jars. The clerk shouted. Savra caught the wheel rim with bandaged hands, tore linen loose, and showed fresh blood.

“Useless market hire,” the sergeant said.

He ordered Teren to take her to the ash station for rewrapping, which moved them behind the oven.

“Inventory is coming,” Teren said once the brickwork hid their mouths.

“I heard.”

“Can we open the flue hot?”

“Not without pulling fire through it.”

“Could that look like a fault?”

“It could become one.”

They reached the ash station. An old laborer kept salve, linen, and three long rakes beside a trench of cooling cinders. Savra held out her hands for treatment while Teren inspected the flue grate. Iron pins held it at top and bottom. The lower pin had warped from heat, leaving a gap large enough for two fingers.

“Maintenance?” Teren asked the laborer.

“After the loaves.”

“Clerk sent us now.”

“Clerk can crawl in himself.”

Savra hissed as salve touched her torn palm. “Smoke pulled west over the last batch. Feastwright wants the draw checked before bridge loaves.”

The laborer looked toward the serving roof, where officers now demanded haste. He did not want responsibility for delayed bread.

“Five minutes,” he said. “If you crack a brick, I never saw you.”

He loosened the upper pin and went to fetch a smaller rake.

Teren pulled the grate. Oven breath struck his face, dry enough to close the throat. The flue ran under the baking chamber for six feet before turning upward into the old kiln. Its floor held warm ash and broken tiles. No person his size should fit. That did not change the space available.

Savra retied her bandages herself. “Copper tube sits beneath the oven lip, probably accessible from the turn. Keep your coat wet.”

“What keeps the ledger from burning?”

“Air gap and clay sleeve.”

“What marks the sleeve?”

“Green salts at the seam.”

“Alarm?”

“Removing copper may interrupt the origin coil.”

“May?”

“I did not build this oven.”

Teren soaked his coat in the spilled cistern water, wrapped cloth across his mouth, and entered feet first so he could retreat faster. The left ankle objected when he folded it. He put the right leg under his weight and pulled himself inward on his elbows.

Heat erased distance. After two body lengths, the yard sounds flattened into knocks and scrapes carried through brick. Ash entered his sleeves. Each breath through wet cloth tasted of old bread and metal.

At the bend, the flue narrowed. Teren rolled onto his right side. The old ankle dragged behind him, boot scraping brick. He found the green seam where Savra predicted, beneath a lip supporting the oven floor. A clay sleeve had been mortared into the cavity. Its cap faced away from the ash flow.

He tried to turn it. Heat had fixed the cap tight.

Outside, a rake struck the grate. The laborer had returned.

Savra said something too quiet to hear. The rake withdrew.

Teren braced his burned thumb against the cap and twisted with both hands. Skin stuck to hot clay. The sleeve opened a quarter turn, then stopped against grit. He took the narrow iron tally pick from his belt, inserted it along the seam, and worked the obstruction loose one grain at a time.

A bell rang above the oven.

Not a meal call. Inventory.

Teren freed the cap.

Inside lay a cylinder of rolled copper sheets around a black iron spindle. Fine wires ran from the outer sheet through holes in the clay sleeve toward the oven. Each wire carried an ember’s ash signature to a scored ledger line. Removing the cylinder would break contact, though perhaps not immediately if heat sustained the last reading.

He could copy nothing in the flue. He pulled the iron spindle free.

The wires snapped in sequence.

Pain traveled through the brick as a sound. Soldiers above stamped or cried out. Burdens that had been held away returned by fractions as the origin coil lost its recorded routes. The table did not collapse because the active seal and food remained. It shuddered.

Teren shoved the cylinder inside his wet coat.

A hand seized his boot.

The old laborer pulled. Teren kicked free with his right foot and drove himself past the bend. His left ankle caught between bricks. For one second the joint took his full weight at the wrong angle. White pain narrowed the flue.

Then Savra’s arm entered, bandages black with ash. She gripped his belt and pulled while he straightened the foot. Together they dragged him out beside the ash trench.

The brown-aproned feastwright stood ten paces away.

He held the stolen transport jar with its lid open. The red coal inside answered the oven’s failing signature. At his feet lay flour cloth and onion skins.

“Savra,” he said.

She rose without denying it. “Who wrote the child class?”

His face changed. “You opened the Kelar seal.”

“Teren,” she said.

They ran.

The feastwright shouted for the gate. Teren took the copper cylinder from his coat and passed it to Savra because his hands needed balance. They crossed the kneading roof, overturning no troughs and wasting no food. Workers moved aside once they saw soldiers coming.

Captain Tas stepped into their route with a drawn sword.

“Give me the ledger,” he said.

“Ask where your blisters went,” Teren answered.

Tas’s gaze dropped to Teren’s burned thumb, then shifted to soldiers behind him who had begun limping as distribution faltered. He did not lower the blade.

Savra threw the sand jar at the yard’s proof brazier.

It struck iron and burst. Sand smothered the proof flame while the stolen coal rolled beneath a ration cart. Every permit token waiting for validation went dull. Carriers surged at the gate, waving unresponsive clay and demanding their pay. For three breaths, procedure blocked soldiers more effectively than a barricade.

Teren and Savra went over the low kiln wall.

They landed in powdered red clay. Teren’s left foot folded, and he rolled down the pit slope rather than fight it. Savra slid after him with the ledger held above her head. Arrows struck the clay where they had crossed.

Old drainage trenches joined the pits below. They ran bent under banks cut by generations of rain. Savra chose north whenever paths divided. Teren tracked hoof noise above. Cavalry spread along the rim but could not descend without circling to the cart ramps.

At the third culvert, they stopped long enough to open the cylinder.

Copper sheets unrolled into concentric leaves, each punched with hearth hooks, location marks, active status, and service dates. Teren counted by columns while Savra read.

“Thirty-two western basin,” she said. “Twenty river. Fifteen salt uplands. Eighteen terraces. Twelve capital approaches.”

“Ninety-seven hearths.”

“Prepared and keyed.”

Teren found three blank channels on the iron spindle. “A hundred positions.”

“Three entries still absent.”

Along the sheet edge ran a sequence of sun marks and harvest cuts. One date had been crosshatched in black salt lacquer.

“Equinox,” Savra said.

“That’s twenty-nine days.”

“Timing for simultaneous service. The ledger does not state purpose.”

It stated scale. Ninety-seven prepared hearths could bind districts larger than any field army. Three empty positions waited for signatures not yet entered. Teren looked for Kelar’s hook but could not distinguish it among the crowded lines without clean steam and time.

Horns sounded above the clay pits.

A cavalry messenger rode north on the bridge road. Another followed, then a full squad. Tas had chosen the route they would protect first.

Savra rerolled the copper ember-ledger around its spindle and wrapped it in her blood-stained flour cloth. “The refugees.”

“Eight miles ahead if Dima kept pace.” Teren calculated rider speed, wagon speed, and the bridge’s single stone causeway. “The army reaches them before noon.”

They climbed from the drainage trench through scrub on the north side. Behind them, the field kitchen struck alarm bells. Ahead, dust rose where cavalry units moved to seal Ember Bridge.

Teren took the ledger from Savra so she could use both injured hands on the slope. They ran toward the bridge with ninety-seven hearths knocking against his ribs.

Chapter 7 — The Ember Bridge

The first cavalry arrow landed in a sack of rice and emptied breakfast across the bridge stones.

Savra dropped behind the wagon wheel as a second arrow struck the axle above her shoulder. Red grains rolled between paving joints and bounced into the gorge. Forty paces south, imperial riders formed two lines at the road bend. Their horses still carried tileworks dust.

“Not shooting to kill yet,” Teren said beside her.

“They want the wagons.”

“They want the ledger.”

“Both fit on a wagon.”

Ember Bridge crossed a volcanic cut where the basin’s eastern canal plunged through black rock. Three basalt arches carried a road broad enough for two grain carts abreast. The bridge had taken its name from fire-colored stone beneath each parapet, not from any hearth. At the northern end, a tollhouse barred the road with an iron-bound gate. Its keeper had locked it when Captain Orel Tas’s messenger arrived, trapping six Free Granary wagons, four refugee carts, and more than eighty people along the span.

The gate could open from either side if they broke its chain. Haro’s people worked beneath arrow cover with a grain hook and hammer. The chain had links thick as a thumb.

South of the bridge, Dima’s last cart stood sideways across the approach. Refugees crouched behind flour sacks and bundles of bedding. Piri lay under the cart, splinted right wrist protected against his chest. Seli had wrapped wet cloth around the wheel hubs in case soldiers used fire.

Savra counted distances. Road bend to first wagon, forty paces. First wagon to south parapet, twelve. Full bridge length, one hundred and sixty. Northern gate, another ten. Seven cookfires burned or smoldered among the carts: three enclosed braziers, two clay stoves, Dima’s medicine fire, and a baker’s charcoal pan. Different ash-signatures. Different foods. Enough for a table if joined deliberately.

Not enough time for a good one.

Captain Tas rode forward under a small square shield. “Savra Kel. Return the Hall ledger and the civilians cross unharmed.”

Teren looked at the refugees. “He may keep that promise.”

“Until their next meal.”

“I know.”

Tas stopped beyond spear reach. His soldiers appeared rested from bound rations, though sweat darkened the horses. “The bridge gate opens when I hold the ledger.”

“Then order your keeper to open it first,” Teren called.

“I did not close the gate.”

“Your message did.”

The distinction sounded sincere. Savra disliked him more for making it.

A Free Granary escort named Varo crawled from the northern tollhouse. “Chain pin’s fixed inside the stone. Half an hour.”

Cavalry could take the wagons in five minutes.

Teren drew the copper ledger from beneath his coat. The field-kitchen alarm had gone ahead of them. Tas’s riders knew the theft; they might not know what the sheets contained. If Teren threw the cylinder into the gorge, the empire retained source copies or memories. The coalition would lose the only evidence that could persuade distant cities.

“We hold twenty minutes,” Teren said. “Haro breaks the gate. People cross by priority.”

“With which fighters?” Dima asked.

“Fourteen escorts. Eight market volunteers with poles.”

“Against sixty riders?”

“Road holds six abreast.”

“Six rested abreast.”

Savra studied the seven fires. A bridge table could spread hauling exertion across people not pulling, share impact pain between defenders and seated volunteers, and move body heat toward anyone going into shock. It could not give a potter spear skill. It could not prevent a blade from opening flesh. It could keep fourteen trained people on their feet long enough for eighty others to pass the gate.

It could also make every diner vulnerable to her weighting.

“I can build a table,” she said.

Dima’s expression closed. “From whom?”

“Only volunteers. Names spoken. Weights shown in salt. Duration until the seventh fire dies or the food releases. Severance by extinguishing each source, with recoil risk stated before anyone eats.”

“We have minutes.”

“Then I speak quickly.”

Teren looked south. Tas had withdrawn to his lines. Field cooks unloaded a lidded pot behind the riders. “Do it.”

Dima caught Teren’s coat. “You don’t command that answer.”

The rebuke stopped him.

Savra said, “No one commands it. We ask.”

They pulled the middle grain wagon crosswise, creating a preparation wall. Dima shouted for seven people responsible for the fires. An Eren Ford baker brought canal rice crusts. Two Kelar women carried chickpea cakes saved from the previous night. Haro contributed dried lentils from a scout bag. Each food had cooked or would cook above its own live ember; mixing small portions through one broth could join the signatures.

Savra refused the imperial ration grain. It carried an oath they could not read.

“Local stores only,” she said. “If you cannot name who milled it, keep it out.”

A man offered smoked goat fat from Tal Jes. Another produced salt fish traded through Ulsor. Savra used the fat to carry heat evenly and refused the fish because its guild salt could divide the table unpredictably. Bitter fenugreek from Dima’s medicine pouch entered the broth as a diagnostic. If any weight turned biased, its flavor would sharpen first.

Arrows struck the wagon wall. Tas’s soldiers had begun testing range.

Dima set a broad copper pot over the central clay stove. “Seat list.”

“First, jobs,” Savra said. “Defenders carry impact and muscular load. Haulers move wagons and break the gate. Seated sharers take pain, fatigue, and heat while remaining behind cover. Cooks carry ordinary share plus any imbalance we fail to place.”

“How many sharers?”

“As many as choose after hearing the cost.”

Dima climbed onto the wagon tongue. “Listen. This meal may make your body hurt where nothing strikes it. It may weaken you while someone else lifts. If a defender suffers a wound, you can carry part of the pain, heat, bleeding pressure, or loss of strength. The cut itself stays with the body struck, but enough transferred consequence can injure you. You may refuse now. Once you eat, the safest release comes when every fire goes out together. Breaking one early can throw its share onto the rest.”

No promise softened the words.

A cooper from the morning kitchen raised her hand. “My daughters go first through the gate. I’ll sit.”

“Name,” Dima said.

“Ressa Min.”

Seli stood. “Seli Var.”

Piri called from beneath the cart, “Me too.”

“No,” Seli said.

“I already know how.”

Savra crouched so he could see her. “Your right wrist is broken and your grain oath may still answer the imperial table. A second bond could overlap it. I will not seat you when we cannot measure that risk.”

His face tightened. “You said people choose.”

“Choice requires a limit the cook can safely offer. I cannot offer you this one.”

Dima watched her answer, neither approving nor forgiving.

Volunteers gave names. Ressa Min. Seli Var. Old Nima from Ruva, still guarding his ribs. Eda the reedworker. Noro the axlewright. Three bakers, two canal hands, a dyer, four parents, Haro, and every Free Granary escort. Teren took a defender’s seat despite his ankle. Savra listed herself among cooks.

Dima put her name last. “Double pain share.”

Savra looked at his hands. “No.”

“You told the boy the cook sets limits. Set one I can choose inside.”

“Your hands do the healing afterward.”

“My hands can take more than a child’s.”

“Size is not protection against concentrated nerve damage.”

“We need the defenders able to keep their grips.”

“Ordinary share.”

“Double pain. Ordinary fatigue. No extra heat.”

He had specified state and boundary as a trained organizer would. Savra could refuse him. Someone else would take the portion or the defenders would carry more. Refusal could become another way for the tablewright to preserve the person she considered useful.

“Double pain, ordinary fatigue, no extra heat,” she repeated. “You sit beside the medicine fire. If your fingers lose position sense, you tell me immediately.”

Dima held up both hands so witnesses saw them uninjured. “Agreed.”

Savra divided preserving salt into seven shallow bowls and set one at each fire. Lines ran from each bowl toward the central pot, visible on dark bridge stone. She used no hidden under-stroke. Weights appeared as notches cut in chickpea dough: one notch ordinary, two for double, a crossed notch for cooks. Defenders’ cakes carried one pain notch and two exertion notches. Haulers took the reverse. Seated sharers carried one pain and one fatigue unless they chose less.

The bridge wind complicated the geometry. A gust could scatter a salt line and separate one fire before the others, causing partial recoil. Noro cut seven channels into strips of damp clay and packed the salt inside them. Each channel ended at the central pot without touching another, preserving distinct source signatures. Dima assigned one keeper per line with a water cup for extinction and a dry cloth to shield the salt, then made each keeper repeat which fire they held.

Fuel also needed equal duration. The baker’s charcoal pan could outlast the medicine brazier by an hour, while one clay stove held only enough tamarisk for twenty minutes. Savra redistributed unlit fuel beside each source without moving live coals. She measured by burn rate, not by visual size: dense charcoal to the short stove, split brush to the slow pan, olive pits reserved for the central flame. All seven needed to survive the evacuation and die on the same count. A stronger fire offered no moral advantage if it trapped the other six inside its remaining bond.

Several volunteers chose less.

A pregnant woman named Osa took a quarter fatigue share and no pain. An elder with weak lungs accepted heat only. Two market porters chose double muscular exertion but refused injury consequence. Savra adjusted dough portions rather than shame them into symmetry. A table needed usable differences, not equal portions displayed for virtue.

Dima read every seat named from the list Teren wrote on the back of a grain invoice. Each person answered aloud. Haro stood at the edge and repeated the count: forty-six seats, including five cooks, fourteen defenders, nine haulers, and eighteen sheltered sharers. The seven fire keepers spoke their hearth sources. Savra announced duration, weighting, and severance again.

Captain Tas advanced his riders ten paces.

“You are binding civilians on a battlefield,” he called.

Savra tasted the broth. Fenugreek remained bitter but balanced. “I am naming them where you can hear.”

“Names do not stop arrows.”

“No. People do.”

She served the cooks first because a cook could not stand outside the burden. Dima drank from her medicine-fire cup. Savra swallowed her portion and felt forty-five other rhythms touch the edges of her own body. No thoughts. No loyalties. Only breath, hunger, fatigue, heat, and the small pains people already carried.

Teren ate next, then defenders, haulers, and sheltered sharers. Every diner held up the marked cake before biting so neighbors could verify the weight. No one received food by mistake.

The table settled across seven cookfires.

Savra felt the bridge through bodies. Teren’s left ankle burned with old damage. Dima’s shoulders carried years of kneading and lifting. Ressa had a cut beneath one heel. One escort trembled from fear, a state hearthbinding could spread as bodily rhythm but never turn into courage. Savra directed no emotion. She adjusted only declared states, using salt lines and portion marks already swallowed.

“Haulers,” Teren called. “Gate wagon first.”

Nine people leaned into the lead grain cart. Their muscular effort spread toward the double-exertion porters and defenders. The wagon rolled as if pushed by more hands, because more bodies performed fractions of the work from behind cover. No one gained knowledge of harness or wheel. Noro still directed where to place the lever.

Haro’s hammer struck the toll chain.

Imperial cavalry charged.

Six riders entered the southern approach abreast. Free Granary escorts braced poles between parapets. They had no room for a full spear wall, but wagon shafts lashed crosswise reduced the lane. Horses refused the first barrier. Two riders forced through and met hooked grain tools rather than swords.

Impact traveled across the table.

Savra’s shoulder jolted though she stood at the pot. Dima grunted beside the medicine fire. Sheltered sharers cried out in scattered sequence. The defenders kept their grips because the force no longer belonged to their arms alone.

“First cart through,” Teren shouted.

The gate chain lost one link. Haro and Noro dragged it from the stone socket. Refugees pushed the iron-bound leaves outward while the grain wagon squeezed through. Its hubs scraped both posts. Beyond lay open north road and a steep track toward river country.

Seli led children after the cart in pairs. Piri crossed on his door litter carried by two haulers. He kept his right arm against his chest and stared at the fighting until Dima ordered him to look north.

A rider thrust over the wagon-shaft barrier. The spear entered an escort below the left collarbone. Flesh opened; no table could move the hole. Pain and shock spread outward. Dima took twice the ordinary portion. His knees hit stone, but his hands remained around a bandage roll.

Savra shifted heat from the wounded escort toward three volunteers who had accepted it. She moved no blood. The man still needed pressure on the wound. Dima crawled to him and packed linen around the spearhead before defenders cut the shaft.

“Fingers?” Savra called.

“Working.”

A second cavalry rank dismounted and came forward on foot, using horses as moving shields. Captain Tas had adjusted rather than spend animals against the barrier. His men pushed the first wagon sideways to open the approach.

Teren saw the change. “Oil the stones.”

Two cooks poured smoked-onion oil from a ration jar across the south slope. They did not light it. Fire would trap refugees and damage the bridge. Boots slipped where hooves would have found purchase. Imperial soldiers advanced more slowly, shields touching.

The second and third wagons passed the gate.

With each load, haulers tired. Their fatigue flowed into seated volunteers in declared fractions. Ressa’s head bowed. Nima vomited over the parapet, then drank water without leaving his marked place. Osa carried only a quarter share and remained steady enough to count children crossing.

Savra moved among the fires, tasting one spoon from each. Different foods revealed different stresses. Rice crust softened when exertion weighting rose. Lentils thickened as heat accumulated. Fenugreek sharpened near Dima’s double pain line.

She added no new ingredients. Changing the recipe mid-service without re-declaring could conceal a weight. Instead she redistributed by moving the existing salt bowls where everyone could see, one finger width at a time, and called each adjustment aloud.

“North fire, pain reduced one quarter. Central fire, cook share increased one quarter.”

Her own right hand flooded with the escort’s spear pain. The wound did not appear, but burned skin split again as muscles clenched. She accepted the physical consequence because she had named the cook’s share.

Tas’s shield line reached the barrier.

Teren fought with a grain hook. He knew convoy spacing, not sword forms. The table gave him no martial skill. Haro shouted when to pull, and Teren followed. Together they caught a shield rim and dragged its bearer into the oil. An escort struck the soldier’s helmet with a mallet, enough to stun without crushing his skull.

Imperial bread kept the attackers rested. Their field table drained exertion elsewhere as fast as Savra’s bridge table spread it among volunteers. Two systems met across twelve paces of stone: one concealed its receivers, the other called names through smoke.

The fourth wagon jammed at the gate.

A rear wheel had swollen after canal water soaked its rim. Noro crawled beneath the axle with a hammer while arrows struck the tollhouse. Teren ordered sacks unloaded. Refugees formed a line and carried grain through by hand.

Captain Tas stepped behind his shields. “Your table cannot outlast our ration.”

“It only needs to outlast the gate,” Savra said.

A field cook beside him lifted a copper witness spoon from the lidded pot. He touched it to a company seal, then pointed toward the bridge. The stolen ledger no longer governed their oven, but the active ration table retained Kelar’s branch notation from its last service.

Savra felt the imperial counter-bond before she saw its effect.

Cold pressure entered the southern fires. The enemy table was not joining the voluntary seats directly. It used overlapping grain oaths among refugees who had eaten imperial relief, trying to reopen concealed positions and pull bridge burdens outward through them. Piri screamed beyond the gate. His splinted wrist jerked.

“Stop the children’s line,” Savra called. “No one with oath bread moves farther from the fires.”

Panic rippled among the carts. Parents thought she meant to keep children on the battlefield.

“Distance does not sever them,” she shouted. “I need the overlap held still.”

She took the copper ember-ledger from Teren’s pack. Copper leaves carried the broken field routes. The Kelar hook darkened against her palm as the enemy spoon reasserted it.

The safe response would extinguish all seven fires together. That would end their table and throw current shares back across active diners. Defenders might collapse before the gate cleared. The imperial branch would remain.

The ledger offered an established route: break the specific entry. Savra unrolled the copper until she found the western basin sheet and matched the hooked well, split roof, and open square from Kelar’s seal. Three wires fed one punched channel.

“Dima, hold the central salt line.”

“With what hand?”

“Both, if you have them.”

He pressed his palms into salt at the medicine fire, physically maintaining its boundary while she worked. Pain weighting continued through him.

Savra used her chipped knife to sever the open-square wire.

Recoil struck every unregistered child who remained in the imperial table, including Piri. His body arched on the litter. The voluntary table caught only his declared absence, not his person; she had refused to seat him. Nothing she had built could receive his share.

Dima changed that with her body.

He broke his own marked cake in half and pressed one piece across the open-square wire while holding the medicine-fire salt. Food from the bridge table touched the imperial route. He had no authority to name Piri into a seat, but he could put his already declared double pain share in contact with the recoil.

The burden entered Dima’s right hand.

His fingers folded inward. Knuckles struck the stone. A cavalry rider’s crushed grip, Piri’s fracture consequence, and the escort’s spear pain converged through the line he held. Savra heard small bones or joints shift beneath his skin.

“Release it,” she said.

“Cut the next wire.”

“Dima.”

“Cut.”

She severed the hooked-well entry. Adults near the west refugee class cried out, but their hidden connection collapsed before the counter-bond could deepen. The split-roof wire followed. Copper snapped under her blade.

Imperial pressure left the seven fires.

Dima lifted her left hand from the salt. Her right remained on the stone, fingers curled without control. The double-share line in his cake had gone dark.

Savra moved to him.

“Gate first,” he said. “My choice. Finish it.”

The fifth wagon cleared. Then the sixth. Haro and four escorts retreated one barrier at a time while refugees carried the last flour sacks through the tollhouse. Tas’s men pushed forward, but their own table had shuddered when three civilian routes broke. Fatigue returned to knees and shoulders. The shield line lost its single rhythm.

Teren saw the opening and ordered no charge. “Withdraw north.”

Defenders backed through the gate. Two carried the wounded escort. Savra and the cooks lifted each fire onto its cart hearth or, where vessels could not move, transferred the live coals into named ember cups held by their keepers. Source identity remained intact. Salt lines shortened visibly as the table crossed the threshold.

Only the central stove stayed on the bridge.

Savra stood beside it with Dima. His face had gone gray. She splinted his right hand flat against a bread board without trying to force the fingers straight. Fine control had already vanished. Whether it returned depended on damage no hearth could wish away.

Captain Tas reached the south end of the span. His sword stayed lowered.

“You have nowhere to take ninety-seven fires,” he said.

“Neither do you,” Savra answered.

Teren shouted from the north road. “All carts clear.”

Savra called the severance sequence. Seven keepers answered by fire name. Six transport cups stood north of the gate. The central stove remained seventh. Every active diner had crossed except Savra and Dima.

“Extinguish on three,” she said. “Recoil returns by current weight. Sit down.”

People sat or braced against wagons. Teren knelt to spare his ankle. Dima placed her left hand around the stove’s sand cup.

Savra counted.

Seven fires died together.

The table released in one hard wave. Haulers sagged under the exertion returning to their own muscles. Defenders felt bruises gather where shared impacts had landed. Savra’s burned palms throbbed with their actual injuries. Dima made no sound when pain settled fully into her damaged right hand.

No hidden seat took the remainder.

Haro pulled the gate closed. The broken chain could not lock, but two wagons backed against it while the refugees moved north. Tas’s cavalry held the bridge without crossing. Perhaps he awaited infantry. Perhaps enough of his soldiers had felt their concealed supports fail to make another charge costly. The reason did not change the minutes gained.

On a rise beyond arrow range, Dima examined her hand. She could bend the wrist. The thumb moved slightly. Three fingers answered only with coarse flexion, and the index finger did not straighten. When she tried to pick up a needle, it slipped twice before she stopped.

“Dima’s right hand needs a proper splint and cold water,” Savra said.

“It needs work it can no longer do,” he replied.

“You chose a declared share. I still set the table that carried it.”

“Both statements stay true.”

He would not absolve her by claiming ownership, nor surrender his choice so she could claim all blame. Savra wrapped the hand while the survivors gathered around.

Teren set the copper ledger on a grain cart. “They’ll keep hunting this. People should know why.”

Savra unrolled the leaves. Forty-six diners who had heard every seat named stood with refugees who had refused, children excluded for safety, and laborers who had moved them all. Smoke from the extinguished cookfires trailed north in seven separate lines.

She read the numbers aloud: ninety-seven prepared provincial hearths, three positions waiting, simultaneous service marked for Equinox. She explained what black salt hierarchy could do when a primary fire held the weighting. No sheet stated Rovan’s purpose in words. His method stated enough. A hundred distant fires, one controlling recipe, whole populations entered through grain oaths, burdens directed by whoever stood at the origin.

“This is Rovan Dast’s Hundred Fires plan,” Savra said. “He means to make the basin eat from one table whether it knows the seats or not.”

Behind her, Dima held her splinted right hand against her chest. Ahead, the refugee carts turned toward the salt road and Ulsor.

Part II

Chapter 8 — Salt City Without a Table

Ulsor measured strangers by the water they carried uphill.

At the first brine terrace, a guild clerk stopped the refugee carts and emptied every jar into marked basins. Fresh canal water went into blue clay. Brackish water went into green. Anything touched by an official Namarra measure entered black vessels for boiling before use. No amount disappeared, but ownership changed shape under inspection.

Teren stood beside the scales and counted while a line of eighty-three people waited in salt glare. Two children had joined them from a canal hamlet; one bridge volunteer had returned south to warn family. They brought six grain wagons, four carts, thirty-one sacks of rice, nineteen of barley, eight of chickpeas, and enough water for half a day if the heat held. Ulsor’s gate rose another mile above them through terraces white with evaporation crust.

“Three jars short,” Teren told the clerk.

The clerk checked her tablet. “One leaked on the north road. Two carried mixed ash and went to black.”

“Black water still counts as water.”

“After boiling.”

“People thirst before your boil ends.”

“Then they may drink their blue allotment first.”

No insult entered her voice. Ulsor had survived imperial measurement by making its own rules more exact.

Terrace channels demonstrated the principle. Each brine pool received a numbered gate, but workers could close any one without draining its neighbors. Rakes, baskets, and evaporation dates belonged to separate crews; shared reservoirs fed them only through valves visible from the public path. Teren had expected a salt city to centralize what scarcity made valuable. Instead Ulsor multiplied boundaries so one bad measure could not spoil the whole season.

The arrangement sacrificed speed. Three clerks recorded water that one imperial assessor would have waved through. Seven vehicle lines occupied ground that could have held a warehouse. Yet no distant official could conceal a failed pool inside a provincial total. The city’s inefficiency looked deliberate, purchased with labor against the cost of trusting one hand. Teren counted that labor too, because autonomy still required someone to lift every gate.

Dima sat on the front cart with her right hand bound flat to a splint. Swelling had darkened the knuckles. She could lift the arm and close three fingers coarsely, but a water cup slipped unless Seli steadied it. Piri’s wrist rested in a better splint made at a roadside mill. Neither complained while Teren argued over jars. That made the accounting more urgent, not less.

Savra waited apart beneath a reed shade. She had given Teren the copper ember-ledger for the climb because both palms remained open beneath their bandages. People along the terraces recognized her name before her face. News from Ember Bridge had outrun wagons through salt merchants. Some workers stared with gratitude. Others touched two fingers to the ground against bad weighting.

A second clerk brought seven shallow salt trays. “One per vehicle fire.”

“Our fires are cold,” Haro said.

“Then the trays remain clean.”

Each wagon rolled across a white line poured between stone posts. Separate salt boundaries prevented accidental mingling of ash-signatures carried in hubs, cookpots, or spilled meal oil. Ulsor did not assume extinguished embers had forgotten where they came from.

Teren approved of the caution and disliked the delay. Both facts stayed true.

At the fourth terrace, Guildspeaker Mava Ul met them without escort. She wore a pale linen coat stiffened by salt at the cuffs and carried a hooked rake used to lift crust from brine pools. Her hair had gone white except for a dark band above one ear. Three guild seals hung from her belt, none resembling imperial measure marks.

“Teren Avo,” she said. “Your Free Granary owes us twelve barge measures.”

“Eight. Four burned at Vesk Ford under your contracted guard.”

“The guard protected people, not rice.”

“Then we owe eight measures and thanks.”

Mava considered him. “Thanks take less warehouse space.”

Her gaze moved to Savra and stopped. “Master Kel.”

“Not master,” Savra said.

“Rank does not vanish because the wearer dislikes its cost.”

“No. It also does not survive desertion as authority.”

Mava touched two fingers to the ground. Salt workers nearby repeated the warding gesture. “Keep her fire outside our lines.”

Dima shifted on the cart. “She has no live fire.”

“Then we agree easily.”

Ulsor opened its gate for refugees, wagons, and grain after recording each name of origin. It did not open a common kitchen. Families received lodging tokens assigned among storehouse lofts, fish-curing sheds, and empty kiln rooms. Every group got an unlit clay brazier stamped for its quarter. Guild law forbade one quarter from lending embers to another during first-night inspection.

Admission took the rest of the afternoon. Teren assigned Haro to the grain count and walked every lodging site himself. The fish sheds offered shade but retained salt in their floorboards, which would draw water from bare feet. Kiln rooms stayed cooler and had stone sleeping ledges, yet their vents opened toward brine pans where night insects gathered. Storehouse lofts kept bedding dry and placed too much weight above working warehouses. He distributed families by injury and mobility rather than by town, then changed the arrangement when Dima pointed out that separating witnesses from familiar caretakers would make every treatment slower.

Ulsor issued water at three measures per adult and two per child. Cooking took from the same allotment. Teren calculated barley at one and a quarter pounds per person for the first night, reduced for children, increased for terrace workers, then crossed out the whole figure because it treated people as standard mouths before any of them had accepted work. He asked each lodging group to name its own cook and report what grain its members could digest. The revised total differed by less than one sack. The process took an hour longer and prevented three old people from receiving barley they could not chew.

Dima established an infirmary in a disused weighing room. He directed splint work with his left hand and held bandage cloth under his right elbow while Seli cut it. When a guild healer offered to take over, Dima accepted examination but retained the patient list. “Help is not custody,” he said. The healer nodded as if Ulsor had written the same rule for bodies that it used for salt.

At the grain yard, Haro found two sacks damp from the bridge. Ulsor clerks would not credit them at full measure. Teren opened both, spread rice across reed screens, and paid three terrace children in clean barley to turn the grain through sunset. Nothing in the bargain could depend on spoiled weight disguised by intact cloth.

The delegation meal took place on a roof above the civic brine tanks. Wind moved across water cut into rectangles, each pool at a different stage of concentration. Beyond the terraces, pale uplands climbed toward salt quarries. No city wall enclosed Ulsor. Any army approaching across the flats would see the guilds drain canals and ruin a season’s harvest before surrendering it.

Servants brought pale barley, salt-baked river fish, sour plum paste, and brine herbs. Each delegate received separate bowls on an individual square of undyed cloth. No serving spoon passed between them. Mava’s fish came from one oven, Teren’s from another, Savra’s from a third. Dima ate barley softened in goat milk from a broad bowl she could brace with her left hand.

“Is this hospitality or quarantine?” Teren asked.

“Yes,” Mava said.

She sat with five guild assessors: brine, quarry, river trade, civic ovens, and oath disputes. Four seats remained empty for guilds that had declined to attend while Savra entered the city.

Teren ate before speaking. Refusing food would insult the shelter already given. Sharing carelessly would insult its boundaries. The fish flesh lifted cleanly from the bone, firm from salt packing and scented by herbs Savra identified as marsh rue.

“No table binds this meal,” Mava said. “Our separate bowls carry separate fires. We may agree without pretending one stomach.”

“That custom would improve half the Free Granary councils,” Teren said.

“Only half?”

“The other half would argue over who gets the larger bowl.”

Mava almost smiled.

Teren set his spoon down. “Rovan Dast has prepared ninety-seven provincial hearths for a simultaneous Equinox service. Three entries remain blank. We stole the working ledger from the army kitchen at Kelar.”

A quarry assessor shrugged. “Namarra makes ambitious feast lists every year. Half serve prestige and half never serve.”

“This one already moves soldiers’ wounds into civilian grain districts.”

“Your proof?”

Teren placed the document pouch on his cloth but did not open it until Mava provided a clean slate between them. Custody rules mattered more when evidence could bind through residue. He removed the complete Kelar seal with bronze tongs, then the Iskar fragment, village symptom record, bridge seat list, and copper ledger wrapped in flour cloth.

The civic-oven assessor leaned forward. “That cloth carries blood.”

“Savra’s hands,” Teren said. “No one ate from it.”

Mava assigned two witnesses to every object. A salt worker dusted the slate’s edges so no hidden line crossed into a delegate’s bowl. Only then did they unwrap the copper leaves.

Savra read the region columns without touching. “Thirty-two western basin. Twenty river. Fifteen upland. Eighteen terraces. Twelve capital approaches.”

The river-trade assessor counted punches. “Ninety-seven.”

“Look at active scoring,” Mava said.

Copper darkened where field heat had sustained live signatures. Twelve entries showed repeated service polish. Seven had received repairs. The Kelar branches Savra cut remained ragged at their holes.

Mava did not accept appearance alone. She sent for two thumb-sized cakes of compressed salt, one from Ulsor’s oldest civic line and one from a quarry fire never entered in imperial trade. The oath-dispute assessor placed each cake near the copper without contact. The civic salt developed faint dampness beside three upland punches, evidence that old shared transactions had left compatible ash traces. Quarry salt remained dry. When she moved the civic cake toward a western-basin entry, its surface roughened as live residue tried to align.

“Prepared,” the assessor said. “Not merely listed.”

Savra asked for a sliver of sour plum skin. Mava gave it only after a witness cut the piece on a separate tile. Held above the Kelar punctures, its acid vapor darkened all three severed wires at once. The reaction matched the active overlap Savra described at Ember Bridge. No person ate, no live ember entered the roof, and each test item went afterward into a brine crock marked for destruction.

The quarry assessor remained skeptical. “A live route proves connection, not intent. A floodgate also connects towns.”

Teren answered with quantities. “The military arc receives one part across twelve declared squad marks. Civilian classes divide that burden under multipliers, including double weight for unregistered children. Connection does not require that ratio.”

“Could a civilian tablewright have altered it after issue?”

“Not beneath fired glaze,” Savra said. “The under-layer entered before the registrar stamps. A Hall kiln made the bias or accepted it.”

Mava required both statements copied: proof of prepared connection and inference of military intent. She would not let urgency promote an inference into a witnessed fact. Teren respected the distinction, though every lost hour enlarged the empire’s advantage.

Teren presented Ruva, Daret Ford, and Iskar Fen by time rather than accusation. Different breakfasts. Local fires. Shared district grain oath. Identical meal bells. Injuries matching cavalry work. He then described the tileworks kitchen: soldiers eating marked loaves, fatigue and blisters easing, origin coil routes breaking when he removed the spindle.

The oath-dispute assessor interrupted. “You observed relief. You did not observe the same pain entering those villages at that moment.”

“No,” Teren said. “Distance prevents a single witness.”

“Then cause remains inferred.”

“Add the seal.”

Savra explained the cold-seat classes under vinegar steam, civilian hooks beneath the cavalry arc, and inverted fractions for unregistered children. She used a charcoal copy rather than reopen the ceramic near food. Piri held up his splinted right wrist when the open-square mark came under discussion.

Mava asked him, “Did you eat imperial grain?”

“Chickpea cake from our kitchen.”

“Did you see a soldier break his wrist?”

“No.”

“What did you feel?”

“A horse falling. Savra says I didn’t see it in my head. My body guessed.”

Savra had taught him the distinction. The boy repeated it without making the event cleaner.

Dima laid her splinted hand on the edge of her separate cloth. “At Ember Bridge, I took a declared double pain share. The imperial cook reopened Piri’s hidden class through the field table. I contacted the recoil while holding our medicine-fire line.”

“By choice?” Mava asked.

“Yes.”

“Could you withdraw at that moment?”

“Not safely without shifting my share onto forty-five other named people.”

“Then the first choice constrained the second.”

Dima looked at her motionless index finger. “That belongs in every instruction we give afterward.”

The civic-oven assessor examined his hand without touching it. She asked him to close each finger, oppose thumb to fingertips, and turn the wrist. Dima completed only the coarsest movements. No magic lingered. Damage had become bodily fact.

Mava listened to every witness before asking what Teren wanted.

“Shelter for the refugees through the hot month. Salt access for opening copied seals. A council of cooks and grain towns before Equinox. Permission to use one guild fire under your witnesses.”

“What do you offer?”

“Eight owed barge measures delivered from Eren Ford stores. Four additional as shelter payment. Free Granary priority for Ulsor fish and salt on any northbound empty hull. Copies of every hearth location we can read.”

“You do not control Eren Ford’s stores alone.”

“My sister keeps its communal oven. The river council holds the grain.”

“Then you offer what other people must approve.”

Teren felt the familiar urge to make the promise firm enough that approval would follow. “I offer to carry the proposal and bind no shipment without their vote.”

“That has smaller weight.”

“It is what I own.”

Mava turned to the brine assessor. They calculated shelter need on clay tablets: water per person, barley allotments, roof capacity, fuel, infirmary linen. Ulsor could absorb eighty-three people for twelve days without reducing salt workers’ winter reserve. Longer shelter required grain deliveries or labor exchange.

Teren accepted those conditions. Refugees capable of work could choose terrace wages, but no labor would offset their food automatically. Ulsor had learned that debt meals became another seat list if left unnamed.

The bargain reached provisional agreement. The four empty guild seats remained empty.

Mava then pointed at Savra. “No fire.”

Teren kept his hands on his own cloth. “You heard the evidence.”

“I believe the danger. I do not accept her at an Ulsor hearth.”

Savra said, “Those are separate judgments.”

Mava’s rake lay beside her seat. Salt scars crossed the wooden shaft. “At Selet Quarry, eleven years ago, workers woke with marching fractures. Forty-two could not return underground. My younger brother lost both knees while standing in a brine pan. Imperial records called it fever weakness.”

No one ate.

“A captured ration seal carried nine salt divisions,” Mava continued. “The Hall’s army crossed the uplands without blisters while our villages bore them. We call it the Nine-Salt campaign because Namarra never gave us another name.”

Savra’s face emptied of every defense except attention.

Mava spoke her old title in full. “Master Feastwright Savra Kel, western march design. Your rank appears on the campaign provisioning orders.”

Teren looked at Savra. At the dyehouse she had admitted field work and leaving after harmed villages. She had not named this table. The ledger between them seemed to gain physical weight.

“Were you present?” Mava asked.

“Yes.”

“Did you make its recipe?”

Savra’s burned hands rested palms down beside her untouched bowl. “I will answer in a hall where the harmed towns can send witnesses. Not in a closed meal with four guilds absent.”

“That sounds like delay.”

“It is a condition against private absolution.”

“Do not presume anyone here offers it.”

“I don’t.”

Mava considered her for a long time. “Until that answer, you do not touch a civic ember. You may cook over cold stone, clean vessels, and advise from outside the salt line. If anyone calls you master, I fine them.”

Savra inclined her head. “Agreed.”

The decision frustrated Teren because they needed her work. It also preserved the very local authority he wanted Ulsor to exercise against the empire. He could not ask a city to resist one master table while demanding that it accept his preferred feastwright.

Mava brought an evidence chest made from salt-cured cedar. Two guild assessors checked its clean interior. Teren transferred the complete Kelar hearthseal, Iskar fragment, village record, bridge witness list, and copper ember-ledger one object at a time. Each custody statement entered the guild tablet. Mava locked the chest with three independent seals and gave no single assessor all keys.

“Ulsor holds the evidence,” she said. “Shelter begins tonight. Council invitations go out at dawn.”

“And fire?” Teren asked.

Mava looked across the roof at the distinct bowls and the empty guild cloths.

“Ulsor offers no shared fire until Savra answers for the Nine-Salt campaign.”

Chapter 9 — A Mouth for the Empire

Issen certified the famine bread while servants carried roast lamb behind a screen.

The public course consisted of nine thin slices, one for each day the capital’s stores had failed twenty-eight years ago. Millet husk roughened the crumb. No oil softened it. A line of petitioners watched from the lower court while officials on the raised platform chewed in commemorative silence.

Behind them, concealed by embroidered linen, kitchen pages trimmed lamb fat into white curls for the private course that followed.

Issen swallowed the first slice. “Fit for the memorial measure.”

The phrase entered the record. It did not say fit for a meal.

Their silver tasting chain lay cold against the throat except at the hollow seventh clasp. The stolen grain of black hearth-salt had remained there for eight days. Issen cleaned the chain each night without opening that link. During sleep, they sometimes felt a child’s right wrist bend again, though no active bond remained. Ceremony trained a mouth to remember danger. Guilt needed no hearth.

Nine tables filled the Hall of Measures. Grain assessors sat nearest the dais, military provisioners next, then canal magistrates, guild delegates approved by Namarra, and foreign merchants whose gifts supplied the private kitchen. Empty benches along the walls represented districts unable to send delegates. The presiding herald named those absences as a sign of imperial reach: even those not present belonged to the common remembrance.

The petitioners entered by ration category. Widows of registered soldiers received clay markers for the lower court. Canal laborers stood behind a yellow cord. Displaced families occupied the outer arcade, where they could hear the herald but not see the tasting table. Their memorial bread came from the same batch, yet pages delivered it in different baskets so no recipient crossed into another administrative class.

Issen checked one basket from each line. The widows’ slices weighed a full civic measure. Laborers lost a finger-width to “workhouse adjustment,” on the assumption that their midday porridge supplemented the course. Displaced families received pieces cut by eye because many lacked current ration tablets. The Hall described all nine breads as common. Its knives disagreed.

They ordered the remaining loaves weighed before cutting. The distribution clerk protested that the memorial docket authorized ceremonial samples, not public allotment review. Issen invoked adulteration authority: unequal cuts changed the effective proportion of husk to crumb and therefore changed what each mouth tasted. The argument stretched law until it covered justice by a corner. It also delayed service seventeen minutes while hungry people watched scales move.

Rovan noticed from the first table. He could have overruled the order. Instead he told the clerk to comply and required officials’ slices reduced to fund the difference. By the fourth bread, every public line received equal weight. The private lamb continued roasting behind the screen.

That contradiction described him more accurately than any accusation Issen could have made. He corrected a visible inequality when its measure reached his hand. He did not question the screen that kept another inequality outside the ceremony’s terms.

Issen tasted the second bread. Ground chickpea, too much ash, a bitter edge from old fenugreek. The flavor pulled Kelar close enough to disturb their breathing, although they knew no name for the distant child or kitchen. They kept their face still.

Grand Provisioner Rovan Dast sat at the first table without eating. His famine bowl held the same nine slices as everyone else’s. No lamb fat touched his place. Whatever exemptions he permitted the court, he did not take that one.

The herald recited figures from the Nine-Day Famine: granaries closed, barges delayed, riots, households abandoned, bodies collected from market steps. Rovan had been a child then. The official account never named what he had eaten or whom he had watched starve.

At slice four, a canal magistrate drank wine beneath the table. Issen smelled anise on his breath when he leaned forward for certification. Memorial rules forbade any other food until all nine measures passed.

“Your cup,” they said.

He covered it with one sleeve. “Medicinal.”

“Then the court physician will have entered it.”

The physician had not. A page removed the wine. The magistrate’s expression promised a later complaint, but he ate the fifth slice dry like the petitioners below.

Small equalities made convincing theater because bodies felt them.

The sixth bread came from red millet. The seventh used canal rice pressed into a hard square. The eighth contained only barley and salt. Each course claimed a province through grain while withholding the province’s ordinary preparation. Tal Jes millet arrived without pepper oil or goat curd. Eren Ford rice lacked lentils and pickled mustard. Ulsor barley carried standardized mine salt, not guild brine. The memorial joined ingredients by stripping away difference.

For the ninth measure, servants brought a shared basin of warm water. Every official dipped one piece of famine bread before eating. Rovan waited until the petitioners received their basin below. Only then did he swallow.

“Let no private store stand between one hunger and another,” the herald declared.

Applause moved across the hall.

Issen certified the water and watched kitchen pages remove the screens. Roast lamb, honeyed roots, spiced fish, and white loaves entered for officials. Petitioners received one extra memorial slice at the doors.

Rovan joined Issen at the tasting table. “You object to the second service.”

“I have not tasted it.”

“You object before taste.”

“The lamb did not wait through nine breads.”

“Staff must prepare the next course while guests complete the first.”

“Preparation has a smell.”

“So does resentment.”

His tone remained mild. Issen met his gaze. “Resentment did not roast the lamb.”

A line appeared beside his mouth, almost amusement. “Come below after certification. The archive kitchen has the old famine menus. You asked to understand policy.”

“I asked to see the seat list.”

“Understanding may improve your questions.”

The private courses required sixteen tastings. Issen found no poison. They rejected one honey glaze because fermented foam concealed a poppy tincture above court allowance. Rovan supported the refusal and ordered the full tray withheld, including his own portion. Sincere standards coexisted comfortably with concealed seats.

By midafternoon, Issen descended to the archive kitchen carrying their chain, tasting spoon, and an authorized slate. This room lay above the hidden test hearth but below the public ovens. No live fire burned. Heat came through pipes from a laundry boiler, keeping wax tablets soft enough to read without cracking.

Menus occupied cedar shelves organized by reign, campaign, civic festival, and emergency ration. Each consisted of two records. The public sheet named dishes and ingredients. The kitchen sheet named source stores, oven signatures, oath classes, expected bodily effects, and tasting sequence. Issen’s office usually received only the first.

Archive rules treated food history as chain of custody. A kitchen sheet recorded who opened each grain jar, how many hours an ember had remained banked, which spoon touched the test cup, and where leftovers went after service. Discarded broth could retain a bond until it cooled or digestion ended in every diner, so waste carts carried extinction marks. Even grease scraped from a pan had a destination entry. The Hall knew how to follow matter when matter threatened its own kitchens.

Population reach received less precision. Oath classes appeared as hooks and initials rather than names. A source clerk might record twelve pounds of barley to the half-ounce while representing two thousand attached households with a single provincial cut. Accuracy contracted around what the Hall owned and expanded around bodies it governed.

Issen selected a menu from a flood-relief service and followed its two records. The public sheet promised warming rice for canal evacuees. The inner sheet expected excess heat to move toward dredging crews working in cold water. That transfer might have helped both groups if every diner knew. Nothing in the public text mentioned the crews, duration, or weighting. A useful outcome did not repair the omission; it merely made concealment easier to defend.

“Were the dredgers told?” Issen asked.

Rovan checked the service year. “The canal magistrate requested the arrangement.”

“That answers who ordered it.”

“Workers knew relief kitchens supported the flood effort.”

“That answers what they might have inferred.”

He closed the flood menu. “You want every administrative fact spoken before every meal.”

“Only the facts entering a body.”

Rovan waited beside a table holding the original famine ledger. He wore no ceremonial black bronze now. His gray robe smelled faintly of dry bread.

“The first day,” he said, opening a wax leaf. “Look at distribution.”

The menu listed millet cakes from South Canal Store Three. In the kitchen margin, two letters appeared beside a three-pronged hearth mark: SC. A second source carried NF for North Foundry. Day two added RW, River Weighhouse. By day nine, thirty-one initials crowded the margin.

“Storehouses,” Issen said.

“Originally.”

“Originally?”

“As ration oaths expanded, the notation moved from buildings to the populations attached to them. A hearth represents continuity better than a warehouse. Warehouses burn.”

“So do kitchens.”

“People rebuild them.”

He handed Issen a menu from the previous year’s canal festival. Provincial ingredients filled the public page. Its kitchen margin carried the same initials as several blackened ember cups beneath the Hall, plus marks Issen had not seen in the test room.

They touched none of the wax. “These menus contain seat reach.”

“They contain provenance.”

“Provenance ends where the ingredient enters this kitchen. These marks continue into households.”

Rovan took time before answering. “A modern ration oath does both.”

The distinction he offered functioned like a door painted on stone.

Issen compared the famine commemoration menu from that morning. Nine provincial grains, nine public sources. In the inner margin, forty-two hearth initials appeared under lines for expected hunger, digestive delay, and shared satiety. The banquet had not carried an active mass bond; no live provincial embers touched its bread. Yet the menu had been written to accept those signatures later without changing service order.

“A template,” they said.

“A measure that can grow.”

“Toward one hundred.”

Rovan did not deny it. “The earlier test established reach. Equinox will establish a stable civic field.”

“How many mouths?”

“Enough that provincial isolation stops deciding who bears scarcity.”

Issen opened the authorized slate. “Name them.”

“Names change daily.”

“Name the hearths.”

“You are looking at some.”

“Why only some?”

“Because the instrument remains vulnerable to sabotage.”

“By the people entered into it?”

“By officials and insurgents who profit from keeping burdens local.”

Rovan walked to a shelf marked REBELLION YEARS. He removed a campaign menu bound in leather and set it beside the famine ledger.

“During the Vesk uprising, river towns withheld grain while upland districts starved,” he said. “The river councils could afford rebellion because other provinces carried the army’s requisitions. Their local prosperity financed refusal.”

“Imperial assessments financed the army they refused.”

“And when the army withdrew, Vesk merchants charged famine rates to the uplands. Local independence did not produce local mercy.”

That much entered the historic record even outside Namarra. Issen had tasted Vesk memorial wine, deliberately sour to recall grain sold late.

Rovan opened the campaign menu. “Suppose the river councils had felt the upland deficit in their own stomachs. Suppose the uplands had felt the broken ribs of dockworkers beaten during seizure. Neither side could have called the other’s condition distant.”

“Feeling does not grant control over the barges.”

“It alters the price of refusing them.”

“Who sets the weights?”

“A central provision office with representation from every region.”

“Under whose army?”

“Under a law built to outlast any army.”

Issen saw the shape of his sincerity. Rovan had not invented hunger as an excuse for command. Hunger had taught him to fear any storehouse closed by a private hand. He wanted every closed door to hurt the person holding its key. He did not apply the same suspicion to the office governing all doors.

“What happens to rebellion under your stable field?” they asked.

“It loses the fiction of clean victory. Burn a granary, and your own children feel the hunger. Wound a soldier, and the cost reaches your household. Let soldiers wound a village, and the capital receives that pain.”

“Equally?”

“According to population and capacity.”

“Those are weights.”

“Justice requires weights. An infant cannot carry an adult share.”

At Kelar, a small body had carried more.

Issen kept that knowledge behind their teeth. “You call it peace through equal hunger.”

“Equal hunger, equal consequence. Not identical portions.”

“Then the phrase conceals its own correction.”

Rovan’s hand rested on the famine ledger. “I remember adults telling me the closed granary held enough. They treated my hunger as information about someone else. If its keeper had felt what I felt, the gate would have opened.”

“Or he would have endured your pain and kept the key.”

“No one endures a continent.”

“You intend to prove that with bodies who did not choose the test.”

“Consent cannot be gathered from every mouth before a canal is dug, a tax assessed, or an army raised. Government acts among people who disagree.”

“A canal can be walked away from.”

“Not by the town it floods.”

He answered too well to become a simple monster. That made the room more dangerous.

Issen touched the seventh link of their chain. Black salt rested inside, evidence taken without lawful warrant. “Where does the First Taster sit?”

“At the capital measure.”

“With what weight?”

“Representative.”

“Can I sever my seat?”

“The supervising tablewright can sever any seat that fails.”

“That gives me no severance.”

“It gives you protection.”

“From the person controlling the fire.”

Rovan closed the campaign menu. “You mistake independent power for safety. Offices constrain one another.”

“My office does not receive the inner menu.”

“You receive it now.”

“Because you want my mouth at Equinox.”

“Yes.”

The admission came without shame. He believed use and trust could occupy the same word.

A kitchen archivist entered to request the famine menus for resealing. Issen watched her inventory every sheet by title and shelf number. The inner menus carried no individual classification beyond their category. A tasting order could access them legally when comparing ingredient provenance. Copying the hearth initials would violate archive rules, not because they named people but because they exposed infrastructure.

Issen returned the authorized slate. “I need to compare today’s bitter fenugreek against previous western-grain services. The memorial bread carried storage contamination.”

Rovan had eaten the same bread. “How severe?”

“Below refusal. Strong enough to confuse a future hearth test.”

“Take an archivist and examine five years.”
