He granted access because the tasting reason remained sound. Issen had not invented the bitterness.

Rovan Dast left for the afternoon grain council. The archivist, Pel Arit, brought menus from five famine commemorations and two western provincial festivals. She sat across from Issen while they compared ingredient marks.

Issen worked slowly enough to remain exact. Fenugreek source. Storage year. Oil carrier. Oven. Beneath those legitimate observations, they tracked hearth initials. SC repeated. NF. RW. Three western marks appeared only after the ration-oath expansion. Two matched regional cuts on the black-salt test apparatus. Four more occurred beside souring instructions designed for rapid reach.

They could not write the initials openly. Pel checked every mark made on Hall slate.

Instead Issen used their tasting chain.

The seven links bore tiny calibration scratches along the inner edge, used to measure spoon depth. During a wash break, they added one pinprick beside the first link for SC, two beside the second for NF, then combinations corresponding to the menu order. The marks alone meant nothing without a key.

For the key, they wrote a list of spice corrections on a permitted paper strip: sesame before fenugreek, mint after plum, rue omitted, cumin doubled. First letters and positions encoded the hearth sequence. A court cook would read only adjustments.

Pel returned with the final menu. “You’re looking past ingredients.”

Issen kept their voice courteous. “The office trained me to.”

“Some things in these sheets do not belong to your certification.”

“Then they should not alter what I taste.”

Pel glanced toward the archive door. “Grand Provisioner says the new field will prevent another famine.”

“Do you believe him?”

“I believe he remembers being hungry.”

“That was not my question.”

The archivist resealed the menus without answering. Before she carried them away, she turned the top kitchen sheet so Issen could see its lower corner. Three additional initials lay beneath the wax flap, places omitted from the public provenance line.

Issen memorized them.

That night, they resumed court duty at a reception for canal magistrates. They tasted wine, fish paste, and sugared nuts while officials praised the morning’s shared austerity. No one asked whether nine slices had satisfied the petitioners.

In the recovery room afterward, Issen opened the hollow clasp. The black hearth-salt grain remained intact. They placed the coded spice strip beside it and copied the pinprick key onto a scrap hidden inside the chain’s storage case.

Seventeen initials. Seventeen distant hearths, each attached to mouths Rovan called representation. None of those mouths could inspect Issen’s list, yet the first honest record of them now existed outside the Hall’s inner menu.

Issen folded the private list beneath the case lining and fastened the silver chain for tomorrow’s service.

Chapter 10 — The Meal No One Bound

Savra had twelve baskets open on the brine-yard stones and no two of them agreed about supper.

One held pale barley polished until each grain looked like a clipped fingernail. Another leaked pepper oil through its reedwork. There were river onions cured in salt, a clay jar of sour plum, two gutted fish stiff beneath their crust, mustard stems from the canal country, meal made from red millet, and a sack of chickpeas that had crossed Ember Bridge in Teren’s third wagon. Around the baskets stood twelve cooks who had agreed on only one fact: none intended to let Savra Kel put their food over her fire.

“Good,” Savra said. “Then we have begun correctly.”

Guildspeaker Mava Ul watched from beneath the shade cloth. Her mourning cords, gray for the relatives lost in the Nine-Salt campaign, lay against a robe white with blown salt. “We asked for proof that you can cook without control. Not proof that you enjoy being contradicted.”

“I don’t enjoy it.” Savra pressed a thumb into the fish’s belly. The flesh returned slowly. Fresh enough, if cooked before sunset. “I trust it.”

The cook from the western pans, a narrow man named Hesh, pulled his basket farther from the chickpeas. “My fire takes no imperial grain.”

“Those chickpeas came from Kelar refugees,” Dima said. She held her right hand close to her apron, the fingers bent with the permanent stiffness Ember Bridge had left in them. Her left hand rested on the handle of an adapted cleaver. “If you mean to insult someone, count accurately.”

Hesh’s ears darkened. He did not return the basket.

Mava had arranged the test in the salt guild’s dyeing yard, where twelve brick fire beds ran beside a channel carrying hot brine toward the settling pans. No hearthseal had been admitted through the gate. Two guild clerks had searched Savra’s clothes, her knife roll, and the seams of her shoes. They had searched Dima as well. Teren had been ordered to remain outside with the Free Granary guards. The guilds wanted no soldier, no captain, and no hidden ember close enough to turn a meal into an argument made inside another person’s body.

Savra understood the precaution. Understanding did not make the search easier to endure.

“Each of you keeps your own fire,” she told the cooks. “Your own salt. Your own serving hand. We will produce one course from twelve vessels. Nothing crosses a flame. Nothing receives a common ash.”

A woman in a reed hat lifted the sour-plum jar. “Then it isn’t one course.”

“It will reach one table at one time. That is enough.”

“An emperor’s definition,” Hesh said.

Savra looked at his basket. Coarse barley, brine herbs, and an iron pan polished by generations of oil. “An emperor would tell you your pan had always belonged to him.”

A laugh escaped someone near the millet, brief and unwilling. Mava heard it. Her face did not change.

The guildspeaker pointed to the central paving, where twelve low tables stood apart in the Ulsori custom. “No common platter. Separate bowls. If your demonstration depends on breaking our hospitality to make a prettier symbol, it has already failed.”

“Keep the bowls,” Savra said. “Move the guests.”

That proposal bought her silence.

She assigned no recipe. Instead she asked each cook what their ingredient refused. River fish refused slow heat after salting. Millet refused too much water. The chickpeas refused haste. Pepper oil refused a wet spoon. Barley refused to share a pot with sour plum until its husk had opened. Their answers came as challenges, but answers still gave her timings.

Work began with the chickpeas because they needed the longest flame. Savra borrowed a broad pot from the yard, filled it at the brine channel’s freshwater inlet, and set it over a fire kindled by the Kelar cook, a round-faced girl named Nerin who had learned in the refugee kitchen. Savra did not touch Nerin’s fuel. She crouched near enough to judge the flame by the movement of steam at the pot’s lip.

“Skim now,” she said.

“I know foam.”

“Then why are you arguing with it?”

Nerin skimmed.

Across the yard Hesh toasted barley dry. The reed-hatted cook split sour plums with her thumbnail. A terrace cook heated pepper oil until one mustard seed gave a single jump, then pulled the pan aside. Dima worked through onions with her adapted knife, rocking the broad blade under her left palm while the curled fingers of her right hand steadied nothing. No one offered help. She had asked them not to unless the cut escaped her.

Savra moved among the fires without crossing the chalk arcs Mava’s clerks had drawn around each hearth. She called time by the brine drops from a cracked ceramic cup: fish after forty drops, onions after sixty, millet covered at ninety. At each fire she asked before touching a lid. Three cooks refused. Their food still had to arrive with the rest, so she altered the sequence around them rather than their work.

By the second hundred drops, rivalry had acquired practical consequences. Hesh’s barley needed the hot brine that the fish cook meant to throw away. The terrace cook wanted onion skins to temper the bitterness in her oil. Nerin needed sour plum to keep the chickpeas from collapsing into paste, but the woman in the reed hat would not surrender her jar to a common ladle.

“No exchange across fires,” Mava reminded them.

“Ingredients may cross before they touch flame,” Savra said. “Only if their owners choose.”

“Convenient.”

“Necessary things often are. That doesn’t make them innocent.”

Hesh stared into his pan. “I’ll take the fish brine if I can carry it myself.”

The fish cook shrugged. “Take what I would spill.”

No one praised them. Hesh carried a cup between the chalk arcs, poured it into his barley, and returned the cup to a basin for scalding. The terrace cook traded a spoon of pepper oil for onion skins. Nerin received three split plums on a shard of clean pottery, not the jar. Each exchange stayed visible. Each giver named the amount.

Savra felt something loosen in the yard, though no hearthbinding touched it. People adjusted because a pan demanded it, not because a seal made refusal painful.

The first vessel finished too early. The fish had drawn firm under salt and heat, its eye gone pearl-white while the barley still held a hard center. Savra asked the fish cook to lift the pan onto warm brick.

“You said forty drops.”

“I judged a larger fish.”

“So your timing failed.”

“Yes.”

Her answer moved through the cooks more sharply than an excuse would have. Imperial feastwrights did not admit mistakes before service. They buried them in garnish, changed the measure, or found a colder seat.

Dima slid the onions from her board. “What does the error cost?”

“Five minutes of resting. The skin may cling.”

“And if it had cost the fish?”

“We would serve eleven vessels and name the missing one.”

Nobody laughed this time.

When the barley opened, Hesh tasted it from his own spoon and signaled with two fingers. Nerin crushed chickpeas against the pot wall, leaving half whole. Dima divided smoked onion among twelve little dishes, her left hand quick despite the drag of her right. The pepper cook drizzled oil in measured drops. Sour plum remained on its own shard. Nothing met until the bowls reached the tables.

Mava’s invited delegates entered at sunset: guild weighers, canal keepers, refugee elders, two Free Granary factors, and a potter whose kilns supplied half the city’s seal clay. Twelve diners sat, one at each low table. Before them stood twelve bowls in twelve local forms. At Savra’s signal each diner rose and moved one place clockwise, carrying nothing. After every bite they rose again. By the end, every person had eaten from every cook, but no fire, ash, vessel, or spoon had joined another.

At the sixth place, the canal keeper asked Hesh for more barley. Hesh pointed him toward the next table because giving an extra portion would disturb the count. At the eighth, the potter passed her untouched pepper oil to a refugee elder who wanted it. At the tenth, two delegates began comparing the fish brine to the mustard stems and called the fish cook over to settle whether the cure could preserve canal rice.

Conversation did what Savra could not have ordered. It crossed the spaces the bowls preserved.

She remained standing until Mava tapped an empty cushion beside the chickpea table.

“You cooked,” the guildspeaker said.

“Nerin cooked.”

“You corrected the fire.”

“I asked her to.”

Mava tapped the cushion again. “Sit where we can see what reaches you.”

Savra sat. Nerin gave her the final portion, scraped from the pot’s bottom where the chickpeas had browned. It carried smoke, sour fruit, and the sandy roughness of a pulse cooked almost, but not entirely, soft. No foreign ache followed the swallow. Her scar did not warm. No soldier’s fatigue pressed behind her eyes. The food contained only what field, hands, fuel, and error had put into it.

“The meal no one bound,” the potter said.

Mava glanced at her. “You make it sound ceremonial.”

“I make seal jars. Everything sounds ceremonial after enough guild speeches.”

A few delegates smiled. Savra did not. She had reached the part of the test no timing could improve.

Hesh set down his spoon. “Tell us what you did for the empire.”

Savra folded her hands above her bowl. She spoke of training in the Hall of Measures, of field tables built to spread marching fatigue among named companies, and of notation that let a feastwright alter weights after the first bite. She said she had worked under Rovan Dast. She said villages had suffered through an imperial campaign table she had helped make. She did not offer the design’s formal name, and she did not pretend omission had kept her clean.

“How many villages?” the reed-hatted cook asked.

“I don’t know.”

“How many people?”

“I never learned the number.”

Her next question cut deeper. “Did you stay to count?”

“No.”

Brine water clicked through the yard channel. Dima watched Savra without rescue in her face.

Mava wiped plum from her thumb. “You want these cooks to learn from you.”

“I want them to survive what Rovan is building.”

“That was not my question.”

Savra looked around the separate tables. “Yes. I want them to learn from me. Skill survives guilt. That makes skill dangerous, not useless.”

“And trust?”

“Trust should not survive on command.”

Mava let the answer stand. She turned to the twelve cooks. “You have seen her work without a seal. That proves she can coordinate supper. It proves nothing about what she will do with an ember.”

Dima reached into her apron and placed two small salt cups on the paving. The guild clerks stiffened, but both cups had come from Mava’s own stores. Dima added two charcoal embers in separate clay saucers, one from her camp fire and one from Nerin’s refugee hearth.

“We can prove one more thing,” Dima said. “Small enough to stop.”

Savra studied the cups. “Who takes the paired share?”

“I do,” Nerin said at once.

Dima shook her head. “Your fire is already named. We need someone who understands the right to end it.” She looked at Hesh.

His mouth tightened. “You want Ulsor in a former imperial cook’s table.”

“I want you in your own.”

The proposed bond used no blended embers. Two saucers heated two barley cakes made from the same dough, each cake marked with one name. A line of clean Ulsor salt separated the saucers. Savra would bridge them only with a spoon of onion oil, equal measure on both cakes. Dima named the burden: the warmth and sting of a hand held over steam for ten breaths. Equal weighting. Duration no longer than twenty minutes. Either diner could sever by overturning their salt cup and scattering its line.

“And the cook?” Mava asked.

Savra took a third pinch of dough.

Dima’s brows lifted. “A pair has two.”

“Then I do not bind it. Under our rule, the cook takes a seat.”

Hesh studied her for a long moment. “Three equal shares. No hidden fourth.”

“No hidden fourth.”

They spoke all three names aloud. Savra touched no one’s cup. Hesh marked the cakes with the blunt end of his own spoon. Nerin placed her ember. Dima counted ten breaths while each diner held a hand above steam, and pain gathered red across Savra’s palm. When they ate, the heat divided. Her own sting diminished, then steadied as Hesh’s and Nerin’s joined it. Nothing vanished. Three palms carried the same modest burn.

At the seventh minute Hesh overturned his salt cup.

The line broke. Recoil returned his fraction across Savra and Nerin, a quick increase sharp enough to make Nerin gasp. Savra held her hand open so everyone could see the color deepen. Dima extinguished both embers, and digestion carried the remaining bond down toward silence.

Hesh flexed his fingers. “It obeyed me.”

“No,” Savra said. “We obeyed the limit you declared.”

Mava rose. One by one, the other cooks stood with her. They did not swear to Savra, and no one asked them to. Each named the defensive work they would accept: finding concealed salt, breaking seals, measuring recoil, teaching diners to leave a table alive.

By full dark, twelve cooks had agreed to return at dawn. They carried their separate embers to twelve separate banks of ash, ready to learn severance rather than battle feasts. Savra stayed behind to scrub the three salt cups clean.

Chapter 11 — Grain for an Unwounded Army

Teren lay under a tamarisk bush with his bad ankle in canal mud and counted the imperial barge by the way its towrope moved.

Six men hauled from the north bank. Two more walked loose beside them, spear carriers by posture even in laborers’ gray. The barge rode deep enough for twenty-eight tons if the hull followed Namarra’s grain measure, but its stern sat lower than its bow by a hand. That meant guards or seal chests aft. A boy steered with a sweep longer than he stood. Three soldiers occupied the deck, each wearing boiled-leather shoulder pieces and the bored faces of men who expected the road to remain owned.

Behind Teren, reeds hid twenty-one Free Granary fighters, four guild archers, and two cooks carrying wet hides instead of weapons. Nobody had eaten a binding meal. Their strength belonged to their own legs, their fear to their own mouths.

The canal narrowed at a maintenance gate thirty paces ahead. Ulsor masons had removed one pin before dawn. When the barge entered the lock, two women beneath the downstream walkway would drive the gate shut with a timber ram. Teren’s people would drop the towline, take the deck, and keep every flame away from the grain.

He raised one finger.

The first towman passed the painted stone. Sweat darkened his back from neck to belt. His bare heels had split against the bank, and every step pressed blood into dust. The second limped. The third had wrapped his palms in cloth gone brown at the center.

None of the soldiers showed a blister.

Teren lowered his hand before giving the signal. He watched the soldier nearest the bow. The man’s boots lacked road dust above the ankle. His face had the gray looseness of fatigue, yet his shoulders stayed level beneath his armor. When the towmen strained around a root, all three soldiers drew breath together, not from effort but from something shared and distant.

“Captain?” whispered Saret beside him.

“Wait for the stern.”

Four heartbeats later Teren saw the black ceramic cap fixed beneath the tiller bench. A seal. Small enough for a company table, large enough to keep a ration bond alive between meal bells.

He showed two fingers, then closed his fist.

The maintenance gate slammed across the canal. Water struck wood with a flat crack. Archers rose from reeds and sent blunt shafts at the tow guards before either man brought a spear around. Saret cut the rope. Six haulers fell backward as tension vanished, while Teren and the boarders ran down the bank with hooked poles.

His left ankle caught in a washout. Pain climbed the old crush line and forced one short step, but he kept moving. A pole hooked the barge rail. Teren used both arms, swung across the water, and landed among spilled millet grains.

The nearest imperial soldier thrust low. Teren knocked the spear aside with the hook, drove its butt into the man’s ribs, and saw the towman onshore fold around the same place before the soldier did. The bond carried impact away quickly enough to make the soldier’s eyes widen without stopping his counterstroke.

“Don’t strike them!” Teren shouted. “Pin the weapons.”

His people adjusted because he had warned them before the ambush that any unexplained resilience meant hidden seats. Two hooked the spear shaft. Saret threw a net over the second soldier. The third drew a short sword and backed toward the stern seal, perhaps to break it, perhaps to increase its weight. Teren could not let either happen while the distant seats remained unknown.

He crossed behind a stack of sacks and shoved a wet hide over the ceramic cap. One of the cooks, Vei, followed. She packed mud around the seal’s air holes, starving the ember without cracking the clay.

Transfer moved badly when a source fire died. The soldier with the sword staggered as his own fatigue returned. A blister rose beneath his thumb in the time it took him to grip the rail. The netted man vomited canal water though none had touched his mouth. Onshore, the towmen stopped gasping.

“Yield,” Teren said. “The bond is dying, not broken. If you smash that cap, whatever remains may recoil.”

The swordsman looked toward the haulers. His blade lowered by a finger.

Saret tightened the net around his companion. “He means it.”

“Free Granary lies with an honest face,” the soldier said.

“Then test my arithmetic. Three of you, six haulers, one sealed ember. You have been walking without sores while they bleed. If the seat list contains only nine names, your captain might survive breaking it. If it contains ninety, you may kill someone you have never seen.”

That calculation reached him. He put the sword flat on deck.

The skirmish ended in less time than the lock took to fill. One archer had a cut across her left forearm. Two towmen carried bruises from falling. Teren’s ankle throbbed. Nobody had died, and the barge still held its cargo.

By noon they had moored beneath a salt warehouse whose guild mark kept imperial patrols away. Teren ordered the prisoners given water before questioning, then began his inventory at the bow.

The first sacks held pale barley, forty imperial measures to a stack. Next came hard red wheat, river rice, chickpeas, and millet. Each sack bore two stamps: the Hall of Measures weight and a ration-oath origin. The origin marks should have named crown fields or taxed estates. Instead Teren found village oven signs pressed beneath wax patches.

Vei heated a thin knife and lifted the patch from a wheat sack. Beneath it lay a three-reed mark.

“Dovet Bank,” Teren said.

Saret leaned close. “That’s south of the reed marshes. No crown land there.”

“Forty families. Two ovens.” Teren had bought rice from them three years earlier. “Write it.”

The next patch concealed a hooked fish from Heron Cut. A barley sack carried the split wheel of Ashel Ferry. Thirty rice sacks bore the low-arch sign of Mere Varo. At the stern, under the deepest stack, they found red millet marked with the hand-and-spade of Orra Field.

Five civilian villages supplied the oath marks. Every one lay along a military road, and none had sold to the Free Granary since spring.

Saret ran a hand over the stacked food. “Requisition.”

“More than that.” Teren cut open one sack and found individually baked ration loaves packed upright between layers of husk. They looked like standard white army bread, dense enough to keep through wet weather. A black line crossed each crust. Not mold. Hearth-salt baked into the scoring.

He selected three loaves from separate layers and set them on an overturned crate. Vei brought the mud-smothered barge seal. Its ember had cooled to a dull red, but faint heat still moved under the cap.

One imperial soldier, the man who had surrendered his sword, watched from beneath guard. He gave his name as Captain Orel Tas, though his armor carried no field bars. His men called him captain without thinking.

“Those are breakfast rations,” Orel said. “You’ll learn nothing by wasting them.”

Teren turned one loaf. The base carried five tiny cuts radiating from a central hole. “Who eats from the other seats?”

“Army provisioning does not publish routes to raiders.”

“Routes are already on the sacks.”

Orel’s gaze moved to the exposed village marks. Surprise tightened one side of his mouth before discipline removed it.

That mattered. A man could participate in a system without knowing its full table. Ignorance did not restore the haulers’ skin, but it changed how Teren should question him.

“Did you know civilian grain oaths remained active after requisition?” Teren asked.

“My company eats assigned bread.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It is what I know.”

Vei broke a loaf. Steam no longer rose, yet the crumb smelled faintly of the last oven that had baked it: rice chaff, old mustard smoke, a little canal damp. She pinched off a piece but did not swallow.

“We test with hands,” she said. “No eating while the full list remains concealed.”

A shallow bowl received warm water and a measure of sour plum to accelerate transfer. Vei floated crumbs from the three loaves, set the barge seal beside the bowl, and named only the participants present: herself, Teren, and Orel. Without access to the concealed list, she could not exclude distant seats. She therefore used no fresh ember and no binding salt. The goal involved reading the old bond’s residue, not waking it.

“Touch the water,” she told Orel.

His manacled hand entered first. Teren followed, then Vei. For several breaths nothing happened. After the sixth, Teren felt pressure under his heel. After the eighth, a raw line opened across his right palm, mirroring no injury he carried. Orel’s breathing eased as the sensation arrived.

Vei withdrew. “Enough.”

Teren kept his fingers in the bowl for two more breaths to judge direction. The pain ran toward them from the seal, not from Orel directly. Behind it came the heavy drag of sleepless labor, a child’s cramped calf, and heat trapped beneath someone’s shoulder blade. No memory traveled with those states. No face or name. Only bodies paying fractions.

He pulled free. The palm looked unbroken, but the hurt remained until Vei buried the seal in wet salt and quenched its last coal.

Across the room Orel flexed his hand. The blister at his thumb had thickened since the bond weakened. Teren looked at the prisoners’ feet. Their socks showed no blood. The towmen waiting in the courtyard had needed bandages before they could walk.

“Soldiers without blisters,” Saret said quietly. “Marchers without swollen knees. While five villages limp for them.”

Orel’s face hardened. “My soldiers marched fourteen days. We bled at Ossen Ford.”

“I believe you.” Teren dried his hand. “The table did not erase your wounds. It kept sending portions elsewhere.”

“No civilian sits in a company ration circle.”

“Then help us open the manifest.”

The captain looked again at the village marks. “If I assist you, my crew hangs.”

“If you don’t, these loaves go back into service and people who never enlisted keep marching under your feet.”

Orel offered no answer. Teren did not press him toward a conversion that one inventory could not earn.

The manifest chest required two keys. One hung beneath the tiller bench. The barge master, captured downstream after trying to swim the lock, had swallowed the other. Dima would have proposed patience and oil. Saret proposed an axe. Teren chose the hinges.

They removed each bronze pin without striking the chest body, using sail grease and a wagon punch. Inside lay wax tablets listing allotments for six hundred troops over twenty days. The ration numbers matched. The seat annex did not name people, only the same five oven marks and a weighting column.

Dovet Bank carried twelve shares for every hundred soldiers. Heron Cut carried nine. Ashel Ferry bore seventeen. Mere Varo, the largest village, bore thirty-eight. Orra Field carried twenty-four. The numbers totaled one hundred civilian shares set against each company hundred. Imperial troops felt half of what happened to them. Villages received the rest according to grain contribution.

Saret looked pleased in the grim way of a man whose enemy had finally written down its crime. “We take the tablets to Mava. The grain feeds us through Halvek.”

Teren had already begun a different count. Twenty-eight tons on the barge. Three tons could support the twelve cooks and coalition guards through the next week. Twenty-five tons divided by the requisition weights could return something close to what each village had supplied. The tow route to all five would cost six days. Imperial pursuit would cost more.

“We return it,” he said.

Silence met the order.

Saret planted both hands on the crate. “We have nine days of grain if every town gives what it promised. Seven if the bridge refugees join us. You want to surrender twenty-five tons to villages that may be occupied before we arrive.”

“They already paid for this grain.”

“We are fighting the people who stole it.”

“With their food.”

Vei sat beside the quenched seal, rubbing stiffness from her wrist. “The loaves may keep the ration bond alive if villagers eat from the same oath grain.”

“Then every loaf gets destroyed or reworked unbound,” Teren said. “Whole grain goes back under local custody. The five villages decide how to bake it.”

Saret lowered his voice. “Captain, mercy is expensive.”

“So is recruitment.”

There it stood: the part of his thinking he trusted too readily. He could convert a moral debt into routes, days, and allies, then call the answer clean because the columns balanced. Returning the grain remained right even if no village joined them. He needed to say that before his arithmetic made virtue look profitable.

“We return it if they close their gates to us,” Teren said. “We return it if they curse us. Mark three tons for our crews because we captured the barge and must deliver the rest. Record every measure. If Mava calls that theft, I’ll answer her.”

The order changed the warehouse. Fighters stopped seeing provisions and started seeing five deliveries. Saret cursed once, then divided the wax tablets. Guild clerks brought clean seals without hearth-salt. The black-scored ration loaves went into soak pits, where water would ruin them before anyone could swallow an active mark. Nothing useful vanished: after the seal residue died, the mash would feed pigs.

Division took longer than seizure. Imperial sacks followed a forty-measure standard, but three had split during the boarding and seven carried damp along their lower seams. Teren would not let a village receive the rotten share merely because its allotment fell last. He ordered every damaged sack opened, the dry grain winnowed onto sailcloth, and the damp grain spread in the warehouse loft where brine heat could reach it without touching salt. Each of the five piles received the same proportion of loss. Saret kept the calculation on a wax tablet while two village factors watched his stylus.

At Dovet Bank’s pile, a guild weigher found the imperial bronze measure shaved beneath its rim. What the manifest called forty measures amounted to thirty-eight and a little husk. Teren had seen dishonest merchant cups before, but this one carried the Hall stamp. He made them test all four official measures from the barge. Three cheated by the same amount.

“Count by weight,” he said. “Use Ulsor stones.”

“That delays us until midnight,” Saret replied.

“It delayed those villages all winter. We can afford one night.”

Teams lifted each sack onto a beam scale. Teren recorded tare weight for cloth, cord, wax, and the grain dust trapped in the seams. His ankle swelled while he stood at the table, so he moved to a low stool and kept counting. Nobody mistook sitting for rest. By the time darkness covered the canal, the promised twenty-five tons had diminished by spoilage and false measure to twenty-three tons, six hundredweight. He divided the shortfall across all five allotments, then assigned coalition grain from their three-ton share to make each village whole under the honest measure.

That choice left his own force with less than two tons.

Vei read the final columns. “You said three.”

“I said they would receive what the tablets claimed the barge carried.”

“You are correcting an imperial fraud with coalition food.”

“Yes.”

“Write that beside your name. Don’t let the numbers make it look accidental.”

Teren pressed his sign into the wax. The note named his decision and the precise grain removed from coalition stores. A future council could accuse him from an honest record. That felt better than leaving a clean column over a concealed theft.

Before sealing the village stacks, they added copies of the seat annex between paired clay tiles. Each copy named the weighting but excluded instructions for waking the bond. The villages needed proof, not a reusable weapon. Vei marked the tiles with vinegar so any later alteration would cloud the writing. One set remained with Mava, another with Teren, and five traveled with the restitution loads.

The captives received ordinary barley from an Ulsor oven whose oath had never entered the imperial seal. Orel smelled the bowl before he ate. Teren understood the hesitation: after learning what bread could conceal, even hunger needed evidence. Vei broke her own portion from the same loaf and swallowed first, without ember or declared seat.

Only then did Orel eat.

Orel watched the work. “You’ll lose Halvek before you reach it.”

“Maybe.”

“The Grand Provisioner will burn what you return.”

“Maybe.”

“You command by maybes?”

Teren tied Dovet Bank’s tablet to its first stack. “I command what we do, not what it guarantees.”

Before dusk, news of the capture had crossed the canal faster than the redistributed cargo. Two river-town boats arrived under unpainted sails. One came from Lorn Steps, the other from Vale Senn. Their elders had seen village children wake with marching cramps after army meal bells. Neither town had promised the coalition more than shelter.

Now Lorn Steps offered sixteen rowers and two shallow craft. Vale Senn offered its north granary key, provided no army table ever touched the stores.

Teren accepted both under written conditions. He did not call it victory.

The barge left Ulsor divided into five protected allotments, each under a local oven mark uncovered from imperial wax. Their coalition column followed with less than two tons instead of twenty-eight. Six days had disappeared from Teren’s campaign board. In exchange, two river towns rowed beside them, and Captain Orel Tas sat under guard watching soldiers’ bread travel back toward the bodies that had carried its cost.

Chapter 12 — Twelve Recipes for Refusal

Dima dropped the first ash-bread into the fire and made twelve cooks watch it burn.

The little barley cake blackened along its scored edge, curled, and opened a seam that breathed fenugreek smoke. Nobody reached for it. Each cook stood behind a separate salt line in the lower guild kitchen, where the ovens faced one another across a floor tiled with old brine marks. Savra had spent dawn inspecting those lines. Dima had spent it hiding mistakes in them.

“Why?” Hesh asked.

“Because you let me name the diners after I lit the ember,” Dima said.

“You named all twelve.”

“I named twelve people. I did not name myself.”

Nerin looked from the burning bread to Dima’s crooked right hand. “You weren’t going to eat.”

“I was going to teach.”

“That’s outside the bond.”

Dima smiled without warmth. “Then stop me.”

Hesh kicked salt across the nearest fire bed. The origin ember hissed under the spill. Nerin used a wet cloth to lift the cake without breaking it, preserving the seal long enough for Savra to inspect. A third cook opened the flue so smoke drew away from the group.

“Correct result,” Savra said. “Poor order. If the cake had reached a mouth, which action would have severed first?”

“The salt,” Hesh answered.

“The ember,” said Nerin.

“Neither,” Dima said. “You prepare an open share before disturbing a live table. Otherwise recoil chooses for you.”

Savra lifted the cake with iron tongs. Dima had scored thirteen tiny seats into the crust, twelve in a ring and one beneath the fold where the dough had curled. The hidden mark belonged to no name. In an imperial field kitchen, a clerk could fill it later from an oath list.

Training had begun the morning after the unbound meal. Mava permitted two days, four guild kitchens, and enough black salt for six demonstrations. She issued none of it to Savra directly. Two clerks measured each pinch into Dima’s left palm, recorded the use, and collected the ash. Twelve cooks attended: Hesh of the western pans; Nerin from Kelar; three Ulsori fish and barley cooks; a canal-rice baker; two Free Granary camp cooks; a terrace-oil keeper from Tal Jes; a potter who fired seal clay; a refugee elder who knew chickpea ovens; and Dima herself.

Savra occupied the thirteenth work place. Under the rule they were building, the cook takes a seat. Instruction did not excuse her.

“Start again,” she said.

This time Hesh read the list before fuel touched tinder. Thirteen named seats. Equal weight. Heat burden from one hand held above steam for five breaths. Duration ten minutes after the first swallowed bite. Severance by scattering an individual salt line, followed by transfer into a declared open cup. Source ember from Hesh’s household pan, ash witnessed by Mava’s clerk. Food: barley ash-bread, one cake divided into thirteen visible pieces.

Dima interrupted him twice. “Whose hand?”

“Mine.”

“Which hand?”

“Left.”

“Say it.”

He did. A diner could not weigh a cost if the cook hid its shape behind a general word.

Savra watched the dough rather than Hesh’s face. He had mixed it too dry from fear that softer bread would blur the score marks. She added no water. Correction belonged to the person carrying the fire. When the cake came off the tile, its edge cracked and two portions threatened to split.

Hesh swore.

“Name the change,” Savra told him.

“Two pieces smaller. I can remake it.”

“Or alter the weight before anyone eats.”

He recalculated aloud. The two smaller portions would receive no special burden because food quantity did not determine magical weighting once the seat marks and salt line had been fixed. Yet smaller bites digested sooner, shortening those diners’ exposure. Hesh extended the dough with two unbound crumbs baked on cold tile, equalizing digestion without changing the table.

Thirteen people ate. Warmth divided across their left palms, mild and undeniable. At the fourth minute the canal-rice baker scattered her salt.

Nerin slid an empty cup into the gap before the last crystals separated. Recoil entered the open seat as heat without a body. The cup’s water steamed. No diner felt the departing share.

“That cup cannot suffer,” Mava said from the doorway.

“No,” Savra replied. “It can hold heat briefly. Not wounds. Not hunger. Not exhaustion beyond what water or clay can embody.”

“So it does not solve your war.”

“It solves one hand leaving one small lesson.”

Mava nodded once. She had no patience for promises enlarged beyond their vessel.

The second exercise used salt-baked river fish. It failed more dangerously.

Reed-hatted Pava built the table from six diners instead of thirteen. Her fish carried an Ulsor ember; sour-plum broth accelerated transfer; a skin of sesame oil should have delayed onset until every diner finished the first mouthful. She named stomach warmth as the shared state, equal weight, fifteen-minute duration. Dima sat at her right. Savra watched from the opposite seat after adding her own name to the list.

Pava poured the oil too early. It clung to the hot bowl rather than spreading over the cooling broth. The first serving carried almost none. The last held a shining layer.

Savra saw it but did not stop the service. The declared burden remained small, and Pava needed to feel what uneven onset did before she trusted a written rule.

Hesh swallowed first. Nothing moved. Nerin swallowed second. Still nothing. By the time the sixth diner lifted her spoon, Pava’s face flushed and Hesh’s lips had gone pale.

“Unequal,” Nerin said.

“Source?” Dima asked.

“Pava’s stomach warmth.”

“Wrong. Look at Hesh.”

The oil delay had reversed across the bowl. Hesh’s nearly oil-free portion woke the souring immediately, drawing warmth out of him before Pava’s heavy-oil serving joined the table. Conservation held, but timing turned equal weights into a temporary theft.

Pava reached for the seal.

Dima caught her wrist with her left hand. Her right could not close firmly enough. “Prepare the landing before you cut the rope.”

Three cooks moved. Nerin placed warm stones inside two open clay cups. Hesh named his seat for severance. Savra stirred the remaining oily broth to distribute its delay and swallowed one measured spoon, not to add a new rule but to bring the late portion awake. Only then did Dima score through Hesh’s salt line with the blunt back of her adapted knife.

Heat jumped into the stones. Pava’s flush eased. Hesh bent over the table, breathing until his own warmth returned to his hands.

“What failed?” Savra asked.

“My pour,” Pava said.

“More exactly.”

“I treated equal weight as equal timing.”

“Write that into your recipe.”

Pava wiped her mouth. “We don’t write recipes for mistakes.”

“Then you write instructions that create them.”

By midday the kitchen wall held twelve clay tablets, each beginning with a refusal. Pava’s read: No fish table begins until the oil has covered every serving. Hesh’s read: No ash-bread enters flame before the cook’s name enters the list. Nerin’s required each hidden fold to be broken open in front of diners. The potter’s required seals to carry a maker’s countermark visible under vinegar steam. Dima’s required a prepared landing vessel before any severance.

Savra added no tablet of her own. Her rules had governed too many people from walls they never saw.

After the failed fish table, they moved to the largest oven room for nested tables. Three local circles took shape on the floor: four Ulsori cooks behind white salt, four river cooks behind gray canal salt, and four camp cooks behind coarse red salt from Tal Jes. Each circle held a separate ember and separate food. Barley porridge carried warmth in Ulsor. Rice cakes carried fatigue in the river circle. Pepper-oil chickpeas carried the ache of holding a squat in the camp circle.

A broad outer line enclosed all three. It did not merge them. Its purpose allowed a declared share to move between circles only when both sending and receiving cooks opened matching gates in their salt.

“Nested tables,” Nerin said. “Small fires inside one boundary.”

“Not one authority,” Savra corrected. “Three authorities agreeing on one passage.”

Dima took the Ulsor cook’s seat. Savra sat inside the river circle. Hesh joined the camp cooks. Each group read its list independently. Nobody in the outer ring gained power to rename an inner seat.

The first transfer moved warmth. Ulsor opened one gate; the river circle opened its matching place. Heat left Dima’s palm and entered Savra’s, distributed among four rice-cake diners. The camp circle felt nothing.

Next came fatigue. Savra had held a grain sack at shoulder height for twelve breaths before eating. The river cook opened outward, but Hesh kept the camp gate closed.

“I refuse it,” he said.

No magical pressure forced his salt apart. Fatigue remained among the four river diners, Savra included. Her shoulders trembled under a weight no longer present.

“Reason?” asked the camp cook.

“Our people need steady legs for tonight’s watch.”

“That is enough,” Savra said.

A refusal did not need moral grandeur. It needed effect.

On the third transfer both gates opened. The squat ache moved from the camp circle into Ulsor warmth, one burden crossing each direction under measured weights. Dima’s injured right hand received no special exemption; they had listed only thigh ache and palm warmth, so her old damage stayed hers. Hearthbinding could not infer kindness. A recipe either named a state or left it alone.

Mava’s clerks tested the outer line with vinegar brushes. No concealed crossing appeared. Then Dima struck the side of a salt jar with her knife.

The jar broke. Black grains spilled under the outer line from a channel hidden in its base.

Three cooks moved toward their seals. Four moved toward the fires. Nerin froze.

“Name what you know,” Savra said.

“Unknown source salt,” Nerin answered. “Crossing beneath all circles. No seat marks visible.”

“First action?”

“Stop eating. Keep embers alive. Prepare open weights.”

Her voice steadied as the sequence gave fear work to do.

This hidden-seal drill carried no live burden beyond the states already declared. Still, abrupt destruction could have thrown those states across remaining diners. The cooks set warmed water for heat, weighted hanging slings for muscular exertion, and three volunteers for fatigue. Every volunteer named a limit. Dima tested the black grains with sour plum; imperial wax rose purple around them, revealing a buried line aimed at the Ulsor circle.

Pava cut the buried channel at two places instead of breaking the central seal. Hesh closed the camp gate. Savra and the river cooks widened their declared fatigue share for three breaths while Dima lifted the compromised Ulsor ember into a tray of its own ash. The hidden route died in sections. Recoil arrived as a manageable pulse: hot palms, shaking thighs, one wave of sleepiness.

Nerin remained still until it passed. Shame tightened her face.

“You stopped,” Dima said.

“I failed.”

“You named the hazard while the rest of us reached for tools. Next time your hands will follow your mouth.”

Dima held out the adapted knife. Nerin took it and practiced the two cuts on cold salt until the motion belonged to her muscles. No table could transfer that skill. Repetition had to do the work.

During the final afternoon, each cook revised a local dish for defensive use. Hesh made barley wafers that showed hidden scoring when snapped. Pava’s fish broth separated suspect oil into visible rings. Nerin baked chickpea cakes around removable personal salt cups. The canal baker used mustard pickle to reveal imperial wax. Terrace pepper oil made unauthorized crossings sting the tongue before bodily transfer deepened. Dima designed a one-handed severance board with grooves that held a seal steady beneath a broad knife.

Testing the recipes required more than tasting. The potter, Sela Vorn, fired six false seals from the same clay and mixed iron filings into two. When heated, ordinary clay expanded evenly; filings made a seal grip its wax lining and resist a clean break. Her refusal tablet required diners to strike every seal cold against a bronze tuning plate. Pure clay answered with a high ring. Tampered clay gave a short, dead note. No magic carried the warning. Material craft did.

Canal baker Omin worked beside her with three jars of mustard stems. He warmed imperial wax under vinegar steam, then brushed each jar’s pickle liquor across the softened surface. Two seals showed their written seats in purple threads. The third stayed blank.

“Safe?” Omin asked.

Savra smelled the brush. The blank wax held too much oil. “No. The oil kept the vinegar out.”

He changed the order, dusting the wax with barley ash before brushing it. Ash drank the surface oil; purple script rose beneath. Omin added the step to his tablet and crossed out the word clear. A seal that revealed nothing had not proved innocence.

The refugee elder, Arda Men, distrusted personal salt cups because thin clay shattered during travel. She formed hers from chickpea dough baked hard around a pinch of salt. A diner could crush the edible cup underfoot, but rain or hungry children might damage it before a table began. Nerin suggested enclosing each one in a pierced nutshell. Arda refused because Kelar kitchens could not depend on imported nuts. She stitched little holders from reed and marked each with the diner’s name in flour paste.

Their argument lasted through two batches. Neither woman softened her standard. The final design used materials a refugee line already possessed: reed, dough, salt, and flour. Savra wrote nothing for them. Local disagreement had produced a better tool than her intervention would have.

At the camp table, Vei tested a lentil paste meant to slow repeated ration bonds. Oil delayed transfer, but soldiers might eat another stamped loaf before the first bond died. She added coarse husk that lengthened digestion and bitter fenugreek that changed under biased weighting. The herb grew metallic when one hidden practice seat received twice the fatigue of the others. Every cook tasted the shift.

“Warning, not protection,” Vei said.

“Good,” Savra replied. “Call it what it can do.”

Hesh objected to any recipe that depended on a trained palate. He crumbled one of his barley wafers over the paste. A concealed line caused the fragments to draw into a wet clump around the weighted seat mark. The effect could be seen by a child who had never tasted fenugreek. Vei altered her tablet to use both signs where barley remained available and the herb alone where it did not.

Dima’s board received the harshest trial. Mava ordered a live goat-blood heat bag set beyond the wall, carrying warmth as an embodied state without placing an animal inside their eating circle. The cooks woke a six-seat practice bond, then locked the seal into Dima’s wooden grooves. With her left hand she turned the broad knife. Her right palm held the board through a leather strap rather than finger grip. The first cut stopped short because the blade met grit.

Pain rose in all six palms as the delayed heat gathered. Dima reversed the board, used its second groove, and finished two sectional cuts before anyone touched the origin ember. Prepared cups took the first recoil. The remaining heat spread across declared diners for three breaths, then ended when Hesh buried the ember in its own ash.

Dima examined the nick in her blade. “A field knife dulls. Add two cutting grooves and require a second edge.”

Sela burned that correction into the board itself. A tool used under panic could not depend on a tablet staying close.

By late afternoon, the floor had become a map of practical limits. Dry regions could not rely on open water cups. Canal settlements could. Refugee lines needed disposable seals because custody changed every hour. River barges needed weighted jars that would not spill during recoil. Terrace kitchens needed oil measures narrow enough to show delay. Twelve solutions remained separate because twelve conditions truly differed.

The twelve recipes shared no flavor, vessel, or fuel. They shared disclosure: source, names, weights, duration, severance, recoil provision, and the cook inside the list.

A rider reached the guild gate before the tablets had dried. His mule carried foam under its harness, and white grain dust covered his sandals. Teren’s mark closed the message tube.

Savra read standing beside the ash oven. Imperial columns had turned toward the Halvek granaries. Storehouse guards reported army ovens traveling in advance. If Halvek fell, the coalition would lose the largest grain reserve west of Namarra. If it held, the empire’s southern road would starve.

“How long?” Mava asked.

“Three days before their main force. Less for the ovens.”

Hesh looked at the tablets. “You trained us to refuse tables. Now you want us at a battle.”

“I want you where seals will be hidden,” Savra said. “Whether you go belongs to you.”

Pava wrapped her fish tablet in oilcloth. Nerin packed the severance cups. One after another, the cooks named where they would serve: gate kitchen, field tents, refugee line, granary yard, river landing. None offered to build a table that made fighters stronger.

At sunset they left Ulsor by separate roads, carrying twelve recipes for refusal under identical rules. Savra traveled toward Halvek with Dima and the unlit seal tray between them. Behind the city wall, thirteen places in the training kitchen stood empty, each salt line broken by the person who had sat inside it.

Chapter 13 — The Battle of Empty Bowls

Teren measured the Halvek granaries against sunrise and found twenty minutes in which an army might still behave like thieves instead of executioners.

The town occupied a shelf above three irrigation channels. Mud-brick walls enclosed the houses, while the grain compound stood outside the eastern gate behind its own low stone barrier. Twelve domed storehouses rose from the yard, each limewashed against insects and capped with a clay vent. Enough winter barley, millet, and rice waited inside to feed the western coalition for five months. Enough also to keep Rovan’s southern columns marching until Equinox.

Imperial troops had reached Halvek two days before Teren. They held the east gate, two watchtowers, and the granary roofs. Their cookfires smoked behind the northern wall where townspeople could not see them. No siege engines had arrived. Captain Orel Tas estimated three hundred soldiers, perhaps another hundred levy spears.

“Estimate?” Teren asked him in the predawn ditch.

Orel’s wrists remained tied, though the rope ran long enough for him to crouch beside the map. “The road carries ten companies. They would leave three at the stores and divide the rest between crossings.”

“You captains all count alike?”

“We eat from the same allotment tables.” Orel looked toward the unseen smoke. “Or we did.”

Teren had not offered him freedom for information. Orel had not asked. The captured officer understood that testimony might keep bodies alive without clearing his own place in the system.

Savra knelt opposite them with a bowl of damp ash. She drew the compound as a cook saw it: fires, ventilation, food routes, possible seal chambers. “If their ovens stand here, behind the north wall, smoke will cross the town before it reaches soldiers on the east roofs.”

“Does that matter?” Saret asked.

“It tells me the ovens were placed for fuel shelter, not fast service. Rations came out last night. Any bond they carry may remain active until midday.”

Teren shifted weight off his left ankle. Six days spent returning grain had cost him the first approach to Halvek, but two river towns now supplied shallow boats for the lower channel. His force held four hundred fighters, eighty town levies who had slipped out before imperial closure, twelve cooks distributed among aid stations, and thirty-seven wagons with less than a week of grain. He needed the stores. Need did not alter the rules they had just taught.

The plan avoided the houses. River crews would enter through the middle irrigation channel, lift the compound’s south gate from inside, and admit Teren’s main force. Archers would suppress the granary roofs with padded shafts and smoke pots. Nobody would loose flame above grain. Savra’s cooks would inspect captured ovens before any soldier ate. Halvek levies would open the town gate only after the store yard had fallen.

“Civilians remain in three refuge halls,” said Naro, the Halvek miller commanding the local levies. He marked them on Teren’s board: bathhouse, southern shrine, old press yard. “The empire told families they would be safer together.”

Savra’s head lifted. “Together near food?”

“The halls have communal kitchens.”

“How many ate last night?”

“All of them. The soldiers issued barley.”

Cold moved through Teren before the air had changed. “Marked loaves?”

Naro shook his head. “Porridge from town pots.”

“That can still carry oath grain,” Savra said. “We cannot assume a table without evidence.”

Neither could they wait. Imperial reinforcements would reach the northern road by noon.

Teren ordered one change: no stabbing blows against roof soldiers until Savra inspected the first captured ration. Hooks, nets, shields, and surrender calls first. Saret disliked the restriction. He repeated it down the line anyway.

At sunrise the channel boats began moving beneath reed mats.

Water reached Teren’s waist when he entered with the second group. Cold tightened his ankle and made the old crushed bones feel packed with grit. Ahead, rowers used hands against the channel wall to guide their boats without splashing. The granary barrier rose twelve feet above them. Its drain grille had lost two bars during the night to Halvek children carrying files under their shirts.

The first boat passed through. Then the second. Teren followed Saret into a culvert that smelled of wet grain and rat dung. Someone above called the dawn measure. Bronze clappers sounded from the store yard, three slow strikes.

Army meal bells answered from behind the town wall.

A shudder ran through Orel, who came under guard at the rear. “They renewed the ration table.”

The culvert opened beneath a stone trough. Teren climbed out and saw imperial soldiers eating on the roofs. Each held half a white loaf. Black scoring divided the crust.

“Now,” he said.

The south gate rose. Coalition fighters poured into the yard behind hide shields. Archers sent padded arrows upward. One roof guard toppled from surprise rather than injury. A smoke pot burst against limewash, covering the western dome in bitter gray.

Teren ran toward the gate winch. His first task involved access, not enemies. He cut the retaining cord, put six people on the wheel, and watched the heavy doors spread wide enough for the main force. Saret’s units entered in four columns, each turning toward its assigned storehouse.

Imperial resistance stiffened at the central scales. Soldiers formed behind grain carts with short spears. A lieutenant shouted that Halvek stores belonged to the Hall of Measures. Naro answered by holding up the town’s granary key.

No one heard the next words. Shields met. Hooks dragged at spear shafts. Teren struck a soldier’s wrist with the flat of his blade.

A scream rose behind the town wall.

It came faintly through mud brick, one high voice cut short. Teren froze long enough for the soldier to hit his shoulder. The blow spun him against a cart.

Saret drove a shield between them. “Move.”

Teren recovered and looked toward Savra. She had heard it. Her face carried the stillness she used when a pot changed taste before anyone else noticed.

Another coalition fighter hooked a soldier’s knee. The man fell hard on the stones. Beyond the wall, several people cried out together.

“Stop striking!” Savra shouted.

The order traveled poorly in battle. Metal and wood filled the yard with their own language. On the eastern roof, an imperial archer shot into Teren’s third column. A Free Granary woman took the arrow through her upper arm. Her brother loosed a pointed shaft back. It entered the archer’s thigh.

From the refuge halls came a surge of screams.

Teren climbed onto a grain cart. “Shields only! Pin them. No wounds.”

His nearest units obeyed. Farther east, two people had already reached the roof ladder. A soldier kicked one in the face. The other drove a knife up under his boot. Every injury produced its answer inside town: a child’s shriek, an old man bellowing, women calling names through walls.

Imperial soldiers heard it too. Some looked toward the houses with confusion. Others used the advantage. The lieutenant pushed forward, knowing Teren’s people had stopped cutting.

Savra reached the nearest dead cookfire, a shallow iron tray beside the scales. She scraped ash into her palm, smelled it, and spat into the dust. “No source ember. Service point only.”

“North wall,” Teren said.

Getting there required crossing the central yard while refusing the ordinary means of battle. Teren ordered two shield lines locked into a wedge. They advanced by pressure, crowding spear points upward and forcing soldiers back without piercing. Every step cost bodies. A shield edge broke Saret’s lip. Teren’s bad ankle turned on loose millet and sent pain up his calf. He stayed upright by gripping a cart rail.

At the third granary, Naro’s levies opened a side lane. Civilians appeared atop the inner wall, waving cloths. One woman held a wooden bowl above her head. Empty.

“Don’t hit them!” she screamed. “Whatever you do, don’t hit them.”

Teren saw blood on her cheek with no cut in her skin.

The wedge reached the north passage. Behind it, built into a disused oil house, six imperial ovens burned beneath copper hoods. Army bakers worked under guard. Ration loaves moved from cooling boards into wicker hampers while a seal clerk pressed black lines into each crust.

Dima’s trainees entered behind Savra. Hesh closed the passage with a shield team. Nerin seized the nearest hamper and broke one loaf over her barley wafer. The crumbs clumped around five marks hidden inside the central score.

“Civil seats,” she said.

Savra cut a loaf into its layers. “More than five. Those marks branch.”

A baker tried to run. Dima tripped him with the end of her severance board and pinned his sleeve beneath her left knee. “How many halls?”

“I bake what they stamp.”

“Which grain?”

“Halvek barley.”

Orel stepped into the oven house under guard. The imperial lieutenant saw him and called him traitor.

“Answer the healer,” Orel said to the baker.

“Three refuge halls,” the man gasped. “Every household bowl. We were told the distribution would prevent panic.”

Savra found the live seal beneath the largest oven. It had been mortared into the floor, a black ceramic cylinder surrounded by nine salt channels. Three channels ran west toward the refuge halls. Six ran east and south toward soldier companies. The civilian lines carried no names, only household bowl marks copied from Halvek’s grain register.

“The town ate porridge from registered bowls,” Savra said. “That placed them inside the seal. Soldiers eat the ration loaves. Wounds move west.”

“How much?” Teren asked.

She brushed sour plum over the exposed channels. Purple weights surfaced. “Four civilian shares to one military share.”

The number struck harder than any spear. Every wound inflicted on a soldier divided mostly among families behind the wall. Four parts carried west, one stayed with the body wearing armor.

Saret wiped blood from his mouth. “Sever it.”

“If I cut the town lines, recoil returns their accumulated share across the soldiers,” Savra said. “That might end the fight. It might also kill everyone holding a burden already.”

“Then extinguish the ember.”

“Same danger, less control.”

Teren looked through the oven-house doorway. His people held nine of twelve granaries. Imperial defenders had fallen back to three roofs and the east wall. By any field count, victory stood close. Another push would secure the stores. Another push would injure Halvek through every body his fighters struck.

“How long until digestion ends it?”

“Last porridge may bind the households six hours. Ration loaves keep the soldiers active until midday if they eat only once.”

“They can eat again.”

“Yes.”

From outside came the sound of wood breaking. An imperial squad had overturned a scale cart into the shield line. Teren heard three impacts, then cries within the town.

Naro gripped the oven door. “My wife and sons are in the bathhouse.”

“Can you evacuate them without feeding anyone?” Teren asked.

“The gates are barred from inside. Imperial guards hold the streets.”

Saret’s eyes stayed on Teren. “We can take the east gate in ten minutes.”

“At what cost?”

“Less than leaving these stores to Rovan.”

That answer used Teren’s own language. He had turned people into necessary columns often enough to recognize the sound.

Captain Orel approached the seal without crossing its salt. “Order the town to empty the bowls.”

Savra looked at him.

“Company procedure,” he said. “A registered vessel carries the household seat while the oath grain remains in digestion. Break the bowl and you weaken the encoded identity. Not enough to sever, but enough to reduce the precision.”

“You knew that?” Teren asked.

“I knew soldiers were told to protect registered bowls during occupation. I did not know why.”

Savra considered the seal. “He may be right. The household mark anchors through the vessel pattern. Breaking bowls would blur individual routes into the hall’s common line. Burden still reaches civilians, but spread wider.”

Teren gave Naro the order. Halvek levies along the wall began shouting toward the refuge halls: empty every bowl, smash the registered pottery, eat nothing from army grain.

At first no answer came. Then clay broke inside the town.

One impact, another, a scatter of hard cracks. The sound spread from the bathhouse to the shrine and press yard. Families smashed cooking bowls, storage bowls, cups stamped for tax measures, dishes inherited through marriages. Imperial administration had made each vessel an address. Halvek destroyed its own kitchens to become harder to wound.

The change reached the oven seal. Purple lines blurred beneath Savra’s plum wash. A soldier stumbled outside after taking a shield blow. Instead of one distant scream, a low groan moved across many voices.

“Reduced,” Savra said. “Not ended.”

Teren climbed the oil-house step and looked over the yard. Nine granaries in coalition hands. The south gate open. His standards above the scales. Imperial troops compressed against the eastern quarter.

“Signal withdrawal,” he said.

Saret stared at him. “We have the compound.”

“We cannot hold it without fighting soldiers tied to the town.”

“We came for five months of grain.”

“And we leave before victory turns every sack into a reason to keep hitting them.”

Saret’s jaw worked. He raised the red withdrawal pennant.

Horn calls sounded across the compound: two low, one high. Confusion answered. Fighters on the roofs had to abandon positions they had bled to gain. Wagon crews who had reached the store doors began shouting that the grain stood open. One unit refused to move until Teren crossed the yard and gave the order himself.

The eastern granary roof posed the first problem. Nine coalition archers occupied it behind a lime parapet, and the ladder below had splintered during the assault. Imperial soldiers held the stairs inside. Teren sent a rope team along the roofline while Saret formed shields beneath them. Each archer descended with bow unstrung and wounded arm strapped before the next person moved. Padded enemy shafts struck the shields. A pointed arrow would have pierced the formation, but Captain Varo had not yet changed ammunition. Teren wondered whether restraint or shortage had made that decision and found he did not care which kept people alive.

At the scales, three Halvek levies refused to abandon the town key. Naro had to take it from his cousin’s hand. The bronze teeth carried their fathers’ tool marks, older than Namarra’s occupation. Leaving the lock open felt to them like surrendering the stores by choice.

“We take the key,” Naro said. “They keep a door without a town.”

His cousin struck the grain cart once with an open palm, then joined the withdrawal.

A wounded imperial soldier lay between the shield lines with a crushed pelvis. Every attempt to lift him sent a corresponding cry from the southern shrine. Dima crouched beside him, assessed the angle, and ordered a door pulled from the oil house. Coalition bearers carried him flat. The decision slowed the center column by forty breaths and saved at least one hidden civilian from repeated movement pain.

“Why take him?” asked the young fighter beside Teren.

“Because leaving him for his comrades to drag still hurts the town,” Teren said. “And because he is wounded.”

Those reasons did not need to compete.

Storehouse crews began closing vents as they left. If imperial troops fired the grain, sealed domes might slow flame. Saret ordered them onward, then countermanded himself when he saw oil jars beside the doors. Four people rolled the jars into the central trough and cracked them under water. Another team scattered oven wood beyond the compound wall. They could not remove every means of burning, but they did not have to make destruction easy.

Near the south gate, a Free Granary standard had caught on the winch. Its bearer climbed back for it. Teren shouted him down.

“They’ll display it as captured.”

“Let them. Cloth costs less than your body.”

The bearer cut the pole instead. Red fabric fell into trampled barley and disappeared beneath retreating feet.

Only then did Teren understand how the phrase would sound outside his own mouth: retreat before victory. Rivals would call it weakness. Hungry fighters would call it theft of what they had won. Halvek families might call it too late because the first blows had already reached them. None of those judgments changed the order. Command meant carrying the consequence after the horn, not pretending one clean reason had produced it.

“Carry wounded, not sacks,” he told them.

A young fighter had already filled her cloak with barley. She looked at him as if he had betrayed the dead. “We won this.”

“Put it down.”

“My village has three days.”

“So does Halvek.”

Her hands opened. Grain ran between her boots.

Withdrawal under an enemy’s gaze demanded more discipline than advance. Teren assigned the freshest shields to the rear and sent wounded through the channel gate first. Archers used smoke to hide movement without setting roofs alight. Naro’s levies remained long enough to relay bowl-breaking orders, then climbed ropes down the outer wall.

Imperial soldiers realized what was happening. Their lieutenant ordered a charge.

Orel stepped onto the passage threshold. “Captain Varo! Hold your line.”

The lieutenant faltered at hearing a superior officer among the enemy.

“You are a prisoner,” he called.

“Yes. You are still responsible for the civilians carrying your wounds. Hold.”

Varo could have ignored him. Instead he looked toward the town, where smashed pottery continued to ring. Perhaps he had not known. Perhaps he knew and understood that charging men who refused to strike would make the arrangement visible even to his own companies. He lowered his spear.

The pause bought four minutes.

At the south gate a frightened mule overturned an aid cart. Teren tried to lift the shaft and his left ankle failed beneath him. Pain drove him to one knee. Saret and Vei raised the cart while Dima pulled a wounded archer free. Teren stood again by locking his boot against a stone, but the joint had begun to swell beyond the leather.

“Get on the boat,” Saret said.

“Last unit first.”

“You can’t walk the culvert.”

“I can crawl it.”

He stayed until the final shield team reached the gate. Savra came after them, carrying no imperial seal. She had scored the exposed channels with vinegar and ash so any later investigator could read them, but breaking the cylinder remained too dangerous while civilians digested the porridge.

“What happens when the bond ends?” Teren asked.

“The soldiers keep their own wounds. Civilians retain whatever reached them. Nothing reverses simply because the table goes cold.”

No magic returned payment after the meal.

They entered the channel as imperial drums began above. Teren crawled through the grille with his left leg dragging. Behind him, Halvek’s compound remained intact. The twelve white domes stood against the noon sky, full of food they had chosen not to hold through civilian pain.

Coalition forces withdrew two miles to the reed flats. Their casualty count reached thirty-one wounded, four dead. Halvek’s toll remained unknown. Runners from the refuge halls reported crushed fingers, spear cuts appearing on unbroken skin, two fractured legs, and one child who had stopped breathing after a roof guard fell. Dima sent healers back under white cloths.

The first healer returned with her white sleeve torn away. Imperial guards had admitted her to the bathhouse but barred her from the shrine. Halvek families accepted bandages and refused coalition food, afraid every loaf carried another seat. Dima ordered sacks opened in public, grain poured loose, and clay seals smashed before cooking. She tasted from each unbound pot with her own name written on a scrap beside it. Only six families accepted portions.

Naro came back carrying the dead child’s registered bowl in two pieces. The fracture crossed a blue heron painted inside the rim. He placed both pieces before Teren without accusation.

“His mother wants to know which soldier fell,” Naro said.

Teren could not answer. The battlefield gave him locations and units, not a line between one rooftop body and one child behind a wall. Hearthbinding conserved injury but erased accountability by dividing it across hidden routes.

“Then write that you do not know,” Naro said. “Do not write battle loss.”

Teren took a wax tablet from Saret. He recorded the child as an unnamed Halvek boy, death linked to an unidentified roof fall through the civilian table. The words lacked a name because nobody had yet given him one, not because the death belonged in a remainder column. Naro watched until the stylus stopped.

Teren lay beside the irrigation bank while Vei cut his swollen boot away. No bone had broken. The old injury had torn something deep along the outer ankle. He could stand with a staff, but not run.

Across the flat, fighters filled cooking lines without talking. They had expected grain porridge from captured stores. Their cooks boiled the last river rice instead. When portions were counted, several pots came up short. People sat holding empty bowls because nobody wanted to take a share from the wounded.

Smoke rose over Halvek near sunset.

At first Teren thought the imperial ovens had renewed. Then flame broke through the first granary vent. A second dome darkened. Fire traveled too quickly for accident, moving storehouse to storehouse along oil poured at the doors.

“They’re burning it,” Saret said.

The coalition watched five months of food become columns of sparks. Halvek townspeople formed bucket lines at the wall, but imperial guards kept the compound gate closed. Heat split limewash from the domes. Grain roared inside like dry rain.

Naro made no sound. His empty bowl rested upside down before him.

Teren understood the choice Rovan’s commanders had made. If the coalition would not seize grain through civilian wounds, the empire would deny it to everyone. The fire also destroyed the most visible evidence beneath the oven floor. By dawn only scorched channels and testimony would remain.

A signal rider arrived after dark carrying sheets printed in Halvek itself. Imperial presses had begun before the granaries burned. The proclamation named the day the Battle of Empty Bowls. It claimed Free Granary raiders attacked protected stores, wounded households through unlawful hearthbinding, and fled after loyal troops saved the town. Rovan Dast promised emergency grain to every family that renewed its oath.

Saret crumpled one sheet. “They prepared the lie before we came.”

“They prepared the table,” Teren said. “The lie followed the same route.”

He wanted to answer with numbers: nine granaries taken, thirty-one wounded, four dead, three refuge halls used as sinks. Numbers could prove sequence. They could not make Halvek families forget which army had arrived under Teren’s standards when pain entered their bodies.

By midnight, refugees began leaving through the south gate. Some came to the coalition aid fires. More turned west without looking at them. Each carried food in cloth or bare hands because their registered vessels lay smashed.

Teren sat with his injured ankle bound to a willow splint and watched Dima distribute the last rice. No one rang a meal bell. Across the flats, hundreds of empty bowls reflected the granary fire until soot covered them. The coalition had retreated before victory, lost the Halvek granaries, and left Rovan holding the story of who had struck first.

Chapter 14 — The List of Distant Hearths

Issen tasted Halvek at noon from a porcelain spoon painted with victorious wheat.

The first course held clarified chicken broth, white barley, and three drops of saffron oil. Court menus named it Granaries Preserved. Forty ministers watched Issen lift the spoon beneath the bronze scales of the Hall of Measures. Rovan Dast sat at the long table’s head, his hands folded beside an empty bread plate.

Safety required Issen to note bitterness, fever, rot, poison, and unlawful hearth residue. Ceremony required them to smile before speaking.

They swallowed.

Smoke touched the back of the tongue first. Not cooking smoke. Granary fire, dry and mineral, carried through barley that had never been close to Halvek. Under it came the bodily residue the cooks had failed to clarify away: bruised shoulders, a cut without a blade, one small chest struggling after impact. The sensations had thinned through distance and repeated service, but First Taster training made thin things legible.

“The course is safe,” Issen said.

Applause moved around the table.

Their mouth had certified the absence of poison. Everyone present heard the presence of justice.

A minister from the southern roads raised his cup. “To loyal Halvek, defended against Free Granary violence.”

Rovan drank water. He had not eaten the broth.

The second course contained twelve little white loaves, each scored to resemble a closed granary. Issen broke one. Black hearth-salt marked no active bond, only a decorative line added after baking. The kitchen had learned restraint in presentation while the empire’s proclamations did not.

“Does victory taste peaceful?” Rovan asked when attendants moved between them.

Issen kept their answer inside ceremonial bounds. “It tastes of smoke and displaced injury, Grand Provisioner.”

“Battle leaves both.”

“Displacement leaves a direction.”

His expression remained patient. “You are paid to identify hazards, Issen. Not to infer policy from seasoning.”

“The body does not separate them for me.”

“That limitation once made you useful.”

Attendants removed the bread before Issen could examine the scoring again. At the far service door, old Rel caught their eye. Rel had dressed tasting tables since before Issen entered the Hall at fourteen. The attendant’s face showed no warning, only the careful vacancy of someone who had heard a dangerous exchange and intended to survive it.

After the banquet, Issen returned the silver tasting spoon, washed their mouth with salt water, and signed the public record. They wrote no poison detected. In the private residue column, accessible only to the First Taster and Grand Provisioner, they wrote smoke; transferred blunt trauma; divided laceration; juvenile breath distress. The stylus paused over source.

Officially, the source read Halvek rebel attack.

Issen wrote concealed seat structure likely and sealed the tablet before caution corrected them.

For eleven days they had copied provincial hearth initials from tasting menus. Menus did not publish seat lists, but imperial courses named ingredient origins in a code of prestige. River rice from one district appeared beside salt from another, while a black-salt notation in the margin indicated ash compatibility. Issen had learned to read absences: a province whose oil appeared without its grain had contributed a burden seat rather than flavor.

Their private list held eighty-nine names. The copper ledger stolen by Savra’s coalition, if court reports spoke true, held ninety-seven. Rovan’s clerks had moved the remaining information after the field-kitchen breach.

The Hall archives occupied three rooms below the public tasting chamber. Issen possessed access to the first, where menus and poison records slept in cedar boxes. The second held ration formulations. The third, called the Quiet Measure, admitted only the Grand Provisioner, chief sealwright, and archive attendant on duty.

Rel kept the keys that afternoon.

Issen waited until the victory table had demanded a fourth course. Bells summoned every senior attendant upstairs to carry sugared fruits shaped like Halvek bowls. Issen entered the menu archive, closed the door, and opened their tasting chain at its seventeenth link. The link contained the grain of black hearth-salt they had taken from the forbidden sample on Day 1.

Salt did not open locks. It rewrote nested arrangements when carried by a First Hearth. Here, without that ember, the grain offered only its ash signature. Issen pressed it into warm wax on the day’s menu box, then compared the mark to wax residue along the locked door of the Quiet Measure.

Both carried the same capital ash.
