By midnight, the compact tile held nine signatures, three refusals, and one open place for Eren Ford. Twelve separate salt lines remained visible on the floor. At their center sat no flame, only the boxed impression and an empty bronze stand shaped for an ember they did not possess.

Chapter 19 — The Convoy of Stolen Embers

Issen tasted the ash before the guards permitted them to see the fire.

Two attendants held a white linen screen across the eastern service court of the Hall of Measures. Behind it, bronze feet scraped stone. The sound came in four careful movements, each followed by the grunt of men carrying more weight than ceremony admitted. Dust had already begun to blow over Namarra’s roofs, a thin ochre veil from the northern road. It gritted between Issen’s teeth and spoiled the official fiction that anything inside the Hall could remain pure.

“First Taster.” Warden Pel held out a shallow silver dish. “Certify the convoy ember.”

For six days he had addressed Issen by office only. Their rooms stood locked at night. Two clerks watched their meals, and a guard followed them even to the washing court. Yet the Equinox ceremonies required a mouth that the city recognized. Suspicion could confine a person; ritual still needed the shape of their obedience.

Issen touched one finger to the ash. The dish held three grains of black hearth-salt, powdered barley husk, and a smear of oil from the brazier’s inner rim. They placed the finger on their tongue.

Warmth moved first, not as heat but as the remembered labor of a public oven: shoulders lifting kneaded loaves, palms burned on stone, bellies waiting through lean years for civic bread. The sensation belonged to bodies linked through old grain oaths. Beneath it lay a newer pressure, narrow and deliberate. Provincial embers strained toward the capital. Somewhere an old woman’s empty stomach contracted. Somewhere else a soldier breathed without feeling the blister beneath his heel.

The sample carried no poison. It carried a design.

“Sound,” Issen said.

Warden Pel watched their throat. “Swallow.”

They did. Refusal now would change nothing except the hour of their arrest.

The attendants lowered the screen. The First Hearth brazier stood in a cedar litter banded with bronze. Soot had blackened its bowl and three of its lion-shaped feet. A hood of pierced copper enclosed the coals, keeping sparks from the procession cloth. No flame showed, but an ember glowed through each hole like a watchful eye. Four Hall bearers gripped the litter poles. Six soldiers surrounded them, and two feastwrights walked behind with sealed jars of oil and fresh charcoal.

Small enough to steal, Issen thought. Heavy enough to make theft ridiculous.

Grand Provisioner Rovan had moved the brazier from the Hall’s inner kitchen to the Equinox pavilion every year since Issen took office. The route normally crossed a colonnade and one guarded square. This year he had ordered a dust procession through five wards, a public display meant to show Namarra that the oldest civic ember still traveled unharmed while rebels burned granaries.

It also placed the brazier among crowds.

Pel lifted Issen’s silver tasting chain from an attendant’s tray. Each linked spoon represented a province whose dishes they had certified. The chain felt heavier than it had at dawn, though its weight had not changed. Pel fastened it across their chest himself.

“You will walk beside the left pole,” he said. “At the spice arch, the fish gate, and the pavilion stair, you will taste the carrier oil. Any deviation, and the convoy returns.”

“Any deviation?”

“Any.”

Formal courtesy gave Issen room to bow. “Then I hope your bearers have eaten.”

The nearest man looked away.

At the court gate, drums began a pace fit for men carrying fire. Outside, thousands waited behind rope lines. Bakers had dusted the road with flour patterns that vanished beneath the first sandals. Children on roof ledges waved strips of red cloth. Market elders cast pinches of grain toward the litter and shouted blessings older than the empire’s measures.

Issen walked at the left pole and counted mouths.

A sesame seller near the first turning wore a blue cord around her thumb. A water carrier had tied the same blue beneath his leather wrist guard. On a balcony above the dyers’ lane, three women shook rugs in a dust wind that needed no rugs shaken. Merchant allies, the ceramic message had promised. Not soldiers. Not tablewrights. People who moved sacks, jars, invoices, and rumors past gates because cities starved without them.

The drums carried the convoy into the dust procession.

Wind thickened along the Avenue of Kilns. Powdered clay curled around ankles and entered every fold of cloth. The brazier bearers coughed. Soldiers wrapped scarves over their mouths. Issen kept their face uncovered. A taster who feared dust would never have survived Namarra’s public kitchens.

At the spice arch, Pel broke the wax on the first oil jar. Issen sampled a drop. Sesame, old smoke, a trace of bitter orange. Harmless. They declared it so, and the feastwright poured the oil through a narrow pipe in the copper hood.

The ember brightened. Warmth touched Issen’s molars.

On the second route, beside the coppersmiths’ court, a mule broke its traces exactly when the water carrier crossed behind it. Clay jars struck the paving. Guards shoved spectators backward. The convoy tightened around the brazier and continued. No one reached for the litter. The disturbance merely taught Pel to expect interruption.

At the fish gate, the sesame seller stumbled under a basket. Silver anchovies spread across the road like spilled coin. One bearer slipped, recovered, and cursed. Again the guards closed ranks. Again nothing happened.

Issen tasted the second oil. Pepper leaf, rendered goat fat, fenugreek browned past bitterness. Beneath those flavors waited a resin that numbed the tongue but did not poison it. A forged burn required pain, not injury. The merchant cooks had measured well.

They let the oil touch the inside of their lower lip.

Numbness came first. Then heat sharpened along the gum where Issen had cut it that morning with the edge of a tasting spoon. They drew breath through their teeth and dropped the dish.

“Stop the ember.”

The words carried farther than a shout because the drums ceased at once.

Pel seized their wrist. “What did you taste?”

“Copper bloom. Hot resin. The carrier jar has taken a kiln taint.” Issen pressed two fingers to their mouth. Blood made the lie visible. “If poured, it may force the oldest ember into a false ash signature.”

One feastwright went pale. Such a corruption had never occurred, which made its consequences impossible to disprove quickly.

“Return to the Hall,” Pel ordered.

“Through this crowd?” Issen asked. “With a suspect carrier open?”

Protocol offered the alternative before Pel could invent another. An origin ember under suspected taint had to be screened from public offerings and tested against an unbound flame. The nearest licensed inspection kitchen occupied the House of Pepper Weights, thirty paces into a side court.

Pel swore softly. “Move.”

The guards turned the litter beneath a lintel too low for the ceremonial banners. Cloth tangled on spearheads. Dust followed them into the passage. Behind, the crowd pushed to see why the First Hearth had vanished from its promised route.

The inspection court held three roofed ovens, stacks of amphorae, and no public exit. Issen had walked it twice in earlier years. It also had a charcoal hatch opening into an alley broad enough for a handcart.

Pel stationed four soldiers at the street arch and two beside the hatch. “Test it.”

A licensed pepper cook waited by the center oven, hands shaking over an unbound charcoal flame. Issen did not know his name. They knew the blue thread sewn beneath his collar.

He poured clear oil into a black cup. “First Taster.”

Issen knelt by the brazier. Their chain clicked against the bronze litter band. Through the hood’s perforations, the old coals gave off a dense heat that smelled of barley crust and generations of scraped ovens. Issen had no black hearth-salt left. Hall searchers had taken the grain from their chamber after the archive theft. Even if they had kept it, one grain could only make the First Hearth recognize copied weighting. It could not make four bearers forget the load, nor convince eight armed men to surrender it.

People had to perform the theft.

Issen held the cup over the unbound flame. The pepper cook counted seven breaths, then tipped in the suspect oil. It flared green from copper powder planted in the jar.

“Down,” Issen said.

The cook overturned a basket of ground pepper into the oven.

Flame struck powder. A red cloud punched across the court. Men screamed, coughed, and clawed at their eyes. The soldiers at the hatch turned away. Through the cloud came the crack of wood: the sesame seller and water carrier had rammed a loaded handcart through the charcoal doors.

Issen found the litter pole by touch. “Set it on the cart.”

One bearer obeyed from training before he understood. A second released his end to cover his face. The litter tilted. Bronze rang against stone.

Warden Pel caught Issen by the silver chain. “You.”

His astonishment hurt more than the links cutting their neck. Issen twisted, not away but toward him, and unhooked the chain at the shoulder clasp. Pel staggered backward with the whole bright burden in his fist.

“Keep the office,” Issen told him.

The tasting chain fell among pepper, anchovy scales, and trampling feet.

Merchant hands heaved the brazier onto the cart. A wet felt cloth covered the copper hood without smothering its vents. The water carrier kicked loose a clay stop under the cart bed, revealing a cradle packed with sand. They lowered the First Hearth brazier into it and rolled through the broken hatch.

Issen followed into the alley as an imperial horn sounded behind them.

Namarra’s service lanes had been built for food, not fugitives. The cart struck baskets of coriander root, squeezed between olive presses, and scattered chickens through a vinegar yard. Ahead, a woman in flour-gray clothes raised a shutter. The allies shoved the cart inside a bakery and dropped the door as soldiers entered the lane.

“Upper oven,” she said. “Move the fire, leave the litter.”

The cedar litter bore the Hall’s stamp and added most of the bulk. Four bakers lifted the bronze bowl by hooked rods while Issen unlatched the hood. The First Hearth came free with a groan of metal. Its three feet fitted into a round bread tray prepared with sand and broken tiles.

No one bowed to it. That helped.

The bakery master covered the tray with a wicker dome used for cooling loaves. “Roof stair.”

Before they could lift it, the wounded pepper cook stumbled in from the alley carrying the Hall’s first oil jar. He poured its remainder into the bakery’s waste crock, then pressed the wax seal against a lump of warm dough. The imprint came away clean.

“Why copy it?” Issen asked.

“Because by sunset every loyal kitchen will be ordered to refuse unstamped carrier oil.” He wrapped the dough in flour cloth. “By sunset we will need oil that appears loyal.”

His answer disclosed the scale of what these people risked. An official could defect once and imagine the act complete. A baker who helped them still had to open tomorrow. The sesame seller still had accounts, kin, creditors, and a stall that guards knew how to find. Each ordinary obligation gave the Hall another cord to pull.

Issen took the copied seal from him. “No. Put it in the sour bin.”

The pepper cook stared.

“If you use the stamp, Pel will compare the oil ledgers. He will find every kitchen that bought the same pressing. Let him believe we carried one prepared jar.” They crushed the dough impression between finger and thumb, breaking the circle. “Change the route instead of multiplying the evidence.”

The bakery master gave one clipped nod. Two younger workers overturned a rack of cooling loaves, not to block the door but to make the room look robbed in haste. Another scraped the blue thread from the pepper cook’s collar and dropped it into the oven mouth. Nobody asked Issen to certify these choices. Their knowledge could reduce the danger; it could no longer command the people taking it.

A spear struck the shutter below as they climbed.

Flat roofs joined much of the ovens’ quarter. Families dried figs there, restaurants kept herb boxes, and kitchen workers carried hot pans between buildings when alleys flooded. Dust turned the tiles treacherous. Two porters took the hooked rods and ran with the brazier suspended between them. Issen followed, their mouth bleeding, their ceremonial robe bright enough to guide every archer in Namarra.

On the second roof, they tore off the red outer layer and shoved it into a bread oven chimney. Underneath they wore a porter’s undyed tunic. The badge of office remained stitched at the throat. Issen cut it free with a baker’s paring knife.

“Left,” called the sesame seller, whose name Issen still did not know.

A roof kitchen opened around a clay stove where six women fried lentil wafers. They did not stop cooking. One pulled a pan aside. Another cast a cup of water on the embers, making steam. The porters carried the First Hearth through the cloud and across a plank to the next house.

Soldiers reached the bakery roof behind them.

“First Taster!” Pel’s voice rose through dust. “In the name of the Hall, stop.”

Issen almost did. Twenty years of training lay inside that command: stop before crossing a kitchen threshold, stop before tasting, stop before contradicting a measure certified above one’s rank. Their body slowed while their judgment continued forward.

The sesame seller caught their elbow. “Are you coming?”

“My name is Issen Mar,” they said, partly to her and partly to the man behind them.

“Then come, Issen Mar.”

They crossed the plank. A soldier stepped onto it before the porter behind Issen could pull it away. The sesame seller tipped a jar of frying oil across the boards. He slipped to one knee but clung on.

The next kitchen belonged to a public lodging house. Forty bowls of millet porridge stood ready for noon laborers. The cook had drawn a salt line around his stove and listed eight kitchen workers on a wax board. No concealed seats showed in the seal. He held up a ladle.

“Runner share,” he said. “Declared: heat and leg fatigue, eight of us to four of you, equal weights, until the coals go dark. Refuse by passing the bowl.”

The porters drank. The sesame seller drank. Issen passed it on.

The cook frowned but did not argue. Consent meant accepting refusal when haste made refusal inconvenient.

Warmth entered the runners’ limbs. The kitchen workers sagged at their stations, each taking a visible fraction of the porters’ exertion. No strength appeared from nowhere. The porters gained what those workers agreed to carry, nothing more.

They crossed three roofs before the soldiers regained the distance.

At the copper-dome kitchen, the planned route broke. Imperial archers had taken the grain exchange roof ahead. Below, mounted guards flooded the west alley. The road south toward Tal Jes began beyond both.

The water carrier leaned over the parapet. “Lower it through the date warehouse.”

“Too narrow,” one porter said.

Issen measured the brazier with their hands, then the roof vent. Bronze projections at the feet made it wider by two fingers. “Turn it on its side.”

“You’ll spill the ember.”

“Not if the ash bed stays packed. Oil the inner lip. Rotate slowly toward the unblackened foot.” The old civic fire had accrued uneven ash. They had seen the residue during every annual inspection and never imagined the knowledge would matter outside ritual.

Kitchen workers brought hooks and a fish net. Together they wrapped the brazier, tipped it, and fed it through the vent. Issen descended first. Hot ash sifted through the hood and burned their forearm, but no coal escaped. Below, dates lay stacked in sealed jars. They guided the bowl past them one handspan at a time.

An arrow struck the roof tiles.

The last porter came down bleeding from the scalp. Issen tasted iron in the air, their own blood and his, and had to separate them by sight. He could still focus. He took the rear hook.

They emerged onto a lane where three grain carts waited under awnings. Each held sacks stamped for a different provincial kitchen. A gray-haired merchant stood beside the middle team.

“East gate closed,” he said. “South road watched. We have two wagons and one fire worth an empire.”

“Tal Jes,” Issen said.

“Riders can take the direct road by dusk.”

“The canal road forks at the lime pits. Send one wagon south with the empty litter cloth. Take the brazier west under millet.”

“That road also leads to Tal Jes, eventually.”

“Eventually will have to suffice.”

They bedded the bronze bowl among sacks, keeping the pierced hood clear. The wounded porter climbed in beside it with a water skin to damp the packing. Issen took the driver’s bench. Behind them, the sesame seller cut the blue cord from her thumb and tied it around the wagon brake.

“What should I call you?” Issen asked.

“Veyra Sen. Oil factor, when anyone believes invoices.” She slapped the wagon side. “Today, an accomplice.”

At the western gate, dust had swallowed the sun. Guards searched carts entering Namarra, not those leaving toward lime country. The wagon passed beneath the arch while alarm bells rang from the Hall of Measures.

Beyond the walls, the road divided around a dry shrine. The decoy took the southern branch in a storm of mule bells. Issen steered west, where the land folded into ochre gullies before turning toward Tal Jes.

From the ridge behind came the flash of imperial spearheads. Far ahead, riders appeared on the western road, dark points against the dust.

Veyra looked from one pursuit to the other. “Which office tells us what to do now?”

Issen felt the bare place at their throat. The chain no longer pulled their posture straight. Beneath the millet sacks, the oldest civic ember of Namarra breathed through its copper hood.

“No office,” they said. “Take the gully.”

The wagon left the road.

Chapter 20 — The Red Millet Terraces

Teren sent the first irrigation gate down before sunrise, and three hundred fields began to drown.

Water left the upper channels in a broad brown sheet. It ran over embankments, filled the footpaths between plots, and turned loose volcanic soil into mud deep enough to seize a cavalry horse at the fetlock. Farmers watched from the stone stairs with their mouths set hard. The crop stood two weeks from cutting. Every stalk of red millet bent beneath the flood that might keep imperial riders from Tal Jes.

“How much survives?” asked the terrace waterkeeper.

“West of the fourth spillway, half.” Teren kept his eyes on the measuring stakes. “East, less.”

“You said a third last night.”

“Last night their scouts had no fire carts.”

Below the lowest terraces, imperial columns moved through dawn haze. Four thousand infantry by Teren’s count. Six hundred mounted soldiers. Twelve ox carts carried clay jars under wet hides, which meant lamp oil or pitch. Behind them rolled portable field ovens with bronze seals nailed above their mouths. The army intended to fight unburned, unblistered, and fed by pain taken from people whose names would never reach this hillside.

Tal Jes had eleven hundred defenders. Three hundred could hold spear lines. The rest served as slingers, water crews, litter bearers, cooks, and farmers who knew every retaining wall. Free Granary wagons had brought grain for two days. The city cisterns could supply five. Issen and the First Hearth had not come.

A signal mirror flashed from the northern watch.

One flash, pause, two. Riders on the gorge road.

Teren’s pulse lifted. “Ours?”

The watcher looked again. “Too much dust. Can’t tell.”

He marked the report on his wax slate. Every column had a quantity. Every route had a time. Uncertainty ought to narrow as scouts returned. Instead, the missing brazier sat in the center of his calculations like a hole cut through the board.

His left ankle had swollen overnight inside its boot. The old crush injury protested each stair, so he distributed his weight through a cut-down spear used as a staff. Pain had once seemed useful to him because it reported damage honestly. Since learning what imperial tables could hide, he trusted pain less and the absence of it not at all.

Lale came up from the communal ovens carrying two flat loaves wrapped in cloth. Flour marked her forearms. Eren Ford’s evacuation had put two hundred river people in Tal Jes, along with the banked ember from her oven and every child she could fit aboard the barges. She had not forgiven Teren for making the decision without asking them. She had still brought breakfast.

“Eat,” she said.

“Bound?”

Her expression tightened. “Red millet, goat curd, pepper oil. No seal. No table. I watched the kneading.”

He took a loaf. The hot pepper reached his nose before he bit. “Any word from the hospital?”

“Dima needs clean linen. Twenty-six patients from yesterday’s skirmish, nine too fevered to move.”

“Put litters beside the west road.”

“She already did.” Lale looked toward the flooding plots. “You have drowned people’s winter grain.”

“I know the count.”

“That isn’t what I said.”

A horn sounded below. Imperial skirmishers entered the first drowned field.

Teren handed back half the loaf. “Keep the ovens ready.”

“For food?”

He did not answer quickly enough.

Lale caught the omission. “Teren.”

“For food,” he said, and watched her go knowing the statement held only the part of his plan she had asked about.

The contingency lay inside a grain chest beneath the command awning. He had prepared it after finding Eren Ford among the hundred cold seats. Removal without replacement caused recoil. If evacuation stripped civilians from Rovan’s design, a replacement table might force the burden onto soldiers who had chosen to fight. That had been the arithmetic he wrote alone. Savra would have called the words might and chosen weak joints, places where command disguised a seat nobody had named.

He had hidden the seal anyway.

At first light, Teren’s ration master had cooked two cauldrons of red millet with charred goat curd. One fed the lower spear companies. The other stood near the allied field hospital, officially reserved for litter crews and defenders rotating off the line. Both burned over mixed embers: Tal Jes terraces, Ulsor salt ovens, Eren Ford, and six Free Granary wagon hearths. The ash signatures gave the food reach. A pair of bronze plates inside Teren’s chest gave it direction.

The plates came from an imperial field-kitchen seal captured on the canal. Savra had shown the cooks how to break its hidden weighting. Teren had copied the arrangement before it was destroyed, replacing five village marks with the hospital grain oath and weighting the fighting companies light.

He told himself he would use it only if the wall failed.

The first wall failed before the sun cleared the eastern ridge.

Imperial sappers pushed wicker screens through the flooded millet and reached a retaining bank. Under sling stones, they drove iron hooks into the dry-set masonry. Oxen pulled from firmer ground. The bank opened like a split sack. Mud, stones, and water crashed into the terrace below, carrying six defenders with it. Imperial infantry entered the breach behind tall shields.

Teren sent forty spears down the west stair and ordered the next irrigation gate broken rather than lowered. Released water took the flank of the advancing column. Men fell. Their shields clogged the spillway. More soldiers climbed over them.

Fire arrows rose from the oil carts.

They came high and slow, points wrapped in burning cloth, hundreds against the paling sky. Terrace crews had soaked roof mats and covered the grain stores, but the millet itself stood dry above the flooded lower plots. Flame landed among seed heads. Wind pulled it uphill.

“Cut three fields,” Teren ordered. “Throw the green stalks into the fire lanes. Move the eastern reserve to the second wall.”

A runner hesitated. “The reserve hasn’t eaten.”

“Give them the lower cauldron.”

That order activated nothing. Not yet.

The defenders consumed millet from the first pot and returned to the walls. They carried ordinary fatigue, ordinary fear, ordinary burns. Teren watched one man drop his spear after an arrow scorched his palm. Another vomited behind the wall and went back to the line. The empire pressed uphill with soldiers whose gait remained impossibly even.

By midmorning, Tal Jes had lost two terraces and eighty-three defenders from the line. The hospital sent word that every floor space had filled. Dima asked for twenty more water carriers. Teren could spare six.

A second mirror signal flashed from the north. Riders had entered the gorge. Still no colors.

“If it’s Issen, they need the city standing,” said the waterkeeper.

“If it’s imperial cavalry, we need the city standing.”

“How?”

Teren looked down at the breach. One more wall gave access to the terrace road. Once the oil carts reached that grade, they could burn the ovens, the hospital, and every gathered ember before the First Hearth arrived.

He went to the command awning.

The seal plates felt cold despite the day’s heat. One carried the fighting companies as twelve shallow notches. The other hid the hospital grain oath beneath the mark for litter crews. The arrangement promised a four-to-one weighting: fighters would keep one share of heat and pain while the concealed seats carried three. Conservation remained absolute. There would be no unwounded army, only wounded people placed where the attackers could not see them.

Teren pressed the plates into clay and sent the fresh seal to the lower cauldron.

“Stamp the bowls for the second-wall companies,” he told the runner. “Serve everyone who comes off rotation.”

“Seat declaration?”

“Fighting companies and support crews, four measures to one.”

It sounded almost true. Support crews included the hospital under the stolen notation. He did not say so.

“Where does command sit?” the runner asked.

Teren took a bowl. “Here.”

He ate first because he had learned at least that much from Savra.

Red millet stuck to his teeth. Pepper oil burned the cut at the corner of his mouth. The bond settled through his limbs when the origin embers caught the seal’s shape. Fatigue lifted from his shoulders, not vanishing but flowing along the table. Farther down the hill, someone else received the heaviness.

He felt the same easing pass through the defenders. Backs straightened. Burned hands closed around spear shafts. Men who had limped to the cauldron walked away with pain thinned to a manageable line.

The counterattack began.

The lower channel offered room for only twelve people abreast. Teren divided the reserve into groups of ten and assigned one terrace farmer to each, because soldiers could read an enemy line while farmers could read water. The first group crossed behind a curtain of spray from the broken gate. The second carried clay lids as shields against burning oil. A third rolled wet wool around the handles of orchard hooks and used them to drag wicker screens apart.

From above, the movement looked too small to alter a battle. At ground level, every body occupied exactly the width required. Farmers opened side sluices after the groups passed, cutting off pursuit. Sling crews received stones in shallow grain baskets rather than sacks so runners could count ammunition by basket and know when a position had five volleys left. Teren shifted twelve water carriers from the eastern cistern, then shifted four back when he saw defenders drinking from flooded plots. Bad water could win an hour and lose a company by nightfall.

A terrace boy reached the awning with an arrow through the loose flesh above his hip. He had carried three bundles of green millet to the fire lane before noticing it. Under an ordinary body’s report he should have collapsed in the first field. The battle-table had spread his pain so widely that he mistook damage for permission.

“Hospital,” Teren told the litter crew.

The boy gripped Teren’s sleeve. “I can carry again.”

“No.”

“My group needs the stalks.”

“They need you not bleeding into a channel.”

As the litter left, Teren felt a needle of pain at his own hip. The share vanished almost at once into the concealed support weights. He knew where the remainder went. Knowledge did not stop him from sending the next group forward.

Teren sent two companies along an irrigation ditch hidden by tall millet. Slingers struck the imperial screen crews from above. Terrace farmers rolled millstones down narrow stairs, breaking shields and forcing men into flooded plots. At the breached wall, defenders locked spears and pushed.

Imperial fire landed among them.

A jar shattered on the parapet. Burning oil covered three people. They screamed, but the sound broke off with unnatural speed. One rolled beneath a wet mat and rose with red skin that should have blistered deeper. Another kept fighting while flame still clung to his sleeve.

Heat entered Teren’s own arm as a broad sting, then dispersed.

He had taken a command share, lighter than the lower seats but real. That fact tried to serve as proof of fairness. It proved only that he had included himself in a wrong done to others.

“Advance the reserve,” he said.

Tal Jes retook the broken wall before noon.

Below, imperial soldiers began to falter. Their own burden-table had too many injuries to export cleanly after three linked villages evacuated. The imperial front ranks showed blisters and exhaustion. Men stumbled in mud. The difference appeared tactical, almost righteous. Teren could see victory taking shape through quantities: another hour, perhaps two; one push down the terrace road; fire carts captured; route held for Issen.

Then a hospital bell rang without stopping.

The sound came from the upper fig court, where the allied field hospital occupied three wine sheds. One continuous iron note meant fire. Two meant bond injury. The bell struck without pattern because whoever held the rope had lost the strength to count.

Dima appeared at the head of the stair. Her right hand curled against her apron, fingers unable to grip, while her left held a blackened bowl.

“Teren Avo!”

Her voice crossed the command terrace. Runners stopped. The waterkeeper turned from the channel maps.

Dima threw the bowl at Teren’s feet. It broke, showing the copper-red stamp impressed beneath its base.

“Whose seal?” she asked.

Teren dismissed nobody. Privacy would repeat the act. “Mine.”

“Name the seats.”

“Lower companies. Litter crews. Command.”

She stepped close enough for him to see a fresh burn climbing from her left wrist beneath the sleeve. Not her injured hand. A new wound.

“Name all of them.”

Around them, fire arrows continued to fall. A horn signaled imperial retreat from the second wall. Teren heard the opening and hated himself for hearing it as an opening.

“The allied field hospital,” he said.

The waterkeeper made a sound like breath punched from the chest.

Dima pointed uphill. “Twenty-six old patients. Nine fevered. Thirty-one new wounds. Four cooks, seven healers, sixteen litter workers. They ate from the support cauldron because your stamped bowls named them. Their burns appeared while your companies kept their skin.”

“I weighted the hospital across all support seats.”

“You hid them.”

“I needed one hour.”

“A patient with a splinted leg has burns inside her mouth. She never went near flame.”

Teren looked toward the fig court. Smoke rose there, but not from burning roofs. Cooks had extinguished the hospital hearth. The origin ember still lived in the lower cauldron, keeping the bond active through every swallowed bowl.

“Break the seal,” he said.

Dima’s face did not soften. “Abrupt severance throws every share back across the companies. Half of them stand above a drop.”

He had known that. The knowledge had occupied a column in his plan, under risks acceptable if the city fell.

“Bring them down by rotations,” Teren said. “Two companies at a time. Sit them before extinguishing.”

“The empire is withdrawing,” said the waterkeeper. “If we pull back now, they keep the lower terraces.”

“Then they keep them.”

A terrace captain came running from the wall, helmet split along one side. “We can take their oil carts. Give me the eastern reserve.”

“No.”

“We have them.”

“No pursuit. All companies return by marked stairs. Anyone who ate from the sealed cauldron sits inside the lower salt court.”

The captain stared at the broken bowl. Understanding traveled across his face. He spat beside Teren’s boot and went to relay the command.

Withdrawal from victory proved harder than retreat under defeat. Defenders saw imperial backs and wanted payment for burned fields. Farmers shouted that the lower terraces could still be saved. Teren sent runners with the same order until his voice roughened: return, sit, do not eat, do not cross the salt court.

He descended with the last command group. His ankle failed halfway down. Pain flared through the old crushed joint, no longer thinned because the hospital cooks had cut one origin ember. He caught the wall and continued.

Inside the salt court, four hundred defenders sat shoulder to shoulder. Some carried burns they had not felt until leaving the line. Others looked untouched. Dima’s assistants moved among them, recording injuries before severance so no cost could disappear into confusion.

Lale stood at the cauldron with a splitting maul. “Where are the plates?”

Teren took them from his coat.

She read the visible notches, then turned the second plate toward the light. Eren Ford’s old seal craft used river curls around communal marks. She found the hidden hospital oath immediately.

“You copied our oven mark.”

“I used the evacuated oath to redirect Rovan’s recoil away from Eren Ford.”

“Into sick people.”

“Into the seats closest to the defense.”

Her eyes met his. “Sick people.”

No quantity changed the noun.

Dima established three temporary salt lines across the court. Fighters with open burns sat nearest the extinguishing crew. Uninjured diners moved to the outer ring. Teren took a place in the center, where the hidden weighting had assigned command its protected fraction.

“You move to the hospital share,” Dima said.

Lale looked at her. “Can the seal be changed while active?”

“Not safely without the First Hearth. We can crack his command notch and let recoil find him by the ordinary seat list.”

“Do it,” Teren said.

Dima used the heel of her broad-handled knife. Her right hand could not hold the plate steady, so Lale pinned it to the stone. One strike flattened the command notch. A second split it through.

Pain arrived like a door kicked inward.

Heat covered Teren’s left arm, chest, and face. His mouth filled with the taste of scorched cloth. His ankle took the weight of men who had run uphill on torn ligaments. Breath shortened under many fractions of panic. None of those states belonged solely to him. He could not identify lives or memories, only embodied burdens redistributed by his recipe.

Across the court, defenders cried out as their shares returned. Dima’s crews held them seated. Water waited in measured jars. Nobody promised the pain would vanish.

Lale swung the maul into the clay seal on the cauldron. It cracked. Dima cut the mixed ash with plain salt, dividing the table into smaller rings. Cooks smothered each origin ember in a planned order: wagon hearths first, then Ulsor, then Eren Ford, leaving Tal Jes for last so the nearest diners carried the final recoil.

At the hospital, bells found a count again. Two strokes. Pause. Two.

When the Tal Jes ember went dark, Teren vomited red millet onto the court stones.

The bond ended. Injuries remained where conservation had left them. Some defenders now bore the burns they had escaped during battle. Hospital patients kept damage already taken before severance. A healer had lost skin along both palms. The woman with the splinted leg could not swallow water without pain.

Tal Jes had held. Its people had paid once in fields, once in battle, and once through Teren’s hidden arithmetic.

Dima made him walk through the hospital before anyone discussed command.

The wine sheds had low doors and floors sloped for washing casks. Patients lay on reed mats between drainage grooves. A man admitted yesterday with a broken collarbone now carried a fire stripe across his back, although he had spent the battle under a tiled roof. Two fevered children from an evacuated terrace house shivered in the heat after sharing the defenders’ sudden cold when wet clothes met mountain wind. The table had not moved disease, but it had laid battlefield states over bodies already laboring to survive their own conditions.

At each mat, Dima asked Teren to read the injury board aloud. She held the boards in her left hand. Her right fingers could steady only the lower edge.

“Transferred heat burn, shoulder and spine,” he read. “Origin uncertain. Support weighting, three shares.”

“Again.”

“Exertion collapse. Breath rhythm recovered. Two shares, possibly more.”

“Again.”

The woman with the splinted leg turned her head toward him. Blisters lined the inside of her lips. Her name, Eda Tel, had appeared on the morning hospital list. Teren had seen it as one entry among twenty-six old patients.

“Her name,” Dima said.

“Eda Tel.”

“Her seat?”

“Hidden under litter support.”

“Her choice?”

“None.”

Eda could not speak. She lifted two fingers, then pointed toward the terrace wall. Teren did not know whether she meant two sons fighting, two burns, or two times she had been used. Hearthbinding carried no explanation with pain. He had created a system in which the person harmed could not even know whose survival had entered her mouth.

Outside the last shed, a cook sifted spoiled millet from the extinguished support cauldron. Nobody could eat the remainder; the cracked seal and mixed ash made its weighting unsafe. Two full baskets went into the burial trench. Tal Jes had defended grain by ruining fields, then wasted cooked grain to end the hidden table. The quantity belonged on Teren’s account along with bodies.

Guildspeaker Mava Ul arrived from the upper council house as the wounded were being sorted. Salt dust streaked her robe. She listened to Dima’s account without looking at Teren. Around them, terrace captains reported that the empire had withdrawn beyond sling range and begun making camp. The city possessed a few hours, not victory.

Mava held out her hand. “Command tally.”

Teren removed the knotted cord from his belt. Each bead marked a company, wagon group, water crew, or allied kitchen under his orders. He had carried it since the Free Granary convoys left the river road.

“Teren yields command,” he said, making the declaration loud enough for the court recorders. “I activated a concealed weighting against the allied field hospital. I named support seats falsely. I relinquish field authority and submit the act to coalition judgment.”

Mava accepted the cord. “You will retain no operational command. You may answer questions of route and supply when asked. The terrace captains’ council holds the walls until we vote a replacement.”

“Yes.”

“Do not make me ask twice for a seal you have hidden.”

“There are no others.”

Mava turned to Dima. “Verify that.”

Dima nodded once.

The command awning came down before afternoon. Its maps moved to the council house. Teren sat outside the field hospital with his boot cut open around the swollen left ankle. He could still count the litters entering: sixty-one from the battle, thirty-eight injuries worsened by the table, eleven healers or workers burned. Numbers mattered because each person needed water, linen, fat for dressings, and a place out of the wind. They could not make the decision clean.

Lale washed soot from her arms at a basin nearby. Her own burden amounted to a blister across one shoulder, transferred from a defender through the hospital seat. She had received it while tending an oven she believed unbound.

“I thought the city would fall,” Teren said.

“I know.”

“If it fell, the hospital would have burned.”

“I know that too.”

“We held long enough for the gorge road.” He stopped. The sentence sought the word necessary before he spoke it.

Lale dried her arms. “Say what you did. Don’t ask the outcome to rename it.”

He looked down at the cut boot. “I used them.”

“Yes.”

“I sat in the table.”

“That did not ask them.”

“I stopped it.”

“After Dima found it.”

Every answer remained accurate. None balanced the account.

Teren pressed both palms against the bench until the transferred burning steadied. “Will you testify?”

“At your judgment.”

“For me?”

“For what happened.” Lale lifted the basin. “You are my brother. That does not turn your act into a necessity.”

She carried the dirty water toward the dressing yard.

From the northern watch came three mirror flashes, then one: a wagon in the gorge, imperial riders behind. No signal confirmed who drove it.

Teren’s body rose before authority remembered it no longer belonged to him. Mava’s new runner crossed the court without waiting for his order. Terrace crews moved toward the gorge under the council’s command.

He remained beside the hospital as the red millet terraces smoked below.

Chapter 21 — The First Hearth in Exile

The left wheel struck a buried stone and threw the First Hearth against its bed of millet sacks.

Issen heard bronze grind under the wicker dome. Veyra pulled the mules hard toward the gorge wall while the wounded porter braced both boots against the wagon bed. A red coal showed through the brazier hood, bright as a cut. If the ash bed cracked, they could lose the old ember without any soldier touching it.

“Slower,” Issen called.

“Riders behind,” Veyra said.

“Dead fire moves at no speed.”

The wagon slowed enough for Issen to climb over the sacks. Dust coated their mouth, filling the cut along the lower gum. They pressed folded wool between the brazier’s nearest foot and the wagon side, then drove a wedge of broken tile under the sand cradle. Heat reached through the cloth. The ash burn on their forearm had begun to blister, but the coal remained nested.

Behind them, imperial horns answered from both ridges.

The gully narrowed toward Tal Jes. Red volcanic walls rose above a track made for goats and terrace carts, not a loaded grain wagon. Three mounted soldiers followed at bow range. More traveled on the southern road, visible only when spear points crossed gaps in the rock. Veyra’s decoy had bought half a day. It had not bought the city.

The wounded porter, Jorin, lifted his head from the wagon rail. Blood had dried through his hair. “Smoke ahead.”

Issen smelled it before they saw it: millet straw, wet fig wood, and pepper oil spilled onto hot clay. Not battlefield smoke. Kitchen smoke, made thick on purpose.

A pale column rose from the next bend. Another appeared higher on the gorge wall. Then five more fires answered from ledges where no sensible cook would build an oven.

Veyra narrowed her eyes. “Friends?”

“Someone knows the ash signals,” Issen said.

An arrow struck the wagon gate behind them.

The mules bolted. Veyra fought the reins while Issen threw their weight across the brazier hood. The left wheel rode the slope, lifting high enough to show daylight beneath it. Jorin grabbed the rear brace with one hand. The wagon slammed level and entered smoke.

Figures moved inside the cloud. A broad-shouldered woman caught the lead mule’s bridle. Her right hand hung stiff near her waist; she controlled the animal with her left.

“Dima Orr?” Issen asked.

“You know my hand. That saves introductions.” Dima dragged the team toward a cut in the wall concealed by smoke. “Get down. The wagon continues.”

“With what?” Veyra asked.

“Millet, your blood on the bench, and a brazier-shaped basket full of hot tiles.”

Four terrace merchants swarmed the wagon. Two unpinned the wicker dome. Another cut the lashings around the sand cradle. A fourth shoved an identical covered basket beneath the sacks. Their movements carried the speed of rehearsal and none of the silence of coercion; each called the task before doing it.

A lean cook stood inside the rock cut, scar crossing her left forearm. Savra Kel did not wear the black robe shown in Hall mosaics. She wore a smoke-stained apron and carried no knives.

“Keep the hood upright,” Savra said. “The north foot has less ash beneath it.”

“You know the First Hearth?”

“I know how Rovan neglects a brazier while polishing its litter.” Her gaze took in Issen’s forearm burn, Jorin’s head wound, and Veyra’s cramped hands. “Who ate from a table?”

“The roof runners. Not us.”

“Good. Then nobody here carries a delayed share we cannot see.” Savra pointed into the cut. “Bring it through.”

The passage opened into an abandoned quarry bowl. Twelve people waited around three cookfires. Every fire burned ordinary millet straw mixed with one visible coalition ember. Names had been written in charcoal on flat roof tiles. Beside each name sat a measure: one cup, two cups, or half. No marks hid beneath clay.

Issen read all twelve. “What are you binding?”

“Runners only,” Dima said. “Heat and leg fatigue. No pain, no wound share. First group carries the brazier to the fig ledge. Second takes it down the terrace stair. Third draws the riders after empty fire baskets.”

“Who takes the weight?”

“Everyone who eats, including cooks. Four resting carriers hold two shares each. Eight runners hold one. Duration ends when we drown each straw fire at its marked cistern.”

A boy no older than sixteen stood beside the upper fire.

Issen looked at Dima.

“He carries a smoke basket, not bronze,” she said. “His mother is beside him, and both volunteered after hearing the weights. If either passes the bowl, the route keeps the gap.”

No hurried replacement. No office declaring that the design required every seat.

Outside the quarry, Veyra cracked the reins. The decoy wagon rolled deeper into the smoke with three merchants hidden under its cloth. Imperial riders shouted and followed.

Savra lifted a bowl of thin millet drink. “Issen Mar. You are injured and have been driving. You do not run.”

“The brazier answers to my tasting office.”

“Your office stayed in Namarra. Does your body have strength for a share?”

Issen’s ceremonial reflex searched for the answer expected by rank. Savra waited without offering one.

“No,” Issen said. “Not safely.”

“Then witness the list.”

Dima read every name aloud. Each person answered with weight, route, and right to pass. Two chose half shares after reporting blisters from yesterday’s battle. The resting carriers sat on reed mats and accepted the doubled fatigue of the runners. Savra drank from the first bowl. Dima took the second. The cooks had seats.

Only then did the others eat.

The voluntary runner table engaged through the three straw fires. No light marked it. Shoulders lowered among the resting carriers as exertion began to reach them from people already lifting the bronze bowl. A runner’s breathing steadied. Another flexed a cramped calf and took position at the forward hook.

“First team,” Savra said.

They carried the First Hearth brazier from the quarry.

Issen went beside it without touching. Their task consisted of watching the hood, smelling for spilled ash, and tasting any residue that escaped. It felt almost insultingly small after the office had trained them to stand above whole kitchens. Small work proved exacting. At the fig ledge, a coal shifted toward the hood. Issen stopped the carriers, used a wet reed to press the ash inward, and checked the smoke for copper taint.

Imperial riders entered the quarry behind them.

The decoy fires had been abandoned with bowls left beside false footprints leading west. One group of pursuers took the trail. Others found the brazier tracks in loose dust and turned toward the ledge.

“Second team,” Dima called.

The first carriers lowered the bowl onto a stone cradle. They sat immediately. Four new runners took the hooks. Nobody praised those leaving the weight; the table had promised relief at a marked point, and relief required no gratitude.

The terrace stair descended along the outside of the gorge wall. Tal Jes spread below, its red millet terraces striped with black from yesterday’s battle. Smoke rose from wet fields. Imperial camps occupied the lower road. The city itself clung to the volcanic slope through retaining walls, ovens, cisterns, and narrow plots of figs.

Between the stair and the nearest gate lay open ground.

A rider appeared above. He loosed an arrow at the carriers.

Dima struck it aside with a clay smoke lid held in her left hand. The impact spun her against the wall. Her right fingers could not close around the edge, but she trapped the lid beneath her forearm and kept it up.

“Move.”

The second team ran.

Heat and fatigue shifted through the declared seats. On the quarry ledge, resting carriers took the climb into their own legs. Savra’s breath shortened beside Issen. She had eaten as a cook, so the table did not leave her clean while others moved her design.

Two more arrows hit stone. A third entered a runner’s thigh.

He fell without releasing the rear hook. The brazier tilted toward the drop.

Issen seized the rod. Pain from the runner did not transfer because the recipe excluded wounds. His injury remained his. His share of heat and fatigue still moved until he severed or the fire died.

“Pass,” Dima told him.

“I can hold.”

“Pass the bowl or keep it. You cannot keep the route.”

The man dragged his personal salt cup from his belt and smashed it on the stair.

His bond ended. Recoil returned his exertion among the remaining named runners and resting carriers, distributed across the open half-share left for route loss. Savra staggered. One carrier cursed. Nobody inherited the arrow wound.

Issen and a reserve runner lifted the hook together. They crossed the open ground under slinger cover from Tal Jes. Stone shot drove the imperial archers behind the ridge. At the first city cistern, Dima kicked over the marked water jar. Far above, the corresponding straw fire hissed out.

Half the table released.

Runners bent over, taking back the ordinary ownership of their breath. The remaining fire carried the final team through the gate and into a roofed grain court. When the bronze feet touched its prepared sand bed, Savra extinguished the last origin ember with brine.

The First Hearth’s own coal continued to glow beneath its hood. Separate fire. Separate custody. The rescue bond ended cleanly around it.

Dima leaned against the wall. “That is how we get a brazier into a city without putting the city in the bowl.”

Issen looked at the injured runner being carried toward the hospital. “He broke his cup.”

“He knew it would hurt us.”

“And you left room.”

“We learned.” Her glance went toward the burned terraces. “Not quickly enough.”

Tal Jes’s allied field hospital occupied old wine sheds above the grain court. Issen entered behind the brazier and found rows of people injured in places they had never fought. A woman with a splinted leg had blisters inside her mouth. Healers wore fresh dressings on their hands. Dima’s new burn disappeared under the sleeve of her left wrist while the ruined fine grip of her right hand remained what she had chosen at Ember Bridge.

Teren sat outside beneath a fig tree with his left boot cut open. No command tally hung at his belt.

Before Issen reached him, Dima brought them to the hospital’s support kitchen. The broken bowl seal lay in a tray beside the hearth, separated into six pieces. Its visible face named litter crews with four ordinary notches. Under the base, a river curl enclosed the hospital oath.

Issen turned the largest fragment with a wooden pick. “This cut comes from the Hall’s sixth campaign alphabet. The clerk reads it as provisions retained near the fighting line.”

“Can you prove what it means?” Mava asked from the doorway.

“In the ration archive, yes. Here, I can show the copying error.” Issen indicated a hooked stroke where the clay had spread. “An original sixth-alphabet support mark bends inward. This one bends toward the Eren Ford curl because whoever copied it worked from a reversed bronze plate.”

Dima’s mouth hardened. “Teren had plates.”

“Then preserve every fragment. Wrap them separately in unbound cloth. Do not fit them together over live ash.”

A kitchen assistant reached for linen, paused, and asked, “Why unbound?”

“Because the clay still carries mixed ash. It cannot activate without food, origin embers, and swallowed seats, but evidence should not share signatures before judgment.” Issen heard how easily instruction returned to their voice. “That is a recommendation, not an order.”

Mava nodded to the assistant. “Do it.”

At the wash bench, Dima showed Issen the morning serving boards. Twenty-six old patients had been written by name. The hidden seal had not erased those names; it had placed a second meaning beneath the category that joined them. Concealment did not always require missing records. Sometimes it survived by making the truthful list answer the wrong question.

“I tasted support broths for years,” Issen said. “We were trained to ask whether litter crews could continue carrying. Nobody asked if the people on the litters had entered through the same oath.”

“You could have asked,” Dima said.

“Yes.”

“Did any rule forbid it?”

“No. The rule made the question seem incompetent.”

Dima used her left hand to scrub millet from a pot. Her damaged right hand held the rim against the bench with the heel of the palm. “Then when you speak in the public kitchen, name the questions too. Crimes like this survive on what respectable people learn not to ask.”

Issen memorized the sentence without claiming it as their own.

They passed the dressing shed again on the way out. Eda Tel slept with her splinted leg raised and her burned mouth open to cooler air. Beside her, a young assistant measured water in spoonfuls because swallowing a cup caused too much pain. Issen could identify the carrier oil used in the Hall eleven years ago by residue alone. That skill could not tell Eda which defender’s fire had reached her. The meal had moved sensation without memory, context, or consent, leaving the injured person with burden stripped even of explanation.

Only then did Issen approach the fig tree.

Teren rose when Issen approached, then steadied himself on the bench. “You reached us.”

“So did your riders.”

“Not mine anymore.”

Mava Ul came from the shed. “Teren yielded command yesterday. He used a concealed seal against this hospital.”

No courteous phrase could make the sight easier to receive. Issen looked from Teren to the patients. “Under support notation?”

His face changed. “Yes.”

“The Hall files hospital oaths under litter provision. I certified three campaigns where that column appeared.” Issen had called the meals safe because no poison entered the officers’ bowls. “I never asked whom support meant.”

Teren sat again. “You came to testify against Rovan.”

“I came to stop pretending the testimony ends with him.”

By afternoon, the First Hearth stood in the largest public kitchen of Tal Jes. Guards remained outside at Savra’s insistence. Inside waited the twelve Ulsor cooks, Dima, Lale, Mava, terrace witnesses, Teren without authority, and every merchant ally who had survived the convoy. Veyra arrived last after abandoning the decoy wagon in an irrigation ravine.

Issen asked for a stool, then removed the remaining insignia from their tunic. The cut badge at the throat had left stitches and a pale square. They laid it beside the brazier.

“My office certified safety by tasting the portion prepared for power,” they said. “I swallowed first and allowed the order to imply that distant mouths would receive the same meal.”

Nobody interrupted.

“I certified ration oil during the Qar campaign when village cold seats carried soldiers’ fever heat. I certified the Hall banquet after the Seln Canal seizures while imprisoned millers received the guests’ hunger. At Halvek, I tasted the victory course after civilians had already taken the wounds. I did not write those seat lists. I made their concealment look safe.”

Issen named each ceremony they remembered. Twelve in full. Seven more with dates but uncertain kitchens. They identified which tasting phrases had concealed absent ledgers: no harmful residue, balanced according to measure, fit for common service. Every phrase had described the food placed before them, never the system carrying its cost.

Veyra listened with her arms folded. Mava’s recorder filled two wax tablets. Savra did not offer forgiveness. She asked for the name of one carrier oil used eleven years ago, and Issen gave it.

When the account ended, their mouth had gone dry.

Mava said, “Will you sign this before public witnesses?”

“Yes.”

“Will you submit to judgment after Equinox?”

“Yes.”

No title protected the answers.

Savra approached the brazier. “Open it.”

Issen unlatched the pierced copper hood. The hinges resisted from years of soot. Beneath lay a packed bed of pale ash surrounding seven coals. Six glowed red. The seventh had darkened along one edge during flight but still carried heat at its center.

Savra held Rovan’s witness spoon impression above the bowl without touching it. Black hearth-salt clung in copied grooves. The old ember responded with a faint blue line around the spoon’s shadow.

“The topology is still writable,” she said.

Dima exhaled. Lale set a clean clay ring around the bronze feet. Each cook laid a separate line of provincial salt outside it, not yet joined.

Issen looked at the blackened bowl on a terrace city’s worktable. “What do we call it now?”

“The First Hearth in exile,” said Mava. “Until a public council decides whose hearth it can be.”

A bell keeper entered from the western tower, face streaked with soot. “The capital signal fires have changed. Equinox provisioning began at noon.”

Savra counted on her fingers, not ceremonially but like a cook measuring what remained. “Three days.”

“Three days until the Feast?” Veyra asked.

“Three days to make a recipe no single fire can own.” Savra lowered the hood but left the latch open.

Around the brazier, the provincial salt lines remained separate. Outside, crews carried the Tal Jes wounded past the kitchen door one named person at a time, while recorders marked each injury and declared share on fresh clay.

Part IV

Chapter 22 — A Recipe with Open Seats

Savra rolled dough across the Tal Jes worktable until it covered a map of the basin.

The dough contained no live ash, only red millet flour, water, and enough oil to keep it from cracking. She pressed one hundred chickpeas into it where Issen’s list placed the intended hearths. Three clustered in Namarra. The last sat in Kelar, marked by a bitter fenugreek seed.

Around the map stood twelve coalition cooks, Mava Ul, Issen, Dima, Lale, and four merchant route planners. The First Hearth rested on a sand bed behind Savra. Its hood remained open. Seven coals breathed under pale ash.

“Show us why the circles from yesterday cannot scale,” Mava said.

Savra drew separate salt lines around four chickpeas. “A local table can disclose its own seats, weights, duration, and severance. We proved that. The First Hearth can hold the circles in one topology without mixing their lists. We proved that too.”

She set Rovan’s witness spoon impression at the center. It carried the copied grooves of a master weighting, black hearth-salt packed into each line.

“When a person leaves one circle, recoil returns to that circle. At twelve seats, we leave a planned share unfilled and spread the return among people who agreed. At one hundred hearths, thousands may sever at once. No local cook can reserve enough bodies for an event in another province.”

“Then do not connect them,” said a fish cook from Ulsor.

“If we do not connect them, Rovan’s weighting enters first and joins every provincial ember to his hidden wards. The counter-recipe must overwrite that topology at activation.” Savra pressed a ring around all one hundred markers, then cut the ring into local arcs. “Connected enough to refuse him. Separate enough to refuse us.”

Dima leaned her right forearm on the table. Her fingers could not pinch salt, so she used a broad-handled scoop held against her palm. “Yesterday’s runner table had an open half-share.”

“For twelve people.”

“Make the open share travel.”

Savra looked at the dough. An open seat could receive recoil if the recipe named its function and left capacity for it. In the experiments at Ulsor, they had kept such seats local, visible, and small. A traveling opening across nested circles needed the First Hearth to recognize it without turning into Rovan’s universal sink.

“Not travel,” she said. “Remain common.”

Nobody answered immediately.

She cut a hole in the dough map’s center.

Lale saw it first. “One seat outside the local arcs but inside the outer topology.”

“Named,” Savra said. “Weighted openly. Attached to every severance line, never to ordinary burden.”

“A person?” Issen asked.

“A body. The magic cannot seat a principle.”

Mava’s face gave away nothing. “Whose?”

Savra placed her left thumb into the hole. The oven scar crossed its rim. “We test before we answer that.”

The first trial used eight diners, four local tables, and four cookfires set along the courtyard wall. Each pair received a different preparation of millet: sour paste to accelerate heat transfer, oil-rich cakes to delay it, plain porridge for immediate equal weighting, and steamed grain held behind a separate salt line. The First Hearth contributed one coal no larger than a pea, carried to the center brazier in an iron cup.

Dima had made personal salt cups from thin clay. Each bore the diner’s name on the outside and chosen weight inside. Smashing a cup would break that seat’s salt connection. It would not erase the diner’s existing injury or hunger. It could only stop further sharing and return unsettled burden according to the topology.

Savra read the list. No one answered for another person. One merchant refused after learning that sour millet might return pain quickly. The refusal left a gap; nobody filled it.

For controlled pain, Dima heated a bronze tasting pin over an unbound lamp. “One touch to my left forearm. No broken skin. Pain share only. No heat transfer after three breaths.”

Her right hand already carried the permanent cost of Ember Bridge. She offered the left because the test could not turn history into an inexhaustible resource.

“Local table one,” Savra said. “Dima source, Veyra partner, equal weight. Cook Savra seated at one quarter through the central witness. Tables two through four receive nothing.”

They ate.

Dima pressed the warm pin to her arm. Pain entered Savra as a bright coin beneath the skin. Veyra hissed. Dima’s face eased as their bodies carried the declared fractions. Across the courtyard, the other diners felt nothing.

“Break,” Dima told Veyra.

Veyra smashed her salt cup.

Recoil returned. Dima doubled over as Veyra’s unsettled share came back through the local line. Savra took a smaller fraction through the cook’s seat. The other circles remained untouched.

“That kills a person if a thousand cups break,” Lale said.

“It kills many local sources,” Savra answered. “Again, with the center open.”

Mava caught her wrist. “You said test before answer. State the seat.”

Savra had spent ten years refusing any table large enough to require her. Refusal had protected others from her control. It had also let her imagine that absence carried no weight.

“The central function takes only severance recoil,” she said. “No ordinary hunger, wound, fatigue, or heat enters it while local cups remain whole. Every participant sees the function on their list. Every local cook may refuse the whole topology. The person in the center may sever, but doing so returns recoil to the local circles unless another named volunteer takes the place.”

“And you volunteer,” Mava said.

“Yes.”

“That is not penance.”

“No.” Savra kept her thumb inside the dough’s central hole. “Penance cannot serve as a weighting rule.”

Dima set down the tasting pin. “Call it what it does.”

“Always-open balancing seat.”

Issen repeated the phrase while looking at the witness spoon grooves. “Open to return, not open to unnamed diners.”

“Exactly. One named body. No hidden replacement.”

Mava released Savra’s wrist. “Write yourself into the test. Not the final feast. We decide that after results.”

They rebuilt the four local salt lines. Savra mixed a pinch of black hearth-salt into dough at the center and pressed the witness spoon impression across it. The First Hearth coal glowed blue at the edge. Each local cook copied only the line connecting their circle to the central opening. No cook received another circle’s seat names.

Savra ate from a plain bowl marked with her full name and central function. The millet tasted of mineral ash and old ovens. It carried no power until the others swallowed.

Dima touched the pin to her left forearm again.

Pain divided locally. Veyra broke a fresh cup.

This time the released share struck Savra alone.

Her arm jerked. The sensation carried Dima’s pain without the heat that caused it, exact in state and stripped of origin story. Dima still retained her source share. Veyra kept whatever pain had entered before she severed. Conservation held.

At the second table, a terrace farmer held his hand over steaming water for two breaths, sharing heat with a baker. The baker smashed her cup. Scalding warmth entered Savra’s palm while the farmer’s hand cooled only by the amount distributed. At the third, two runners climbed the courtyard stairs until their legs shook. One severed. Savra’s thighs filled with the returned exertion. The fourth table delayed hunger through oil-rich cakes, then broke both cups together.

That recoil put Savra on her knees.

The courtyard stones came close. Her empty stomach contracted with the hunger of two people who had fasted since dawn for the test. Their local cook kept a declared fraction. Savra took the unsettled return. Nothing came from the other tables except recoil released by choice.

Dima crouched beside her. “Can you continue?”

“Yes.”

“That answer belongs to the seat, not the recipe designer. Do you continue?”

Savra checked her hands. Steady enough. Breath fast but regular. No wound had entered. “I continue for one more break.”

At Mava’s signal, nobody broke another cup. The distinction mattered. A balancing seat did not give the room permission to spend the person inside it merely because capacity remained.

They extinguished the local fires in reverse order. The First Hearth coal returned to its bronze bowl. Savra’s hunger persisted until digestion settled the table, but no delayed share escaped into an unlisted body.

The cooks spent the next four hours trying to break the design.

They removed a local ember before a salt cup broke. Recoil found Savra through the central line. They soured one table and oiled another. Timing changed, conservation did not. They left a diner’s cup unbroken while the diner walked beyond the city gate. Distance made no difference after swallowing, as expected. They placed two people with the same name in different circles and discovered that the cup marks needed hearth identifiers as well as personal names.

Savra corrected the dough map with a barley awn beside every name: hearth, person, weight.

Issen tested the spoken declaration. “If a Namarra baker hears only their local list, how do they know the center exists?”

“Every list begins with it,” Savra said. “Name of balancing volunteer, current weight, severance consequence. Then the local seats.”

“So the one common fact is who pays for departure.”

“Yes.”

Lale stood opposite Savra. “If ten thousand people break cups, you die.”

“Possibly before ten thousand.”

“Then local circles inherit whatever remains.”

“If the central seat fails without severing, the topology loses its route. Recoil returns along the local salt lines. The cups still let people leave before that happens.”

“You cannot promise safety.”

“No.”

“What do you promise?”

“That nobody enters hidden. Nobody stays because a replacement has been coerced. Every burden remains real.”

Lale looked toward the hospital roof. “Teren could have said the last sentence.”

“He could have. Visibility does not make a decision just.” Savra pressed the central dough flat. “It makes the person deciding answerable to those who can see the cost.”

Mava called the political review after sunset. The cooks sat in a circle without bound food. Each held a copy of their local test list. Nobody held the whole design except in memory and in the dough map, which would be cooked and eaten unbound when they finished.

The Ulsor fish cook objected to one central body. “You are rebuilding a master seat.”

“A master sends weight outward,” Dima said. “This one receives only what diners reject.”

“Savra still becomes indispensable.”

Savra answered before anyone defended her. “Then name successors before activation. A chain of volunteers, each able to refuse before taking the seat. If no one takes it, the table remains local and Rovan’s bond may enter where we fail.”

Mava added the succession disclosure. Dima volunteered second, subject to a limit because of her right hand and hospital duties. Veyra volunteered third. Six others added names with stated conditions. Nobody praised them.

Teren attended from outside the circle. Mava had invited his logistical advice, not his vote. “One central kitchen cannot reach one hundred hearths in time,” he said.

“It does not need to,” Savra replied. “We need enough entry fires inside Namarra to overwrite the capital’s three ovens when Rovan activates them.”

“How many?”

“Twenty-four gives us the routes Issen identified. Fewer may work. We do not pretend to know the threshold.”

“Then plan for twenty-four and expect losses.”

Mava’s gaze hardened.

Teren corrected himself. “Plan twenty-four routes. Leave every missing kitchen as a deliberate gap. Do not replace it without council agreement.”

The revision cost him. Savra saw that and did not call it redemption.

Twenty-four merchant kitchens assembled before midnight. Some belonged to the twelve trained cooks; others paired a trained tablewright with a baker, oil factor, caravan cook, or public-oven keeper who knew Namarra’s wards. Each team memorized one local arc and the common opening declaration. Route planners assigned festival gates, grain carts, service alleys, and lighting times.

No written master copy would travel.

Savra divided the recipe into spoken passes. First: source ember, local fire, food, names. Second: hearth-person-weight marks inside every salt cup. Third: central volunteer declared before local seats. Fourth: witness-spoon line copied in black salt but kept outside each circle until imperial activation. Fifth: any smashed cup severs its owner and sends unsettled recoil to the always-open balancing seat.

Teams recited until another kitchen could correct them. Issen introduced small errors on purpose: a missing cook’s seat, an unnamed successor, oil added before souring, a central line drawn through rather than beside local salt. Every error had to be identified by someone who did not belong to that kitchen.

Memorizing words did not prove that hands could reproduce the recipe. Savra sent the teams to separate corners of Tal Jes with equal packets of millet flour, black salt, plain salt, and oil. Each had to build its arc on a cold hearth while a witness from another route watched without helping. The fish cooks drew salt by pinching from the bowl. Dima poured hers through a narrow horn because her right fingers could not hold a pinch. A caravan baker used the squared edge of a flour board. Different hands produced different lines; only placement and declared meaning had to match.

The seventh team failed when its oil factor put the central line beneath the local ring. In a live hearth, the arrangement would have made the balancing volunteer a source for ordinary local burden rather than a recipient of severance recoil. Savra scraped the whole diagram away.

“Correct the line,” the oil factor said.

“Build the kitchen again.”

“We know the error.”

“You know this error. Fatigue will make another.” Savra returned the packet unopened. “If a witness catches a central-line fault, extinguish, discard the food, and start with cold vessels. Nobody repairs topology over a live ember.”

The delay cost them two hours and one measure of flour. Teren, observing for the route council, noted the loss without asking her to accept a quicker correction.

At the northern cistern, the fourteenth team discovered that spoken declarations changed meaning across provincial dialects. An Ulsor phrase for an unused bowl sounded in Tal Jes like a diner who had not yet eaten. Issen replaced it with physical terms: cup whole, cup broken, seat receiving, seat closed. Each team had to point while speaking. Translation could vary; the visible state could not.

Veyra tested route memory by moving markers on the ground. “Your festival gate closes. Your assigned oven has imperial guards. Your second cook does not arrive. What remains?”

The teams answered separately.

“Route closes, kitchen stays dark.”

“No substitute cook from the street.”

“Carry the ember onward only if the next named kitchen accepts it.”

“Leave a deliberate gap.”

One public-oven keeper offered to split her team if another disappeared. Mava refused the proposal. Splitting would create a twenty-fifth kitchen that had never rehearsed its local arc and might use half-heard names. The network’s strength had to include the ability to remain incomplete.

Near midnight, Dima placed a broken practice cup before each group. “Tell me what the person who breaks this owes you.”

Several answers came quickly: warning, reason, time to brace. Dima shook her head.

“They owe none of that. If they can warn us, warning helps. If they cannot speak, the break still counts. You volunteered for recoil with open eyes. Do not turn your preparation into their obligation.”

Savra watched the correction settle uneasily. A recipe could disclose a right and still grow a custom that punished its use. No salt line could prevent that. The witnesses would have to.

At dawn, all twenty-four teams recited without correction.

Savra put the dough map into the largest unbound oven. Heat swelled it around the chickpeas, then cracked the central hole. People ate the bread with lentils and no seal.

Only the witness spoon impression remained as a portable master. Savra placed it on the hearthstone.

Mava said, “Once broken, we cannot copy the topology again.”

“Twenty-four kitchens can.”

“People forget.”

“Then no object should let one person correct them from above.”

Dima held the impression steady with the heel of her right palm. Savra used a borrowed cleaver. Her own knives remained in Mava’s custody.

One blow split the copied spoon bowl. A second broke the handle grooves. They distributed the fragments among four cold salt jars, where no live ash could answer them.

Behind Savra, the First Hearth burned under civic guard. Before her, twenty-four merchant kitchens carried the recipe in separate memories, each with a route and the authority to leave a gap.

Savra washed the borrowed cleaver. Hunger from the test tightened through her again, a delayed wave from the oil-rich cakes. She gripped the edge of the basin until it passed.

Mava laid the final volunteer list beside her hand. “You occupy the first balancing seat if the kitchens affirm at Namarra.”

“If they affirm.”

“No command title. No restored office.”

“No.”

Outside, merchant teams began loading flour, salt cups, oil jars, and twenty-four covered ember pots for the road. Each pot carried one visible route mark and no authority beyond its named kitchen.

Chapter 23 — The Man Who Called Hunger Peace

Rovan tested the west public oven by laying his palm against its outer brick.

Heat entered evenly from the firebox. No cold seam marked a cracked flue. Inside, two hundred white loaves rose in standardized pans, each stamped with the measure of Namarra and the grain oath of its ward. Bakers moved around him with lowered eyes. Their work continued because cities expected bread even when officials brought soldiers into kitchens.

“Open the fourth damper,” Rovan said.

The oven master obeyed. Flame drew harder beneath the stone deck.

“The Equinox batch will scorch at that draft,” she said.

“It will carry three provincial oils. The added fat slows the crust.”

“The old recipe used the First Hearth to settle them.”

Rovan removed his hand. Soot marked his palm in a gray square. “The old recipe has changed.”

He had known for four days that the brazier had vanished from the Hall. Warden Pel’s report described pepper dust, a forged burn, merchant accomplices, and the First Taster’s tasting chain left on the inspection-court floor. Riders had recovered the cedar litter and a decoy basket. They had not recovered Issen Mar.

At dawn, a signal from Tal Jes confirmed that a covered bronze fire had entered the terrace city. Rovan no longer had the comfort of imagining the thieves had extinguished what they stole.

He walked around the west oven while clerks read the revised preparation. Ninety-seven provincial hearths still carried sealed imperial embers. Three capital civic ovens supplied the missing anchors, making one hundred target fires. Under the intended design, the First Hearth would have overwritten differences in their nested weightings and made the whole table answer one central recipe. Without it, the three Namarra ovens had to force a common current through standardized loaves and simultaneous lighting.

The bond would engage roughly. Some provinces would receive burden before others. Recoil would rise wherever local embers resisted. The hidden wards had sufficient capacity if the activation did not lose more than eight hearths.

“Seven,” corrected the clerk beside him.

Rovan looked at the wax calculation. “You have not counted Kelar.”

“The refugee seal remains active.”

“Issen carried the list. Assume Savra knows its purpose.”

The clerk changed the figure to eight.

A design that required optimism had already failed. Rovan did not call the new plan elegant. Elegance belonged to demonstrations and commemorative feasts. States survived through what remained functional after theft.

“Proceed with all three ovens,” he said. “Move first lighting forward by one watch. The provincial kitchens will follow the capital signal.”

The oven master’s hands stopped over a tray. “The festival gates will still be admitting merchants.”

“Then the gates will close.”

Rovan left before she could turn concern into objection.

The west ward had kept one old famine wall behind its public granary. New plaster ended at a jagged vertical line, exposing mud brick darkened by cooking smoke. As a boy, Rovan had waited against that wall with a clay ration disk clenched in both hands. The disk promised one cup of millet. The granary held grain. The official measure said the ward had enough for everyone if distribution remained equal.

By the sixth day, private courtyards had begun baking at night.

He remembered the smell more clearly than faces. Sesame oil warmed behind closed shutters. Barley crust charred on a household stone.
