A family three doors away fed its servants while the public line received half cups because reserve figures had changed. Hunger made every hidden meal an accusation. People struck one another at the pumps, then at the granary doors, then over a dead mule before its blood cooled.

His mother kept two handfuls of lentils sewn into a cushion. She opened it on the seventh night. Rovan watched her divide the lentils between him and his younger cousin while an old aunt turned her face toward the wall. Nobody called the division cruel. It preserved the children most likely to live.

The cousin died anyway after drinking canal water.

On the ninth day, soldiers opened a merchant cellar and found enough grain to feed the ward for three days. The merchant had kept it dry behind false brick while public measures reported nothing. A crowd beat him to death before the first sack reached the street.

No account of Rovan’s childhood famine recorded what the grain tasted like when it finally came. He remembered weevils crushed into meal, smoke from the merchant’s house, and the shame of chewing while bodies remained beside the cellar door.

He had drawn the wrong lesson with perfect conviction: private sensation made private morality. If every full stomach carried a fraction of every empty one, no household could close its shutters. If every province felt the wound it permitted elsewhere, war would cease before a spear moved.

Later study taught him that hearthbinding could enact the conclusion. Government taught him that nobody surrendered private advantage voluntarily at continental scale. Someone had to set the weights.

The boy at the famine wall had not imagined becoming that someone. The man crossing Namarra’s ward square considered the distinction sentimental.

His carriage waited by the old granary. Rovan chose to walk. Festival workers hung red canopies across the avenue. Potters stacked thousands of thin salt cups at public tables, believing them ceremonial vessels for the Equinox toast. Hall agents would fill those cups after activation so no one could break a seat before the bond settled. The design protected the table from panic.

It also protected Rovan from refusal.

He entered the northern infirmary through its laundry court. The public wards occupied the sunward rooms. His inspectors took him down two levels, beyond storerooms of vinegar, linen, and burial salt. A bronze seal closed the lowest passage.

“Capacity?” Rovan asked.

“Six hundred twelve bodies fed and marked,” said the ward superintendent. “Another eighty expected from debt court tonight.”

“Condition?”

“Stable enough for transfer. We excluded the dying.”

The hidden sink wards smelled of boiled barley and damp stone. Beds stood in rows beneath low vaults. Some held debt prisoners, some army invalids, some people collected from streets under fever ordinances. All had eaten grain-oath porridge for three days. Clay tags at their beds listed categories rather than names: reserve breath, heat sink, hunger depth, pain dispersal.

Rovan stopped at a woman whose left leg ended below the knee. “Why pain dispersal?”

The superintendent consulted his board. “Old cavalry wound. High tolerance. No family petitioner.”

“Tolerance does not increase capacity. It delays reporting.”

“I can move her to heat.”

“Move her to ordinary hunger. She has less tissue to carry thermal injury.”

The woman watched them as if they discussed a vessel. Rovan met her gaze.

“You will receive hunger from the southern wards,” he said. “Your own meals continue.”

“Can I say no?”

The superintendent shifted.

Rovan did not lie. “No.”

“Then why tell me?”

“Because concealment adds no mercy.”

She laughed once. “You built this room under the laundry.”

Her answer followed him to the next row.

The ward superintendent had arranged the rooms by bodily state. Hunger reserves received thin barley porridge at dawn and a measured lentil meal at dusk, enough to keep them stable while preserving capacity to carry the festival’s expected want. Heat sinks slept under damp linen with braziers removed. Breath reserves occupied the driest vault, where attendants counted respirations through glass timers. Pain dispersal took the largest chamber and the highest dose of poppy milk, although intoxication itself could pass through an active table if the seal admitted it.

Rovan stopped an attendant before she poured a second cup for a man tagged pain dispersal. “One dose only after first lighting. If he enters dulled, the table will spread the drug state as well as pain.”

“He has been crying since morning.”

“Then sit with him.”

“I have forty beds.”

“Assign another attendant.”

The superintendent consulted her tally. “We have no reserve staff below.”

Rovan looked along the row. Administrative design had reached its physical edge: one pair of hands, forty beds, a rule that required an unavailable second person. He could transfer an attendant from the public ward, reducing care there; bring a guard down, though guards were not trained for the work; or let the man cry.

“Move two record clerks from intake,” he said. “Boards wait. Bodies do not.”

The superintendent marked the order. It would improve the night for perhaps eight people. Rovan accepted the improvement without mistaking it for structural change. In his own account, this honesty distinguished him from officials who called every local mercy justice.

A service corridor connected the wards to an underground kitchen. Three cauldrons held barley at different thicknesses. Kitchen workers weighed salt on Hall scales and pressed each bowl onto the appropriate bed tag. The system needed food to carry ash signatures and grain oaths; labels alone could bind nothing. Rovan checked the storage jars and found enough grain for two more days.

“What happens on the third?” he asked.

“Great Table distributions should reduce capital stores needed by then,” said the kitchen overseer.

“Should.”

“The provisioning plan assumes provincial equalization.”

Rovan opened a lentil jar. Weevils moved beneath the top layer. “Sift these. Increase oil by one finger in the hunger ward after activation. If the southern provinces carry less scarcity than projected, reduce the ward weight before reducing food.”

A sink ward could not create hunger. It could only receive contractions, weakness, nausea, and the bodily timing of deprivation from joined diners. Feeding its occupants did not nullify incoming state, but starving them first would reduce their ability to survive the transfer. The cruelty had to remain provisioned in order to function.

In the breath chamber, a prisoner raised his hand. “My tag says two measures. The board at intake said one.”

Rovan took the clay tag. A fresh groove crossed the back, doubling the weight.

“Who changed this?”

No attendant answered.

The superintendent found the intake board. One measure remained written beside the man’s number. A lower clerk had increased tags to meet capacity after sixty army invalids were marked for removal.

Rovan snapped the tag in two. “Restore every intake weight. Find the clerk who changed them.”

“For discipline?”

“For testimony. Then reduce the activation ceiling by the missing capacity.”

The prisoner watched him. “You think keeping the first wrong makes you clean of the second.”

“No,” Rovan said. “I think unmeasured changes make any system indistinguishable from appetite.”

“And measured ones?”

“Can be stopped at a limit.”

The man looked at the locked door. Rovan continued before the obvious answer became speech.

At the kitchen exit, he found a stack of blank bed tags already cut. Enough for three hundred more bodies. He had authorized reserve material for emergency displacement, not intake. The superintendent said debt court expected only eighty.

“Burn the blanks,” he said.

“Provisioner?”

“If the activation runs short, an empty tag will become an invitation to fill it.”

“You ordered the gates locked. There will be arrests.”

“Arrest does not create a sink seat without my seal.”

Rovan signed the destruction order and watched a clerk carry the tags toward the kitchen fire. He did not notice that he had framed restraint as the persistence of his personal control. He saw only a boundary held against lesser officials.

Official plans called these wards balancing reserves. Savra had called them cold seats when she first discovered their use in the Nine-Salt campaign. The older term had spread among rebels because it described what the notation tried to remove: living bodies kept outside the visible warmth of the meal.

Rovan had ordered names preserved in sealed ledgers. He had mandated minimum rations, medical inspection, and exclusion of children after the first trials. Each reform reduced arbitrary harm. None changed the absence of choice.

“Grand Provisioner.” The superintendent pointed to a boy on a lower cot.

Not a child, Rovan saw after looking twice. A small adult with scarred cheeks and a baker’s burn along one forearm.

“Tam Vesso,” the superintendent said. “Festival cook from the south ward. Arrested copying oven schedules for merchants. Execution set at dusk.”

Tam sat up. “You moved it from tomorrow.”

“Gate security,” said the superintendent.

Rovan examined the burn. Old, cleanly healed. “You worked a public oven.”

“Third south.”

“Your team?”

“Arrested or gone.”

“You know its damper faults.”

Tam looked at him with the deliberate blankness of someone refusing to offer useful fear.

“The south oven must carry sour millet at first lighting,” Rovan said. “Its rear deck runs cool. You will supervise the corrected draft.”

“And after?”

“Your execution will be stayed pending review.”

The superintendent said, “Provisioner, the charge carries no review.”

“It does now.”

Tam’s mouth tightened. “You need a cook, so you call the rope a review.”

“I need thousands fed through an oven you know. You need time in which outcomes may change. Neither need requires us to decorate the exchange.”

“If I refuse?”

“The sentence proceeds.”

Coercion stated plainly remained coercion. Rovan knew that. He also knew Tam possessed a choice between two real conditions, while the bodies around them possessed none. Degrees mattered in provisioning even when moral language preferred clean divisions.

Tam swung his feet to the floor. “I supervise the oven. No binding my old crew through my oath.”

“Agreed. Their marks are already void under arrest.”

“I taste the first batch.”

“The Hall supplies a taster.”

“I taste it or hang.”

Rovan considered. Tam might poison the batch, but tasting first would make that difficult. “Accepted.”

The superintendent cut the bed cord.

Rovan paused at the last ward, where sixty soldiers slept after receiving wounds at Halvek without visible cuts. Their bodies had carried exported pain after the Halvek field table faltered. The empire’s own system had made them evidence against its claim of controlled distribution.

“Move these soldiers aboveground,” he said.

“They add four hundred pain shares.”

“They have already carried assigned burden. Use the debt intake.”

One act of limit did not cleanse the ward. It reflected Rovan’s preference for accounts that could still distinguish service from seizure. He left before the soldiers woke.

At the Hall of Measures, the festival security council waited around a map weighted with grain scoops. Reports placed merchant convoys at all six gates. Twenty-four unusual kitchen licenses had appeared in the previous two days, though some might represent duplicated names or ordinary festival growth. Issen’s route knowledge made every service entrance suspect.

“Close the festival gates at sunset,” Rovan said. “Inspect all live embers against Hall registries. Detain unlicensed tablewrights. Ordinary cooks continue service under guard.”

A commander touched the north gate marker. “Closing traps thirty thousand visitors inside.”

“It also traps any coalition kitchens.”

“If the crowd panics?”

“Feed them. The bond will equalize hunger after first lighting.”

“Until then?”

Rovan moved three reserve wagons to the public squares. “Open these stores at gate closure. Standard half ration, no hearthseal before the Equinox batch.”

The council recorded the order. He did not call soldiers to clear the tables. A festival remained useful only while people believed they attended a feast.

Night gathered over Namarra as workers prepared the Hall pavilion. The Great Table curved around a central cauldron broad enough for six cooks to stir at once. One hundred narrow salt channels ran outward across the floor toward symbolic provincial settings. Beneath those channels, hidden bronze conductors joined the real oven seals.

Rovan inspected his place.

A high-backed chair stood on a stone dais, with writing surface, signal levers, and a speaking horn connected to the oven courtyards. Its front legs touched the outer salt ring. Its rear legs did not.

“Move it back one handspan,” he told an attendant.

The attendant obeyed. Now the entire chair stood outside the salt line.

From there Rovan could monitor weighting changes without receiving the Great Table’s hunger, heat, wounds, or panic. Feastwrights had argued that command required an unburdened body. He had accepted the logic because a hand on the measures needed steadiness.

Tam Vesso, brought under guard to inspect the south-oven signal, looked at the chair. “Equal hunger?”

“Equal distribution among the joined provinces.”

“Not to you.”

“The person adjusting the weights cannot be impaired by them.”

Tam gave him the same laugh as the woman in the buried ward. “Convenient work.”

Rovan rested one hand on the chair back. He could have ordered the cook removed. Instead, he looked along the salt channels toward one hundred empty settings. Tomorrow those lines would carry enough shared pain to make invasion irrational, rebellion physically costly, and hidden plenty impossible.

His own exemption appeared temporary against that scale, a technical necessity rather than private advantage. The explanation fit every measure he trusted.

“Take him to the south oven,” Rovan said.

Guards led Tam away. In the squares beyond the Hall, gate bells began to ring. Iron leaves closed one by one across the festival roads, sealing merchants, cooks, pilgrims, and coalition agents inside Namarra.

Rovan sat beyond the salt and ordered the first oil warmed. An attendant set a plain cup of water at his right hand, outside every channel. He drank while bakers below turned loaves meant to join thousands of bodies. No borrowed hunger tightened his stomach. No sink prisoner’s breath entered his chest. The steadiness he had reserved for command felt, to him, like evidence that the system still possessed a mind.

Chapter 24 — Merchants at the Festival Gates

Teren’s grain cart reached Namarra after the gates had closed.

Iron leaves met across the southern arch, studded with bronze bosses and guarded by two ranks of soldiers. Outside waited six hundred festival merchants whose licenses had become worthless between one mile marker and the next. Goats bawled inside wicker pens. Oil jars warmed under noon sun. Drivers argued that fruit would rot, fish would sour, and pilgrims would riot if the city kept its own feast beyond locked walls.

Teren stood on his bad ankle beside thirty sacks of red millet and did not look at the covered ember pot beneath the driver’s bench.

Twenty-four kitchens approached Namarra by six roads. None carried a complete list of the others. Teren knew their planned gates, staging wards, courier phrases, and latest acceptable arrival. Mava’s route council had granted him coordination because he could count carts and travel time. It had not restored command. Every merchant cook retained the right to turn back.

At the arch, a Hall inspector raised a speaking trumpet. “Only contracted provisions enter. Present manifests by ward seal. Unlicensed live fire will be confiscated.”

Veyra stood at the front of Teren’s convoy in a clean oil factor’s robe. Dust had turned its hem the color of old blood. “My contract supplies the Hall’s west tables,” she called. “Your city owes spoilage if you refuse delivery.”

“Present the tablet.”

She held up a clay manifest fired three months earlier. The contract had once covered two jars of sesame oil. Merchant hands had altered none of its letters. Behind it, however, waited legitimate supplemental orders purchased from bakers who feared the lockdown would empty their stores. The coalition had not forged every fact. It had used the city’s appetite to make facts arrive in a dangerous arrangement.

The gate opened one person’s width.

Inspectors searched the lead cart. They pierced oil-jar seals, dug rods into flour sacks, and checked every axle box. One soldier found the packet of thin clay salt cups.

“Festival ware?”

“Twenty dozen,” Veyra said. “West Hall annex.”

He picked up a cup. The outside carried no name yet. Hearth-person-weight marks would be made only when local diners chose seats. “Why so thin?”

“Because thick cups cost more to break.”

The soldier smiled despite himself and put it back.

A second inspector rolled a registry table into the lane. On it stood shallow dishes of ash labeled by licensed public ovens. Every incoming live fire had to be touched with vinegar steam and compared for color, clumping, and oil residue. The method could identify an unregistered hearth signature. It could not detect a cold pot wrapped beneath grain unless guards uncovered it.

Two carts ahead, a caravan cook declared a lawful brazier used to warm infant food. Her registry matched a canal tollhouse, not any Namarra oven. The inspector confiscated the coal and returned the empty bronze cup. She accepted the loss without looking toward Teren. Her coalition pot traveled separately inside a bale of felt on another team’s contracted wagon; the declared brazier had existed only to satisfy the expectation that a caravan carried fire.

Teren had opposed the sacrifice during planning. One additional live coal seemed useful. The cook had asked whether usefulness gave him custody of her tollhouse ember. He had answered no and still disliked the subtraction. Watching her reclaim the cold cup reminded him that a plan could include loss chosen by the owner rather than loss he ordered for everyone.

At the gate scale, soldiers weighed Veyra’s oil wagon. The axle came up nine measures heavier than its manifest. The hidden salt cups accounted for only two.

“Unload it,” the officer said.

Veyra climbed down. “Your scale pan holds rainwater.”

The day had not seen rain.

“Unload.”

She removed the first jar and set it on the paving. Oil factors behind her began calling prices and spoilage clauses. Fish merchants joined them. Noise tightened the inspection lane until the officer ordered spears lowered.

Teren studied the balance frame. One rear foot rested on a wedge of broken pottery, tilting the crossbeam toward the wagon side. Whether accident or extortion, the false weight could be proved without exposing any cargo.

“Lift the rear foot,” he said.

The officer turned. “Who spoke?”

Teren pointed with an open hand. “Your frame stands on a shard. Remove it or charge the city nine measures on every cart.”

A gate clerk crouched, pulled out the pottery, and reset the pan. The wagon balanced within one measure. Merchants laughed with the cruelty of people briefly spared a search.

The officer waved Veyra through but marked her manifest with a red slash for inspection at the west annex. The coalition’s bakery transfer now had to occur before a second inspection, under workers whose names the Hall could trace.

Teren’s cart came third. Its grain sacks contained grain. Concealment depended on placement rather than false contents. Two sacks rested across a shallow wooden frame under which lay the coalition’s covered ember pot, black salt wrapped in waxed cloth, and a broad mixing bowl. An inspector would find them by unloading the cart. The gate line had more than a hundred contracted wagons and three hours before heat spoiled the fish.

“Open six,” the inspector ordered.

Teren cut the ties on the six sacks marked with blue millet stems. Real grain poured into the sampling bowls. He had placed those sacks where an efficient search would choose them: corners, center, one near the axle.

The inspector plunged a hand into the fifth. “Tal Jes millet.”

“Yes.”

“Rebel city.”

“Also a city that sells grain. The Hall’s standard does not forbid profit.”

“You sound educated for a carter.”

“I count loads.”

A horn sounded inside the arch. Soldiers dragged two bakers from a handcart entering through the pedestrian channel. One had live ash in a stoppered cup. Inspectors converged on them.

Teren kept his hands visible.

The gate officer stamped Veyra’s manifest. “West annex, direct route. Any diversion voids entry.”

“Then clear your festival pilgrims from my road,” she said.

Their carts entered Namarra.

Inside the walls, closure had made the avenues denser rather than empty. Visitors already admitted could not leave. Public squares filled with people demanding food from reserve wagons. Soldiers stood at intersections checking kitchen licenses. Festival banners hung above ordinary panic, red cloth snapping over arguments about half rations.

Veyra led the convoy west for two streets, then stopped beside a contracted bakery. Workers rolled out replacement carts carrying white flour to the Hall. Under awnings, loads changed without any wagon standing empty long enough to invite suspicion.

The ember pot from Teren’s cart went into a baker’s cooling basket. Salt cups entered crates of festival pottery. Black salt passed inside a sealed jar labeled burnt cumin. The red millet continued to the west annex as the manifest promised. Every concealed item moved through hands already assigned to one kitchen.

Teren took a flour cart north with no visible weapon. His old left ankle caught on a paving stone. Pain rose through the joint, cleanly his own. At Tal Jes he had let other bodies carry what he chose to hide. Namarra required him to walk without such relief and without calling endurance proof of anything.

The first staging court occupied an inn kitchen near the dyers’ ward. Five route couriers waited beside cold ovens. Each wore a strip of uncolored cloth tied differently: wrist, throat, basket handle, sandal. The signs reported arrivals, not orders.

Teren laid wooden counters on a flour board. “South gate: kitchens one, four, and nine entered. Kitchen seventeen turned east after inspection closed its manifest lane. No confirmation from fourteen.”

A courier placed counters for the canal gate. Another reported three teams through the north cattle arch before closure. Nobody asked whether seventeen should return. Its cook would decide from the alternate route plan.

Lale entered carrying a basket of pickled mustard stems.

Teren knew her gait before he saw her face. She had entered through the river postern with an Eren Ford oven contract and spent four hours waiting under guard while inspectors matched her communal seal to imperial records.

“You got through,” he said.

“So did the ember.” She set the basket down. “Team six has a cellar oven in the rope ward. The owner accepted the local list and refused the successor position.”

“Then the successor line passes to eight.”

“Already recorded.”

Their old working language returned without warmth. At Eren Ford, they had coordinated flood harvests this way: one reported a fact, the other changed the tally. Teren had once mistaken efficiency between siblings for permission that extended everywhere.

“How is your shoulder?” he asked.

“Blister closed.”

“I have dressing fat.”

“The kitchen has it.” Lale looked at the counters. “Mava said you coordinate arrivals only.”

“Yes.”

“Then do that.”

He moved no team marker without a courier phrase. The limit felt like a rope around his hand. It also kept twenty-four separate kitchens from becoming the extension of his fear.

By midafternoon, twenty-one teams had entered. Kitchen seventeen arrived through an undertaker’s provisions lane after its original gate refused live ember. The cook sent the prescribed message: route changed, list unchanged. Kitchen fourteen remained silent.

Issen came to the inn kitchen wearing a laundry worker’s gray tunic. The tasting-chain scar still crossed their neck, a red line rubbed raw by dust.

“The Hall’s service court admits washing carts until evening,” they said. “Rovan has moved first lighting ahead one watch. South and west ovens already warm carrier oil.”

“How close can you get?” Lale asked.

“To the Hall cauldron if Pel has not changed the lower stair guard.”

“And if he has?” Teren asked.

“I remain outside it.”

No brave promise. No replacement plan requiring another body.

Issen carried no live ember. Their route passed through the Hall because they knew tasting signals and sink-ward notation, not because the final table needed them at any magical seat. Dima’s kitchen would establish the First Hearth in a spice warehouse two courts away. Savra remained there with the balancing-seat bowl under civic witnesses.

Teren checked the sun. “Laundry carts leave in twelve minutes.”

Issen took one pickled mustard stem from Lale’s basket and ate it. “Unbound?”

“Everything in this room,” Lale said.

“Good.” They bowed, not as First Taster but in thanks, and went.

The twenty-two confirmed kitchens separated before the late bells. No procession connected them. Teams left in pairs carrying ordinary goods: lentil flour, fish baskets, oil jars, festival cups, charcoal, folded awnings. Couriers took different alleys and never carried more than one route report.

Teren walked the outer circuit with Veyra, checking only physical placement. At the pottery ward, an ember pot rested behind a kiln whose own fire would disguise its heat. In the canal quarter, a cook stored black salt inside a brine crock above flood level. Near the old famine wall, a public baker had placed salt cups among drinking vessels and posted the local seat declaration beneath a price list.

The baker had written nine names. An earlier plan listed eleven.

“Two refused after the gates shut,” she said before Teren asked. “They have children outside the walls. They will not take a burden that may keep them from the gate when it opens.”

“Does the kitchen still activate?”

“With nine. Weight remains equal. Central line unchanged.”

Veyra checked the dough measure against the smaller list. “Reduce the batch. Food left over after first service stays unsealed.”

The baker crossed out two bowl marks but did not scrape away the names. She wrote refused before eating beside each, preserving the decision rather than making the smaller table look as though it had always been intended.

In a rope-maker’s cellar, kitchen six had an opposite problem. Four inn workers asked to join after learning the plan. Lale had refused them because the team had not rehearsed their names or weights. The workers now carried water, knowing they could support the kitchen without entering its magic.

“Could the local cook add them safely?” Teren asked.

Lale answered, “Possibly.”

“Then why not?”

“Because possible under a locked gate is how a seat becomes necessary before anyone admits it.”

He recorded no disagreement. Her judgment controlled the kitchen.

At the eleventh site, a cracked oven flue forced the team to move from a sesame shop to a courtyard laundry. The local tablewright drew the same salt arc on a portable clay hearth, then waited while each diner inspected it. A change of location did not change the seat list. A change of origin ember would have, so they carried the shop coal in a lidded pan and left the laundry fire unbound.

The sixteenth site had no private room. Its cooks worked at a festival stall between two imperial ration tables. They hid nothing except the black salt packet, which remained sealed until activation. Their declared diners stood in the same public line as everyone else but would receive marked cups and stamped chickpea cakes. Proximity would not bind the crowd. Swallowed food and the named seat list would.

Teren checked firewood at each stop. Twenty-three kitchens required enough dry fuel to stay lit through first service and long enough for digestion to settle, perhaps six hours under festival conditions. Teams had cached wood in oven yards, but the gate closure increased ordinary demand. At current burn, seven sites would run short before midnight.

He gave the quantity to Veyra, not an order.

She rerouted two legitimate charcoal deliveries and asked three kitchens whether they would accept fig wood, which burned cooler and needed wider dampers. One refused because its recipe depended on rapid souring over steady heat. The refusal remained. The other two changed fuel and reheated test water before accepting the delivery.

No central bond moved those decisions. Couriers, questions, and time carried them.

Each kitchen had selected diners from its own workers and merchant allies. Festival crowds had not consented and were not listed. The counter-recipe could enter Rovan’s topology through twenty-four fires without binding everyone nearby. Food carried the table only to people who swallowed from those kitchens.

At the eighteenth placement, a courier ran up with no cloth visible. She had torn it away, the signal for compromised route.

“Kitchen fourteen reached the bronze-workers’ court,” she said. “Pot placed in the south wall oven. Before the cook drew the salt line, Hall agents entered. Four arrested.”

“Ember seized?” Veyra asked.

“Not found yet. Hidden behind the damper brick.”

“Can another team light it?” Teren asked.

The courier looked at him. “No other team knows fourteen’s local arc.”

He knew the answer. His mind still produced alternatives: send half of kitchen thirteen, combine lists, pull a trained cook from the First Hearth warehouse, teach someone from memory. Every option treated preparation as transferable authority.

“The fire remains dark,” he said.

Veyra removed the fourteenth counter from her board and left its square empty. “Deliberate gap.”

“Can we get the cooks out?”

“Separate operation,” she said. “No promise before activation.”

Teren accepted both limits.

A pottery wagon brought confirmation from kitchens twenty-three and twenty-four near dusk. All planned ember pots now lay inside Namarra: twenty-three in accessible kitchens, one hidden behind the damper of an arrested team. Placement did not equal activation. The coalition possessed twenty-three hands able to draw their own salt lines.

They regrouped in the spice warehouse after dark. The First Hearth sat inside a ring of cold provincial salts. Savra knelt at a workbench shaping the central bowl. Dima checked crates of personal cups using the heel of her right hand to hold each stack while her left counted.

Mava’s civic witnesses recorded the route board. Teren reported without interpretation.

“Twenty-four fires placed. Twenty-three kitchens active. Team fourteen arrested before drawing its line. Ember pot remains hidden and dark.”

Mava asked, “Recommendation?”

“Leave the gap.”

“Rescue?”

“After the bond breaks or if the local council detaches people who were never assigned to a kitchen fire.”

Lale stood across the table. She did not praise the answer. She marked the empty square on her copy.

A knock sounded from the courtyard: two, pause, one. Issen’s signal. They had entered the Hall of Measures.

Savra looked at the route board. “Rovan moved the lighting.”

“How early?” Teren asked.

“One watch, perhaps more.”

The cold kitchen had no command tally. Twenty-three cooks stated their readiness in turn. Each could still refuse at the final declaration. The fourteenth square remained bare.

Teren counted the replies because counting had been assigned to him. When the last kitchen answered, he set aside one counter rather than pretending the board held twenty-four.

Beyond the shuttered warehouse, the festival gates locked another night’s worth of hungry people inside Namarra. Kitchen couriers went back into those streets carrying no central command, only twenty-three separate confirmations and one openly empty square. Teren watched them leave in different directions. His work ended where each local list began. He could count the fires that remained; he could not spend a missing team by assigning somebody else to become it.

Chapter 25 — The Night Before the Great Table

Savra found a hairline crack in the seventeenth salt cup and broke it before anyone could mistake it for a choice.

The thin clay split between her thumbs. Dima swept the pieces into a discard bowl with her left hand. Her right palm held the next stack against the worktable while she tapped each rim with a wooden spoon. Sound separated whole cups from flawed ones: a clear note for sound clay, a dull click for hidden fracture.

The spice warehouse kitchen had gone cold. No live ember remained except the First Hearth under its copper hood in the adjoining court, surrounded by civic witnesses. Twenty-three merchant kitchens waited across Namarra. Their fires stayed banked, their black salt sealed, and their local food unbound.

“How many spare?” Savra asked.

“Forty-one sound,” Dima said. “Eleven rejected. We distribute two per kitchen and keep none here beyond our list.”

“One each leaves eighteen.”

“I know what subtraction does.” Dima tapped another cup. Clear. “Two each. A cup broken by accident before eating should not force a diner to leave because we planned exactly.”

Savra accepted the correction. Planning spare vessels did not create replacement seats. It gave the same person another chance to mark a choice.

At the long flour table, Mava conducted the final conference. Couriers represented kitchens that could not risk sending cooks. Each carried a cold clay tile showing only local readiness, not names. The actual lists remained at each fire.

Mava called the routes by number.

“Kitchen one?”

“Ten seats, cook included. Two conditional successors for the central function. West grain annex.”

“Kitchen two?”

“Seven seats. One refusal recorded. Canal laundry.”

The answers continued. Some teams had fewer diners than at Tal Jes. One had changed fuel. Three had reduced batches because locked gates cut ingredient deliveries. Kitchen fourteen received no call. Mava left a full breath of silence at its place and moved to fifteen.

No one offered to fill the gap.

Savra listened for the errors fatigue encouraged. One courier said when the cup breaks rather than if. She made him repeat the declaration until departure remained possible, not expected. Another reported the balancing seat after local names. Issen corrected the order through a message smuggled from the Hall: central cost must be declared first.

Every kitchen affirmed three choices separately. The cook accepted a seat in their own local table. Each diner knew that a smashed cup would sever them and route unsettled burden toward the named central volunteer. The team accepted darkness if its own line failed.

Then Mava asked the fourth question. “If Savra Kel cannot continue, does your kitchen accept the first successor named on its list?”

Twenty-three answers returned. Two said no and chose local severance instead. One accepted Dima but no further successor. The others accepted the sequence with different limits.

A universal table would have called those differences defects. Their plan depended on keeping them.

Dima finished the last cup and flexed her right fingers. Nothing moved except the thumb. “All sound.”

“You should not take second position,” Savra said quietly.

“Because of my hand?”

“Because you have spent two days treating Tal Jes and crossed Namarra under guard. Fatigue enters the seat.”

“It enters yours too.”

“I am first.”

“That is sequence, not capacity.” Dima set the spoon down. “My condition remains: if recoil interferes with my ability to manage severance, I break my cup. Veyra chooses whether to take third. You do not preserve me by revising what I volunteered after the lists left.”

Savra looked at the broad-handled knife in Dima’s apron. “Agreed.”

The word did not ease her.

They packed the sound cups in chaff and divided them by kitchen. Each pair received one marked cloth: white for a cup broken before eating, red for a deliberate severance after swallowing. The cloth did not carry magic. It let a local witness record why a vessel disappeared without demanding an explanation from the diner.

At kitchen eight, the courier reported that imperial inspectors had ordered all festival pottery moved to public tables. Dima replaced their cups with thin lamp bowls from the dye quarter. She struck each one, discarded four with dull notes, and showed the courier how to write the hearth-person-weight marks around the wick groove.

“Will breaking a lamp bowl count?” he asked.

“The salt line recognizes the named vessel, not its trade,” Savra said. “But the diner must see which bowl holds the seat. No swapping after service.”

Kitchen nineteen had only thick drinking cups that would not shatter under an injured hand. Dima wrapped one in cloth and pushed it from the table with her right palm. It hit the floor, rolled, and stayed whole.

“Unacceptable,” she said.

“We can score the clay,” suggested the courier.

“A score may split while serving.” Dima took two of the spare thin cups. “These go to nineteen. Their local list has six diners, so one spare remains here.”

Savra noticed Dima had kept count without moving her lips. Injury had changed her work, not erased it. The broad-handled scoop, rim tapping, and table-edge grip all cost time. Their plan had to include that time rather than praise adaptation while scheduling as though her right hand still pinched and turned.

A runner from kitchen three arrived breathless. One diner wanted to revise from a full share to half after hearing the Hall had moved first lighting.

“Has she eaten?” Mava asked.

“No.”

“Then the local cook redraws the weight in her cup and reads the whole list again. Nobody votes on her reduction.”

“What if the others object?”

“They may refuse their own seats.”

The runner left with no permission slip because local authority already covered the change.

Savra went to the route board. Twenty-three separate squares carried charcoal marks. Kitchen fourteen’s square held only the word dark. She ran one finger beside it without filling the blank.

“What happens to their hidden pot after?” she asked.

Mava answered, “Civic evidence if we survive. Extinguished by its owners if they are released. It does not become coalition property because they were arrested.”

“Good.”

“You expected another answer?”

“I expected myself to want the ember.”

“Wanting it does not change custody.”

The exchange closed one more place where urgency could have become appetite.

After the couriers departed, Teren waited in the flour room. He had removed his grain-merchant coat and wore plain Free Granary clothes without a command tally. His left ankle had swollen above the boot. On the table between them lay a roll of gray knife cloth.

Savra knew the pattern of the knots. She had tied them in Ulsor before giving her tools to Mava.

“Guildspeaker authorized their return,” Teren said.

“Why are you carrying them?”

“I asked to.”

“That sounds like authority borrowed for a gesture.”

“It may be.” He considered the accusation instead of denying it. “Mava said you could refuse the messenger as well as the knives.”

Savra untied the cloth. Savra’s knives lay inside: the cleaver sharpened asymmetrically for fast joint work, the herb knife, and the narrow seal cutter. No imperial marks remained on the three handles. Mava’s salt guild had wrapped a plain copper wire around each grip to show civic custody ended by recorded release.

“These do not make you master feastwright,” Teren said.

“No.”

“They do not mean Tal Jes trusts you.”

“No.”

“They mean the kitchen assigned to the balancing seat needs tools you know.”

Savra tested the cleaver’s edge against her thumbnail. “And you wanted to return what I surrendered after confessing what I did.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Because I used the same argument at Tal Jes. Different scale, same hidden seat. I thought knowing the need let me decide who carried it.”

She rolled the knives closed again. “That does not put us on equal sides of an account. Nine-Salt lasted a campaign. Villages carried it for months.”

“I know.”

“You know a sentence about it.”

Teren looked toward the cold oven. “Yes.”

The answer left no argument to defeat.

Savra lifted the roll. “I will use them. You do not get forgiveness for bringing them.”

“I did not come for it.”

“People often come for things they know better than to name.”

“Then judge the work after.” He shifted weight off his left ankle. “If there is an after.”

No embrace followed. They returned to the kitchen conference with a workable loyalty and damage that remained named.

Lale had prepared the private unbound meal in a storeroom without salt channels. The phrase sounded grander than the food: red lentils, canal rice, pickled mustard stems, leftover chickpea cakes, and one bowl of pepper oil. She had cooked over charcoal scraped clean of coalition ash. No hearthseal touched the pots.

Savra sat with Teren, Dima, Lale, Mava, Veyra, and an empty place for Issen, who remained inside the Hall. Nobody took the head because the room had no head chair.

Lale served the rice. “Say it.”

“Unbound,” Savra said.

“Source fire?” Dima asked.

“New charcoal from the dyers’ yard. No civic ember.”

“Seat list?”

“None.”

“Then eat.”

The private unbound meal moved nothing between them except dishes. Savra’s hunger stayed hers. Teren’s ankle pain did not enter another body. Dima’s right hand remained difficult, so Lale steadied the rice bowl while Dima served with her left. Assistance crossed the table without magic and without pretending all burdens had become equal.

Teren asked Lale for the mustard stems. She passed them without looking away from her plate.

“After tomorrow,” he said, “if there is a hearing about Tal Jes, I will not ask you to speak privately first.”

“You already asked whether I would testify.”

“I asked from the hospital bench. I meant whether you would defend me.”

“I know what you meant.”

“I am withdrawing the request.”

Lale set the mustard dish beside him. “You cannot withdraw pressure I already carried. You can stop adding to it.”

“Yes.”

Their exchange ended there. The meal provided no ritual through which a brother could consume a sister’s anger or spread it until neither had enough to name.

Veyra divided the last chickpea cake into seven unequal pieces because it crumbled under the knife. She put the largest beside Dima, who had skipped the afternoon ration while inspecting cups.

“Not equal,” Dima observed.

“Not hidden,” Veyra said. “You need food. I want the crust.”

She took the smallest, darkest piece. Ordinary distribution could account for difference through speech, adjustment, and refusal. It did not need equal sensation to become legible.

Savra ate pepper oil with rice and felt sweat collect under her collar. Soon a table might send thousands of bodily states toward her. For this final quiet hour, heat came from the food she had chosen, and nobody else had to carry it.

Mava ate quickly. “At first signal, twenty-three kitchens open black salt. Second signal, they draw local lines. They do not feed until Savra confirms the First Hearth has taken the central topology.”

“How does she confirm?” Teren asked.

“Blue edge along all common grooves,” Savra said. “If fewer than twelve answer, we abort the overwrite and tell kitchens to keep their fires local.”

“If twelve to twenty-two?”

“Proceed with whatever answers. Gaps remain gaps.”

Veyra tore a chickpea cake. “And if Rovan lights before the couriers reach us?”

“We feel it,” Savra said. “His current will enter the provincial ash carried in our pots. The First Hearth may answer through the spice court.”

“May.”

“Yes.”

The meal did not invite vows, but silence made room for truth.

Savra looked at each person. “The balancing seat may kill me before the universal bond breaks. If I lose speech, Dima decides whether I remain capable. If I smash my cup, recoil returns local. No one holds my hands closed.”

Dima nodded. “My right hand cannot. My left will break it if you ask.”

“If I cannot ask?”

“Then I use the limits written at Tal Jes: failed breath count, loss of posture, inability to swallow. Not courage. Not usefulness.”

Mava said, “Recorded.”

Teren did not object that the plan needed Savra alive. Lale did not call the choice noble. They ate until the bowls emptied.

Afterward Savra washed her own plate. She dried the three knives and placed them beside the central bowl. Around the warehouse, runners settled in shifts while cooks checked dampers. Namarra’s Equinox bells remained hours away.

A blue line flared under the First Hearth’s copper hood.

No coalition cook had opened black salt.

Savra dropped the cloth. Heat entered her scarred left forearm, followed by the distant contraction of an empty stomach. The imperial current had reached the stolen brazier through ash signatures already joined elsewhere.

From the Hall, a signal horn sounded one long note.

“Rovan lit the first ember early,” Teren said.

Savra took up her knives. “Wake every kitchen.”

The cold warehouse filled with moving feet before the official night bell struck.

Chapter 26 — The Feast of a Hundred Fires

The first imperial bite reached Savra through the stolen brazier before her own porridge had boiled.

Hunger closed around her stomach. A second body’s heat pressed behind her eyes. Then came the split sensation of a blister opening under a heel, distant but exact enough to make her lift one foot from the warehouse floor.

Under the First Hearth hood, blue fire raced around seven coals.

“Rovan’s current,” she said. “Open black salt.”

Dima cut the waxed packet with her broad-handled knife. Mava’s witnesses drew back as Savra poured salt into the common groove around the brazier. In twenty-three kitchens across Namarra, couriers would be carrying the same order. Some would arrive late. A universal system enjoyed speed because it did not pause for answers.

The central bowl waited beside Savra’s knives. Plain red millet, thin enough to swallow under pain, had cooked over a Tal Jes ember nested beside the First Hearth. Around its rim, Dima had written Savra’s name, hearth mark, chosen weight, and function.

Mava read the declaration. “Always-open balancing seat: Savra Kel. Receives unsettled severance recoil only. No ordinary local burden. First successor Dima Orr under stated capacity. Every local table may refuse the common topology.”

“I affirm,” Savra said.

“Body condition?”

“Hunger not mine. Heat behind eyes. Blister pain at right heel. Breath steady.”

Dima recorded the entry.

Savra ate.

The millet carried black hearth-salt and old ash. It tasted of burned crust scraped from civic ovens, bitter enough to make her jaw tighten. Connection settled through the left forearm scar first. The imperial current pressed toward the First Hearth, trying to flatten every provincial signature into Rovan’s weighting. Savra laid both hands beside the common groove and looked for the blue edges of answering fires.

One appeared. Then four. Nine.

At twelve she gave the proceed signal.

Teren’s couriers struck roof pans in the sequence the route council had approved. Twenty-three local cooks opened their pots, drew separate salt lines, and began feeding only their named seats. No signal compelled them. Three kitchens remained dark after the first call. One answered late. Another sent refusal because soldiers had entered its court.

Seventeen blue edges held.

“Enough to enter,” Savra said. “Not enough to hold.”

Rovan’s first course strengthened.

Across the city, standardized white loaves had gone out from public ovens before the official bell. People ate because the locked gates had reduced half rations and because bread still looked like bread. The imperial seat lists reached them through old grain oaths. Hunger began moving out of soldiers, officials, and favored wards toward the hidden chambers under Namarra. Pain followed. Heat left bakers standing at overfired ovens and entered people lying beneath damp stone.

Savra felt only the current crossing the First Hearth, not memories or names. Hearthbinding could carry an empty stomach’s contraction. It could not tell her who had gone without eating.

“Kitchen five active,” called a courier. “Kitchen eleven active. Kitchen twenty reports arrested witness, cook proceeding with six seats.”

Blue fire reached twenty.

At each merchant kitchen, named diners swallowed a sour first bite to enter their local arc. Cooks then carried trays into festival squares and announced the choice available: eat a marked portion, inspect a personal cup, hear the central cost, join the local table, or pass. People already trapped inside Rovan’s bond could redirect their own seat through the black-salt topology only by eating from one of those fires. The coalition had food for hundreds, not the whole capital.

Hundreds could make openings through which local hearth signatures reasserted themselves.

The first salt cup broke near the canal ward.

Recoil struck Savra under the ribs. One diner had joined, felt a hospital patient’s breathlessness passing through Rovan’s current, and left before a second bite. The cup’s clay connection snapped. Burden already received remained in that body. The unsettled share came to Savra.

“Breath shortened,” she reported. “Posture sound.”

Dima counted. “One.”

More cups broke.

Not everyone left. A woman at the west annex kept hers after learning that her hunger would remain shared with eight neighbors at equal local weight. Two porters smashed theirs when imperial wounds entered the arc. A public baker held his cup in both hands and waited through one full transfer before choosing to stay. Choice produced no single moral shape.

Salt cups breaking sounded across Namarra like pottery stalls collapsing one vessel at a time.

Courier reports arrived faster than Mava’s witnesses could press them into wax.

At the canal laundry, imperial soldiers ordered the local diners away from the fire. The cook did not defend the hearth with their bodies. They carried the marked pot into the washing room, where steam hid the salt line from the courtyard, and asked every diner to affirm again because the conditions had changed. Three broke cups without eating a second portion. Four stayed. The cook reduced the sour paste rather than forcing the smaller group to carry the original timing.

Kitchen six in the rope-maker’s cellar received thirty frightened neighbors asking for protection from the imperial bond. Its list named seven. Lale stood in the doorway and explained that entering an unrehearsed person could misroute recoil into the others. She offered unbound rice and shelter instead. Some neighbors accused her of hoarding safety. She kept the line closed.

At the pottery ward, a woman who had joined the local table held her whole cup above the paving but did not release it. Her son had smashed his after a soldier’s burn entered his chest. The mother stayed because her own hunger had begun easing through a circle she trusted. Nobody required family members to make one choice.

Veyra’s west-annex kitchen sent out trays with the declaration written on flour boards. An imperial clerk read the central cost, took a marked cake, then broke his cup at the first transferred wound. He continued helping people read their options while carrying the pain already received. Severance removed no history from his body.

The relay itself had no magical speed. Runners crossed alleys. Bakers struck shutters. Oil sellers passed phrases over market walls. When a patrol blocked one street, kitchens waited without inventing a distant order. Rovan’s system moved through seals established over years; the coalition answered through feet already tired from one day.

A Hall signal changed from one long horn to two short. Issen’s prearranged code reported hidden-ward weighting increased. Teren sent no command to accelerate service. He gave the code to each courier and let local cooks decide whether their diners needed to hear it before the next bite. All twenty-one active kitchens chose to repeat their lists.

The repetition cost time while the imperial current deepened. It also meant that a person joining after the horn heard a changed risk rather than yesterday’s promise.

Savra took each return.

Hunger arrived in waves from people who had swallowed oil-delayed loaves hours apart. Heat striped her arms. Exertion filled her thighs with the climb of runners she could not see. A fraction of panic tightened her throat, embodied timing without thought. Dima kept the list of states and watched Savra’s breath.

“Twenty-one fires,” Mava said.

“Which absent?”

“Fourteen, fixed gap. Seven under search. Twenty-two silent.”

The topology pulled unevenly toward the south wall. Kitchen fourteen’s hidden pot carried an ember but no salt line, food, or swallowed seats. Rovan’s current found that unopposed ash signature and drove through it like water through a missing gate.

The First Hearth flared white.

Savra’s central bowl cracked along its rim. Recoil from three local circles reached her at once. She fell against the worktable, knives jumping in their cloth.

“Break?” Dima asked.

“Not my cup.”

“Your choice, not mine.”

“Continue.” Savra forced her back straight. “Move fourteen’s arc outside the common groove. Do not close the gap.”

No witness spoon remained to copy the correction. Twenty-three kitchens held the recipe in memory, and kitchen fourteen held nothing active. Savra could alter only the First Hearth’s central groove, making space around the missing signature rather than supplying it.

She used the narrow seal cutter to lift black salt from one segment. Her hand shook. One grain fell inward.

“Stop,” Dima said.

Savra stopped. Dima could not pinch the grain with her right fingers. She dampened a broad spoon, lifted it with her left, and removed the misplaced salt. Mava’s witness scraped the contaminated section clean. They redrew the gap together.

Blue fire divided around an empty span.

The collapse eased. Kitchen seven returned under guard pressure with four diners rather than nine. Kitchen twenty-two sent a dark signal; its owner had withdrawn access to the oven. The cooks extinguished their pot and left. The common topology held twenty-one active fires and two deliberate absences beyond fourteen.

“Proceed with twenty-one,” Savra said.

No one went to replace them.

At the Hall, Rovan increased hunger weighting.

Savra knew because her stomach contracted until she retched onto the floor. Nothing came up but red millet water. Dima held her shoulders from behind without taking any magical share. Veyra opened the warehouse door, where three runners waited for the order to move the First Hearth.

Mava said, “The local arcs are established. Why risk the brazier?”

“Rovan still controls the three capital ovens. From here I can cut his provincial reach, not the sink wards beneath his own hearth.”

“You may not reach the Hall.”

“Then the local tables survive while Namarra’s hidden seats carry the capital.” Savra wiped her mouth. “Issen entered to open the service court. The plan includes movement.”

Mava looked to each civic witness. They affirmed the recorded route. She did not grant permission as though the brazier belonged to her alone.

The runners lifted the First Hearth by iron hooks. Savra carried the central bowl. Dima carried Savra’s cup in her left hand, wrapped but accessible. Veyra went ahead with a flour sack. Teren waited in the alley with route-council authority for movement only.

“East lane closed,” he said. “Hall guards at the dyers’ arch. The laundry court remains open according to Issen’s last signal.”

“How many escorts?” Mava asked.

“Six volunteers. No table.”

“Who chose the route?”

“The runners after hearing both.”

They moved.

Namarra had become a city of crossed hungers. People staggered in streets after imperial pain entered them. Others stood abruptly straighter because wounds moved away. At a public table, a soldier dropped his loaf and stared at blood appearing under an old woman’s bandage. The bond gave neither person the other’s memory. He knew only that his spear cut had eased while her side opened.

Merchant cooks shouted local declarations above the Hall bells. Some listeners accepted marked chickpea cakes. Others overturned the trays, believing every bound meal imperial. A cook let the food fall and did not force it between anyone’s teeth.

An imperial patrol reached the west kitchen as Savra’s group passed the next alley. Soldiers kicked through the salt line. The local cook smashed the origin ember pot. Recoil returned to six diners and the central seat.

Savra’s knees buckled.

The runners kept the First Hearth level while she leaned on Dima. Burn pain crossed her chest, followed by a runner’s exhausted breath and the cold of somebody lying on infirmary stone.

“Posture failed,” Dima said. “One condition.”

“Recovered.”

“Recorded, not erased.”

At the laundry court, gray-clothed workers opened a coal hatch. Issen waited inside with blood on one sleeve that did not belong to them. They had freed Tam Vesso from his oven guard; the festival cook stood beside them holding a long paddle.

“Rovan started the second course,” Issen said. “He is sending public-oven heat to the northern hidden sink wards. Pain goes below the infirmary. Hunger goes to debt intake.”

“How do you know?” Savra asked.

“The Hall control bowls carry colored oil. I tasted the ladles.”

“Did you swallow?”

“Spit, rinsed, no seat mark. I remain unbound.”

Tam lifted a ring of keys. “South oven crew closed their damper. Rovan has two capital fires.”

A local refusal had removed one imperial anchor. Savra felt the topology sag, then pull harder through the remaining ovens.

They entered the Hall through the washing passage.

Above, the pavilion roared with voices. The Feast of a Hundred Fires had begun before guests took ceremonial places. Hall attendants tried to seat officials while bodies across the city carried one another’s states without explanation. The great fired-clay cauldron stood in a bronze cradle over three joined fire channels. White broth churned inside, thick with standardized grain, provincial oils, and black salt.

Rovan sat beyond the outer salt line with one hand on signal levers. His chair remained untouched by the currents crossing the floor. A small command cup rested beside him, half empty.

Savra smelled bitter fenugreek in the steam.

Kelar.

The refugee kitchen’s ash signature carried the final balancing sink. Rovan had put the bitter herb into the control broth so a feastwright could taste whether that route remained open. The flavor sharpened under biased weighting exactly as it had when children took cavalry wounds.

Rovan saw the brazier. Grief crossed his face before calculation covered it.

“You kept it alive,” he said.

“People did.”

“Put it beside the cauldron. We can still stabilize this.”

“Name the wards under the infirmary.”

His hand stayed on the levers. “Balancing reserves prevent chaotic recoil.”

“Name them.”

Issen stepped onto the pavilion platform and turned the speaking horn toward the public gallery. “Six hundred twelve marked before debt intake. Hunger, heat, breath, and pain categories. Names sealed from diners. Refusal unavailable.”

Rovan’s attendants tried to close the horn shutter. Issen braced it open with the tasting-chain clasp Pel had left on the control table, recovered from the inspection court and kept as evidence. Silver bent under the hinge.

“You have given a number without its balance,” Rovan said. His voice carried through the second horn at his chair. “Those wards prevent random recoil into children, crowded markets, and field hospitals. Every body there receives food and medical watch. If the reserves close now, the Great Table throws its unsettled weight wherever capacity remains.”

Savra heard the persuasive part because it contained a physical truth. Abrupt severance could scatter burden. He had arranged the topology so his chosen victims appeared to protect everyone else from chaos created by the arrangement.

“Then open their names and let them break cups,” she said.

“The wards cannot sustain individual severance under activation.”

“Because you gave them bed tags, not cups.”

“I gave the continent a design that survives panic.”

“You designed away every answer except yours.”

A control clerk pulled the pain lever halfway back. Rovan caught the motion and returned it to its marked stop. “Do not improvise.”

The clerk stared at a wax tablet where ward reports arrived through runners. “Breath reserve is over measure. Two attendants collapsed.”

“Transfer one share to public infirmary staff.”

“Named?” Issen asked through the horn.

Rovan did not answer them. He ordered the clerk to use the emergency category, another label broad enough to conceal whoever happened to be working upstairs.

Savra tasted bitter fenugreek again as the Kelar sink opened wider. The flavor sharpened past bitterness into metal. Rovan had chosen the refugee kitchen because ration oaths there changed quickly; displaced people arrived, ate, and left without officials updating names. Administrative instability became magical capacity.

“Kelar is refusing,” Mava called from the stair. A courier had arrived from the west signal post. “Their seal went dark.”

The corresponding line on the Hall floor flickered. Rovan pushed more oil into the west public oven to compensate. The act drove heat toward the northern ward and made three attendants cry out behind the control screens.

“Your balancing reserve is failing,” Savra said.

“Because you are cutting it without carrying its share.”

“I am carrying every departure people choose from our tables.” Her body shook hard enough to make the knife strike the cauldron rim. “You still have not entered yours.”

Guards moved toward them. Tam swung his paddle into the nearest oil channel. Hot carrier oil spilled across the stone, cutting one signal groove. Dima threw plain salt over it. Steam burst upward.

Teren did not order the escort. The six volunteers chose positions around the runners, blocking guards without drawing blades. Mava held up the civic witness tablets and shouted the names of Tal Jes hospital victims. Confusion slowed the pavilion long enough for Savra to reach the cauldron.

Rovan pulled the hunger lever.

The command bowl beside him trembled. He had eaten from it. Its seal named him as a witness seat with zero burden, allowing his body to sense activation timing without receiving the states. A zero weight still created a seat.

Savra set the First Hearth into the empty fire channel.

Blue flame climbed the cauldron.

She poured her central bowl into Rovan’s broth. The Many-Table topology entered through twenty active merchant kitchens now, then nineteen as another local fire went dark. Each kept its own salt line. Their shared groove named only the balancing function and the visible right to sever.

Rovan looked down at the blue flame. “You have built one sink and put yourself in it.”

“I built a seat people can see and leave.”

“They will spend you.”

“Some will. Others will keep their cups. You never let them answer.”

He pushed the pain lever toward the northern wards.

Issen seized the speaking horn. “Break or keep your cup by your own condition. No witness may stop you.”

Couriers repeated the call outside. Across Namarra, pottery began to shatter again.

Recoil hit Savra with enough force to drive her hands into the cauldron rim. Hunger folded her. Heat burned without flame. A hundred small injuries arrived as bodily fact: bruised ribs, cut palms, split lips, overworked calves. No skill came with them, no loyalty, no memory of how they happened.

Dima stood at Savra’s left with the cup ready.

“Breath count failing,” she said.

“Not yet.”

“Two conditions.”

Savra could no longer feel her feet. “Hold until local lines divide.”

Rovan’s chair remained outside the salt line.

Savra used the seal cutter on the blue groove connecting his command cup to the cauldron. She did not add a new person. She changed the zero weighting on a seat he had already entered by swallowing.

“Rovan Dast,” she said. “Witness seat. Command cup. Existing mark.”

He understood. His hand left the lever.

“You cannot set a weight without the witness impression.”

“Twenty kitchens remember what you taught one object.”

The First Hearth answered their distributed lines. Blue light reached Rovan’s cup.

He knocked it from the table. The cup struck carpet and did not break.

Issen kicked the chair forward. Its rear legs crossed the outer groove. Tam’s paddle swept plain salt behind it, closing the ring. Physical geometry joined the rewritten witness seat.

Rovan inside the salt line received his first ordinary share.

Hunger took his voice. He bent over the command table, one arm across his stomach. Heat from public ovens flushed his skin. Pain from the wards returned through the controls he had set. Not all of it, not punishment equal to every harm, only the conserved fraction assigned to a visible participant.

“Now,” Savra told Issen. “Read the local choice.”

Issen spoke through the horn. “Your hearth. Your name. Your weight. Cup whole or cup broken. No hidden replacement.”

The phrase traveled through couriers and kitchen shouts. People kept cups. People broke them. Some local tables lost every diner and went dark. Others held with two or twenty. Rovan’s universal ring fractured at each separate salt line.

The missing kitchen fourteen remained missing. No pain rushed into an invented substitute. Its provincial signature fell back to its own hearth as the common groove split around it.

Beneath the infirmary, hidden sink seals cracked under recoil. The bodies there kept injuries already received. New transfers stopped as local tables severed their route. One public oven retained a neighborhood bond because its diners had not broken it. Another went dark when Tam’s crew flooded the firebox.

The Great Table ceased to have one weight.

Rovan reached for the central lever again. Teren caught his wrist, then looked to Mava rather than deciding the end alone.

“Detain him,” Mava said under civic seal.

The escort pulled Rovan from the chair. He remained conscious. Issen took the command cup as evidence.

Savra’s sight narrowed around the cauldron. The First Hearth burned too hot beneath wet broth. Dima shouted that her posture had failed again. Savra tried to answer and could not swallow.

“Limit reached,” Dima said.

She struck Savra’s salt cup against the bronze cradle.

It broke.

The central seat severed after the universal groove had already fractured. Recoil returned along surviving local lines, each carrying only its own unsettled share. Diners cried out but no hidden ward received it. Dima refused the successor position because Savra’s incapacity had come after separation; taking the seat would reconnect what no longer needed a center.

Tam opened the cold-brine cistern above the Hall fire channels. Brine flooded the overheated cradle. The great cauldron’s glazed clay lining contracted around boiling broth.

A crack ran from rim to base.

White porridge poured across the salt channels, washing black salt into harmless mud. Attendants dragged the First Hearth clear before the bronze cradle shifted. Its seven coals stayed alive.

Savra lay among broken cups. Hunger remained after severance, not as continuing transfer but as damage from too many contradictory bodily timings. Her stomach cramped as if it had not eaten for weeks, eased, then cramped again. Thousands of delayed oil-weighted shares had crossed her in minutes. The table had ended. Her body did not know when fullness belonged.

Dima brought unbound barley water and touched one drop to Savra’s tongue. Her swallowing muscles contracted before the liquid reached the throat. She turned her head and coughed it out.

“No hearth food,” Dima told the attendants. “Nothing stamped, no ration oath, no shared-cup broth. Her body has carried too many seat timings. We do not test whether another table settles them.”

A Hall healer offered poppy milk. Dima smelled it, checked its vessel for ash residue, and refused until someone produced a sealed unbound jar. Pain relief could not be allowed to create another route into Savra while active embers still burned in the city.

Savra tried to say that hunger had returned. Dima already had two fingers at her throat, counting the dry swallow that never completed.

Dima checked her breathing. “Savra. Swallow.”

Water touched her mouth. She could not.

Around them, guards lowered weapons as the pavilion floor lost every imperial signal line. Rovan sat under watch beside the cracked cauldron, alive and shaking. Outside, separate kitchen bells answered one another across Namarra without falling into a single rhythm.

Savra heard them as she lost consciousness, nineteen fires still burning apart. None matched the others, and no single hand could close them all.

Chapter 27 — After the Cauldron Broke

At dawn, Teren counted seventeen granary locks and three thousand people outside them.

The crowd filled Measure Square from the imperial storehouse to the Hall steps. Some carried broken salt cups. Others carried bowls, sacks, or nothing. Namarra’s public ovens had gone dark after the Great Table split, and nobody trusted the stamped white loaves left on their racks. Hunger had returned to the bodies that owned it. It had also remained as transferred injury in people who had never agreed to carry anyone else.

Teren stood on the granary stair without a command tally.

Mava’s provisional council had asked him to count stores, routes, and daily need. The request gave him a task, not authority over the square. He wore a clay token marked inventory witness. Two Namarra grain clerks held matching tokens. Lale represented public-oven workers. A terrace merchant recorded damaged wagons.

“North granary,” Teren called. “Seventeen thousand white-grain measures, sealed dry. West cellar, four thousand barley, two thousand spoiled by flood. Hall reserve, unverified.”

A man in a burned imperial coat shouted, “Those stores fed the army while our children carried wounds.”

“Open all of them!” somebody answered.

Hands struck the first door. The bronze lock held.

Teren’s body wanted to put guards across the stair, form lanes, appoint distributors, and make the crowd obey a plan before fear consumed the stores. Tal Jes had taught him what that instinct could hide inside the word need.

He turned to Mava. “Recommendation: open north first under four ward witnesses. Issue one measure per six people. Keep the west cellar closed until spoilage is separated.”

“Who chooses distributors?” she asked.

“Each ward sends two. Granary clerks retain measure control. Kitchen council witnesses the seals.”

“Army?”

“Same queue. No separate ration.”

Mava faced the square. “You heard the proposal. Ward kitchens answer.”

The argument lasted while hungry people waited. Three wards demanded two measures. The south ward refused Hall clerks because those clerks had certified hidden sink stores. Public-oven workers insisted on inspecting grain oaths before any sack entered a hearth. None of those disputes could be solved by opening a door and calling the contents free.

A stone struck the granary façade.

Then another.

At the north street, sixty imperial soldiers appeared in shield order. Their commander carried an order board bearing Rovan’s seal. The crowd turned from the locks.

“This store remains under Hall custody,” she called. “Disperse.”

Mava descended one stair. “Grand Provisioner Dast is under arrest. His seal carries no force.”

“My soldiers have eaten half rations for two days. If that door opens without guard, the store is gone by noon.”

“Then guard the distribution.”

The commander looked at Teren. “Free Granary wants imperial grain.”

“People want grain,” he said. “Your soldiers count among them.”

“They held the city.”

“So did bakers, laundry workers, sink-ward attendants, and people who broke tables. Nobody gets a service weight.”

A group from the river quarter pushed toward the soldiers. One carried a torch despite full daylight. “Burn their barracks. Take what they took.”

Teren stepped down before he could ask permission. He stopped at the last stair and turned back to Mava.

“May I address routes?”

“Yes.”

He pointed toward the imperial barracks. “Their stores contain lamp oil, bow cases, and two days of military grain. Burn them and you lose food plus the wagons needed to move this grain. Seize them without inventory and officers hide what remains.”

“Then kill the officers first,” the torchbearer said.

“No.” The answer left Teren too quickly, carrying command’s old edge. He lowered his voice. “Rovan survives for judgment. So do the people who served him unless a hearing finds a crime. Revenge looting gives every keeper reason to burn a ledger before surrendering it.”

“Easy mercy from a captain.”

“I used patients at Tal Jes. My hearing begins today.”

The admission moved through the crowd. Some knew already. Others cursed him by a name he had not heard since Eren Ford. He stayed on the stair.

The imperial commander lowered her order board. “You submitted?”

“Yes.”

“Before whom?”

“Tal Jes witnesses, sink-ward representatives, coalition kitchens, and this city if it joins the council.”

She studied the crowd behind him. “My soldiers guard the north store if they receive the same ration in a separate line.”

“No separate line,” Lale said from the witness table. “Same line, weapons stacked under their own watch.”

The commander objected. Two ward delegates objected to any armed guard. Mava negotiated the physical terms: soldiers stacked spears inside a rope square; six remained shielded at the doors; six unarmed ward workers stood beside them. The grain clerks broke the first imperial seal in public.

When the doors opened, cool dry air rolled into the square carrying the smell of wheat husks.

Nobody rushed immediately. The argument had built lanes people recognized as their own, imperfectly. Ward distributors entered with empty measures. Clerks read sack stamps. Public-oven workers marked any grain oath linked to a dangerous seal and sent those sacks to unbound milling only.

The imperial granaries opened one by one over the next six hours.

Breaking seals proved faster than moving food. North granary’s stairs admitted one sack at a time, and its loading yard faced an alley too narrow for two wagons to pass. Teren proposed a one-way circuit through the old parade ground. Ward drivers walked it before accepting, measuring corners with their own axle poles. Two turns had to be widened by removing festival stalls. The stall owners recorded every plank taken and which kitchen would return it.

Measure disputes began at the first issue table. Imperial cups held one twentieth more grain than Ulsor guild cups and one twelfth less than an Eren Ford household scoop. Standardization had helped the Hall collect tax while letting public kitchens use local vessels that made rations appear generous or mean. Issen brought the bronze master measure from the Hall under two witnesses. Each ward marked a temporary clay cup against it, then kept its own cup rather than surrendering all distribution to the master object.

At the barracks store, soldiers refused to release mule teams unless their wounded received grain first. Sink-ward delegates objected that many soldiers had eaten full rations while civilians carried their injuries. The dispute stopped six wagons.

Teren counted travel time and said nothing until Mava asked for a route consequence.

“Without those mules, the north infirmary receives grain after dark. With them, every listed patient receives a midday share. That includes soldiers upstairs and sink occupants below.”

The commander asked, “Equal amount?”

“Set by each ward kitchen after medical need is read publicly. Not by rank.”

A former breath-reserve prisoner lifted his intake tag. “I want the soldier who held my door to carry my sack.”

One guard stepped forward. The commander allowed eight more. They moved grain under ward supervision without calling the labor payment for what the ward had done to the prisoner. Restitution began as transport and could not end there.

Near the east store, looters found oil jars marked for military lamps. A kitchen worker tried to seize them for frying. Another person wanted them poured into the street so the army could not use them. Teren identified two jars tainted with pitch, unfit for food, and six containing clean sesame oil. Witnesses sent the pitch to guarded civic stores and the sesame oil to three unbound kitchens. Destruction would have felt decisive. Separation fed people.

Teren counted without deciding who deserved food. North store supplied ward kitchens. Barracks grain went to field kitchens around the infirmary. The Hall reserve contained black-salt contamination and stayed closed under joint seal. West barley required sifting and went first to people able to boil it outside bound ovens.

At midday, a group broke into the Grand Provisioner’s private store. They found dried figs, fine rice, and eight jars of old lentils. Mava had the goods carried to the same measured lanes. Rovan’s household did not receive protection from evidence, but neither did the crowd get trophies.

His arrest became public at the second bell.

Mava stood on the Hall stair with Issen, two Namarra magistrates, and witnesses from the hidden wards. Rovan appeared behind them without ceremonial robe. His hands remained unbound because guards surrounded him and because public humiliation could not substitute for custody. He still looked like a provisioner inspecting a delay.

Issen read the charges: concealed seat lists, coerced ration oaths, unauthorized bodily transfers, false safety certification, unlawful imprisonment for sink capacity, and activation of the Great Table after loss of the First Hearth. Their own name followed among officials submitting testimony.

A woman from the northern ward shouted for Rovan to be put in a pain table.

“No,” Dima said through the speaking horn. “We do not prove hidden punishment wrong by building a public one.”

The crowd did not become peaceful. Rotten fruit struck the steps. Guards lifted shields around Rovan. He flinched once and remained standing.

Teren watched from the grain lanes. Survival preserved the possibility that records would be opened, subordinates questioned, and provincial harms traced. It also required people harmed by him to see him fed under guard. Justice had logistics too, none emotionally clean.

Captain Orel Tas, held under coalition guard since Halvek, transferred with the witness files to a Halvek civic panel. He surrendered his field-kitchen orders and sought no immunity; the panel kept him in custody until every concealed household list could be recovered.

Lale and Dima built emergency field kitchens in five squares. The first served unbound barley with onions and no seal. The second offered a voluntary fatigue share for water carriers: names on a board, equal weights, two-hour duration, cooks seated, cups thin enough for Dima’s right palm to break against a table edge. The remaining three stayed unbound because no trained witness could supervise them.
