Nobody called the unbound pots wasted magic.

By Day 35, the queues shortened. By Day 36, provincial delegates occupied the Hall kitchens instead of its banquet rooms. They slept beside ledgers and opened sink-ward records name by name. The First Hearth rested in a public court under four rotating civic seals. No single seal opened its gate.

Teren’s Tal Jes judgment began in the old flour exchange.

Mava chaired only the procedure. Eda Tel, whose mouth had burned while her leg lay splinted, sat among the hospital witnesses. Dima presented the cracked seal fragments. Issen explained the reversed sixth-campaign notation. Lale gave the sequence of Teren’s evacuation decision, hidden plate, false support declaration, and later request that she defend him.

Teren stood without advocate by his own choice until Eda objected.

“You choosing no help does not help us,” she said carefully through scarred lips. “It makes your punishment another plan you control.”

The council appointed a record advocate whose task involved testing facts, not excusing them. Teren accepted.

He described the red millet terraces from first gate release to final severance. He gave quantities where he knew them and said I do not know where pain shares had erased origin. He did not use the word necessary.

“Did you believe the city would fall?” asked a terrace witness.

“Yes.”

“Do you still believe it might have fallen without the seal?”

“Yes.”

“Then why call the act wrong?”

“Because I made the hospital carry my uncertainty without naming them. The danger did not give me their answer.”

Dima asked, “Would you activate it again?”

“No.”

“If refusing meant losing Tal Jes?”

Teren looked at Eda. “I would give the choice to the people carrying it. If there were no time, I would not use them.”

The words cost more than claiming a changed heart. They admitted that command might have to endure defeat rather than convert another body into a resource.

The provisional ruling removed Teren from military or hearth authority until a full interprovincial judgment. He would work only under elected kitchen councils, disclose all copied seals and route contingencies, and provide restitution labor to Tal Jes and the hospital. The council reserved further penalty after patient testimony and damage accounts. Lale voted with Eren Ford to accept the provisional ruling. She did not ask for lenience.

Outside the exchange, Teren handed over his remaining private route notebooks. One contained nothing incriminating, only bridge weights and canal depths. Surrendering harmless pages felt foolish. Partial surrender would have let him decide which knowledge others could inspect.

On Day 38, the federation vote began.

Ulsor proposed that every burden-table require a local kitchen council, published names, weights, duration, a cook’s seat, and breakable personal cups. Tal Jes added independent injury records. Eren Ford required that no regional council could activate another hearth without its local vote. Namarra sink-ward delegates demanded a law against category seats that concealed names.

Arguments lasted two days. Some provinces wanted the First Hearth destroyed. Others feared imperial remnants would seize provincial seals if no common defense remained. Mava proposed civic custody with four rotating keys and no authority to approve a table. Any multihearth action would require local councils to opt in separately.

The key question consumed a full afternoon. If all four seals belonged to provinces that regularly traded together, they could become a smaller empire by habit. If one belonged permanently to Namarra, old authority survived under a new title. The delegates settled on rotation by lot every sixty days, with no two keys held by adjacent provinces and one reserved for a council that had declined the previous multihearth action.

“Reward refusal?” asked a river delegate.

“Give refusal custody of the thing it refused,” said a sink-ward witness. “Otherwise only agreeing provinces ever touch the lock.”

Eren Ford proposed that each local seat list remain posted for one digestion cycle after severance, long enough for injuries to be recorded. Ulsor wanted permanent copies. Tal Jes objected that permanent public names could expose prisoners, fugitives, or people hiding from violent households. They compromised on names held by local auditors and public counts showing seats, weights, injuries, and reasons for withheld identity.

Issen refused the title First Auditor when three delegates offered it. “No office should certify a table alone,” they said. “Elect at least three, and let any one stop service for missing disclosure. Approval requires the local council.”

The delegates revised the article.

Teren sat behind the Eren Ford table as a logistics witness. He could answer how many carts a rule required, how long notices took to reach canal towns, or how much clay one hundred personal cups consumed. He did not vote. When a delegate quoted his convoy work as proof that one coordinator should hold emergency authority, Lale answered before him.

“His work proves routes need a practiced counter. Tal Jes proves a practiced counter cannot choose hidden seats.”

The emergency article assigned temporary route coordination but barred the coordinator from changing lists, weights, cooks, or local fire status. Teren copied the limits into his notebook under public eyes.

The provisional federation passed by twenty-seven delegations, with six abstentions and three refusals. Refusing provinces retained trade access and control of their fires. No bond enforced the vote.

Savra remained unconscious through all of it.

Dima fed her drops of unbound barley water, then spoonfuls, recording every hunger wave. Some came minutes after food. Others struck while Savra’s stomach remained full. No live ash entered the room. Her body had kept the injury after the table ended.

On Day 40, Savra wakes became the phrase runners carried badly through the Hall, as if waking erased what waited afterward.

Teren reached the infirmary after Lale and found Savra sitting against rolled blankets. Her face had thinned. Savra’s knives lay on a tray beyond reach.

“Can you eat?” he asked.

“Sometimes.” Her voice scraped. “Not from a bound hearth.”

Dima stood beside the bed. “Not now. Likely not again. Any table may give her body another timing it cannot separate.”

Savra looked at the tray. “Give me the herb knife.”

“No,” Dima said.

“I am not asking to join a table.”

“You cannot stand.”

“Then bring the board here.”

Lale set a chopping board across Savra’s knees and placed one smoked onion on it. Dima gave her the small knife handle first, watching whether the hand held steady. Savra cut the onion into uneven slices. The first hunger wave doubled her over. She waited, breathing through it, then finished the second half.

No one called the work recovery. She could not taste from a bound pot, verify a shared burden, or occupy the cook’s required seat. Under the new rules, that barred her from designing any hearth table alone.

It did not bar her from cooking food that moved no pain.

Teren carried the sliced onion to the infirmary’s unbound cauldron because Savra could not yet walk. She corrected the amount of oil from the bed, and Lale ignored one correction until she tested the pan herself.

Outside, twenty-seven delegations posted the provisional kitchen laws. Inside, Savra listened to ordinary onions strike hot oil, every body keeping its own hunger until the meal was served. Dima marked the pot unbound in charcoal, not because anyone doubted it, but because the new practice began where witnesses could still see the cook. Teren took bowls to the corridor under Lale’s direction. Savra stayed at the bed with the knife washed beside her. When the first bowl returned empty, she asked who needed the next one and did not ask who deserved it.

Chapter 28 — The Festival of Many Tables

One year later, Issen waited in Kelar Market while a twelve-year-old auditor rejected a feastwright’s salt cup.

“The name mark crosses the weight,” the girl said.

The feastwright, a river man three times her age, turned the cup in his hands. On its outside, wet clay carried his name and the Eren Ford hearth curl. Inside, a half-share line ran too close to the final letter.

“I know what it says,” he replied.

“You know because you wrote it. The diner who breaks it should not have to.”

Around them, the morning audit continued. Kitchen councils had built temporary tables beneath the spice awnings where Savra once fled imperial inspectors. Each hearth displayed source ember, food, seat names, weights, duration, severance method, cook’s seat, and two elected witnesses. Diners handled their own cups before any grain entered a pot.

The feastwright scraped his mark away and began again.

Issen pressed a small notch into their audit tablet: defect found locally, corrected before service. They no longer wore silver across the chest. Three auditors worked the Kelar festival, elected from different councils. Any one could halt a kitchen for missing disclosure. None could approve it alone.

The girl’s name, Pava, appeared on the council slate beside Issen’s and an elderly Ulsor brine keeper’s. Her age had provoked objections until she read six deliberately flawed seat lists faster than every senior clerk. Election did not make her infallible. It gave her objections a recorded route.

“Cup clear,” Pava said after the correction.

The Ulsor auditor checked the cook’s seat. Issen verified the origin ember against the custody jar. Three separate marks opened service.

Across Kelar, ninety-six visiting hearths had sent cooks or witnesses. Four had declined: two because harvest was late, one because its council opposed multihearth festivals, and one because fever closed its road. Their absences remained on the public board. Nobody carried substitute embers.

Local Kelar fires brought the visible count to one hundred, though no recipe joined them all.

The first tables began after the sun cleared the cloth roofs. Ulsor served pale barley and salt-baked fish in separate bowls, joining only six water carriers who had chosen to share leg fatigue for the afternoon’s work. An Eren Ford table used red lentils and pickled mustard stems to distribute oven heat among bakers. Tal Jes offered peppered millet to a small group of terrace veterans who chose equal pain shares during a scar-treatment session supervised by Dima.

No table served as the festival’s center.

The audit rules required inspectors to search for concealment beneath accurate words. A table could list every diner and still hide a second weight under a category, as Teren had done at Tal Jes. Issen asked cooks to lift bowls, turn cups, steam seals with vinegar, and explain every notch to someone from another province.

At kitchen thirty-one, a Namarra bread guild had listed oven support beneath five named bakers. The phrase carried no additional names, but its old Hall meaning could absorb anyone fed through the guild oath.

“Who does support include?” Pava asked.

“Only the five above,” said the guild cook.

“Then erase the category.”

“It explains their work.”

“Their names explain their seats. Their work can go on the public board.”

The cook appealed to Issen. A year earlier the office of First Taster might have resolved the dispute from precedent. Issen declined. The three auditors halted service and sent the question to the local kitchen council. The council removed the category before the ember was opened.

At a Kelar chickpea table, one diner tested her cup against the paving before eating. It broke. The cook marked vessel failure, no seat entered, gave her a spare, and read the full list again. Later the same woman swallowed two bites, felt a neighbor’s oven heat, and chose to break the second cup. That break severed an active seat. Two similar pieces of clay carried different records because the body’s condition had changed between them.

A visiting council from the salt uplands brought no bound table at all. Its members opposed redistributing pain but wanted to trade seed and attend the common course. Festival organizers gave them an awning equal in size to the hearth kitchens. Absence of magic did not reduce their civic standing.

The four declining provinces appeared on every count board. Their blank positions kept auditors from announcing universal participation. Children asked why the board held spaces for fires that never arrived. Cooks answered with weather, harvest, disagreement, and illness rather than calling the spaces failures.

Issen moved from one to the next, tasting only unbound inspection portions prepared before seals touched food. Their palate still found what official kitchens tried to hide: old oil under fresh spice, souring delayed by a warm jar, black salt clinging to a spoon groove. The work no longer let a mouth certify safety for unseen bodies. Taste began questions. Lists and witnesses answered some. Diners answered the rest.

At the Tal Jes awning, Dima cut goat curd with a knife whose handle had been widened to fill her right palm. A leather loop fixed the blade against her hand. Her wrist and shoulder supplied pressure that fingers once provided. She worked slower than before Ember Bridge, and the service schedule included the difference.

A young cook reached to finish the cuts for her.

“I’ll ask when I need you,” Dima said.

He withdrew.

She divided curd into eight portions, then checked the scar-treatment list. “Two pain shares withdrew this morning. Reduce the sour millet or the transfer enters too fast.”

The local cook adjusted the pot. The two withdrawn names remained posted with the notation refused before service. Dima did not call them weak. The people staying did not receive praise for courage.

“Your knife loop passed inspection,” Issen said.

“My knife does not need your approval.”

“The food board does.”

“That one can have it after Pava reads the weights.”

Dima’s mouth bent at one corner. Time had not made her gentle about offices. Issen valued that more than admiration.

Near the old refugee kitchen, Teren unloaded sacks under the supervision of Kelar’s elected kitchen council. He wore a route worker’s red cord and carried no weapon. His left ankle still stiffened after long roads, so a younger driver handled the upper stacks. Teren counted measures at ground level.

“Thirty-two chickpea, eighteen barley, seven lentil,” he reported.

Councilor Pava’s mother checked the receipt. “Your route slate says eight lentil.”

“One split in the north wagon. Spoiled by ditch water. Loss witnessed at the bridge.”

“Compensation?”

“Free Granary route fund, subject to your claim.”

He did not promise replacement from another town’s allotment. The council signed for what arrived.

Teren saw Issen and lifted two fingers in greeting. A line of petitioners waited beside him: Tal Jes hospital representatives reviewing his third restitution route, Free Granary drivers contesting axle schedules, Eren Ford delegates asking for canal estimates. His knowledge remained useful. His decisions did not travel without other signatures.

Lale supervised the communal oven across the lane. She and Teren spoke over a flour board, close enough to hear, far enough that neither could pretend the year had restored an earlier ease. Their sibling bond had learned procedure because affection alone had once carried too much unspoken pressure.

At the public notice wall, a bell announced the annual judgments.

People gathered beneath the awnings. Issen joined Mava on the low platform while recorders unrolled the provincial findings. Rovan Dast’s case occupied three linked scrolls. Every participating court had heard sink-ward survivors, ration clerks, soldiers, cooks, cold-seat villages, and the accused.

Mava read the joint sentence.

“Rovan Dast is permanently removed from all grain, hearth, custody, teaching, and public office. He remains in civic confinement subject to provincial witness access. He must answer archive inquiries and identify concealed seat systems, but no testimony reduces sentence or controls restitution. He receives the ordinary public ration and may not be entered into a burden-table for punishment, labor, or demonstration. Provincial claims for specific injuries continue independently.”

No execution. No hidden torment. No return to private life.

A Halvek delegate shouted that he deserved every wound from the granary battle. A woman once held in the Namarra heat ward answered, “I will not share a table with him to make you feel balanced.”

The argument continued after the formal reading. The sentence did not heal burns, restore Dima’s hand, or return years to villages that carried marching armies. It preserved Rovan as a source of evidence without making testimony a route to forgiveness.

The archive work had already changed three judgments. Rovan identified a ration-oath index hidden inside bridge-maintenance accounts, leading auditors to two villages whose residents still carried unexplained campaign injuries. He also refused to name one retired feastwright, claiming the person had followed his direct order. The courts treated the refusal as obstruction, not loyalty, and extended witness restrictions. Useful evidence did not buy him control over the record.

A Tal Jes delegate asked whether ordinary public rations amounted to mercy. Mava answered by reading the confinement ledger: the same grain measure as the ward where he remained, medical care under two witnesses, no private stores, no command over cooks, and no salt cup because he could not be entered into a table. Keeping him alive cost food. Killing him would also have been a distribution chosen by power.

The heat-ward woman spoke again. “Do not call my refusal to torture him forgiveness.”

Nobody did.

Restitution reports followed the sentence. Seized Hall stipends funded dressing oil at Tal Jes, rebuilding at Halvek, and new communal ovens in seven cold-seat villages. The amounts did not equal the injuries. Several councils refused coin until imperial land records were corrected. Others took grain first and reserved further claims. A common verdict did not require common remedies.

Eda Tel had sent a written statement from Tal Jes. Scarring in her mouth still made travel painful. Her statement asked that every future table record not only names and weights but the ordinary reason a person happened to be available: patient, prisoner, worker, traveler, child. Categories could not define seats, yet social conditions still explained why the same bodies were repeatedly asked.

Pava added the proposal to the next council agenda rather than announcing it as law. Endings, Issen had learned, required minutes.

Issen’s own judgment followed. They had received no confinement. The courts barred them from sole tasting certification or lifetime office, required annual public testimony, and assigned five years of paid restitution work funded from seized Hall stipends. Some sink survivors considered that too little. Issen had no rebuttal that could weigh it into adequacy.

They read their acceptance aloud.

After noon, the festival tables began severing in their own times. Ulsor’s water-carrier fire went dark when the cistern work ended. Eren Ford kept its baker table through the last oven batch. Tal Jes ended early after one veteran’s cup cracked accidentally; the cook discarded the affected food and severed all eight seats rather than guess whether the break carried intent.

At each site, auditors posted final injuries and unsettled shares. Named records went to the local council. Public boards showed counts and any identity withheld for protection. The law allowed no hidden seats: every magical participant had a recorded name accessible to independent auditors, while public anonymity could protect the vulnerable without turning them into a category sink.

By late afternoon, all bound fires had ended.

Ending required more than letting flames die. Each cook checked the last swallowed time, waited through the promised duration, and extinguished the origin ember in its own vessel. Auditors counted whole cups, broken cups, and cups lost before service. They compared injuries against the opening boards. Where one diner’s fatigue persisted beyond the expected interval, the local table stayed under observation rather than being declared closed for the schedule’s convenience.

Ash from bound fires went into labeled cold jars. It could be returned to its home hearth, buried, or kept as evidence according to local decision. None entered the common ovens. Water used to wash salt lines drained into settling pits instead of kitchen cisterns because black hearth-salt residue could cling to grease. Ordinary sanitation became part of magical severance.

At the Eren Ford oven, one coal resisted extinction beneath a crust of ash. Lale found it before the unbound flour arrived. She lifted the coal into the correct custody pot and washed the oven deck again. A year earlier, a rushed cook might have called the glow too small to matter. The accidental Kelar bond had begun with mixed embers nobody intended to join.

Pava walked the market ringing a handbell only after all three auditors signed the separation board. The bell did not proclaim safety forever. It reported a completed inspection at one hour on one day.

Kelar cooks swept salt lines from the market stones. They washed tables, changed ladles, and brought out vessels that had never touched live ash. The next course belonged to everyone who wanted food without a seat.

Savra directed it from the old refugee kitchen.

She wore no feastwright robe. Her knives hung from a plain belt. A year had returned weight to her face but not regular hunger. Some mornings she woke ravenous after eating before bed. Some afternoons a full bowl could not convince her body that food had arrived. Dima’s standing rule remained absolute: no bound bite, no tasting over live ash, no cook’s seat.

That kept Savra outside every hearth table under the laws she had helped establish.

It did not keep her outside the kitchen.

She had designed the unbound common course: chickpea cakes browned on iron plates, smoked onions folded through lentils, flatbread from mixed provincial flours, Ulsor sour plum served separately, Tal Jes pepper oil passed by hand. Each ingredient kept enough distinction to be recognized. The course promised no equal sensation. People could take pepper or refuse it, eat more bread, trade a plum for onions, or leave.

Dima handled the slicing station beside her. Her loop knife chopped mustard stems. Pava carried audit boards away because an unbound course required no magical approval, only ordinary food inspection.

“Oil,” Savra said.

Lale checked the pan before adding it. “Not yet.”

Savra waited. Authority over heat belonged to the person at the stove, even when another cook had designed the meal.

Issen approached the service table with an empty bowl.

Savra looked at the audit tablet. “Finished?”

“All tables severed. Three injuries retained. No missing records. Four refusals before service, nine cups broken after eating, one accidental break that closed its local table.”

“No hidden transfers?”

“None found. That is not the same as none possible.”

“Good.”

Certainty had once served empire better than safety.

Savra filled Issen’s bowl from the lentil pot. Her own portion waited in a blue dish at the end of the table. Dima had marked it unbound in charcoal despite everyone knowing. Repetition made practice visible.

When service slowed, Savra sat at the market’s long common board. Teren occupied one end beside route workers. Lale sat with Eren Ford bakers. Mava argued about key rotation with two refusing provinces. Dima lowered herself beside Savra, keeping the adapted knife on the table rather than hiding her hand.

Nobody shared a magical seat.

Savra tasted alone. The first bite brought smoked onion, lentil, and a late edge of fenugreek from the Kelar pan. Her stomach tightened with a hunger wave that belonged to the injury she carried, not to any stranger presently connected. She set the spoon down until the contraction passed.

Dima continued eating. She did not ask whether Savra needed the burden shared. Teren did not watch from across the table. The people nearest made space without turning attention into ceremony.

Savra lifted the spoon again.

A child from the salt uplands approached with a plate containing only flatbread. “My council did not bring a table,” he said. “Can I take onions?”

“If you want them.” Savra held out the serving spoon, then stopped. “Do you want me to serve or do you?”

He took the spoon and chose three pieces. Nobody checked a hearth mark. His council’s refusal had not excluded him from the unbound food.

At the other end of the board, a Tal Jes veteran set down a whole salt cup kept from the afternoon table. She had stayed through its full pain share. For the common course the cup carried no function, so she used it to hold plum stones. A tool of severance did not need to become a sacred badge.

Issen closed their audit tablet before eating. No signature certified the board. Ordinary food law still applied: clean water, sound grain, declared allergens, fair measure. Hearth authority did not.

As dusk came, cooks relit small festival lamps from their own hearths. One hundred fires rose around Kelar Market, some blue at the base from mineral salt, some orange from fig wood, some pale over charcoal. Salt lines did not connect them. No central cauldron waited. The First Hearth remained locked in Namarra under four rotating keys and could authorize nothing by itself.

Children carried unbound flatbread between tables. Auditors closed their tablets. People who had refused every bound dish ate from the common course beside people who had kept their cups whole.

Issen stood to see the market from end to end. A hundred separate flames illuminated different bowls, different hands, and every seat that had existed written where witnesses could find it. Darkness remained between the fires. No cook rushed to erase it.

Together they gave enough light for the meal.
